===== FILE: docs/notes/dataops/data-management-at-scale.md =====
# Data Management at Scale — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Data Management at Scale
- Title: Data Management at Scale: Best Practices for Enterprise Architecture (1st Edition)
- Author(s): Piethein Strengholt
- Branch: dataops
- RepoScope profile: dataops
- Primary source file:data-management-at-scale-best-practices-for-enterprise-architecture.pdf
- Note status: partially-ready-for-YAML

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Distributed domain-driven data architecture and Scaled Data Architecture principles.
  - Data Product encapsulation: data code, metadata, access control, and binding data contracts.
  - Enterprise metadata management: technical, operational, and business metadata architecture.
  - Master Data Management (MDM) integration within distributed, domain-owned environments.
  - Federated computational data governance, policy-as-code enforcement, and automated self-serve platform engineering.
- Out of scope:
  - Proprietary vendor database engine setup or low-level SQL optimization tuning.
  - Individual programming language syntax tutorials (e.g., Python vs. Scala dataframe syntax).
  - Physical network infrastructure routing protocols or hardware cable installation.

## 3. Source spirit card
- Source title: Data Management at Scale: Best Practices for Enterprise Architecture
- Core architectural posture: Decentralize data management by distributing ownership to domain teams as Data Products, unified through federated computational governance and self-serve platforms.
- Primary operational invariant: Domain data assets must be published as version-controlled Data Products governed by explicit Data Contracts, metadata declarations, and automated quality SLAs.
- Default abstraction layer: Domain-oriented Data Products equipped with standardized metadata, explicit schema contracts, bounded context APIs, and self-serve infrastructure.
- System boundary definition: Distributed enterprise-wide, covering operational source domain outputs, data product integration boundaries, enterprise metadata catalogs, and governing platform APIs.
- Target failure model: The monolithic "data lake swamp" trap where centralized teams become organizational bottlenecks, data quality lacks domain ownership, and schema changes break downstream consumers without warning.
- Primary trade-off accepted: Accepts increased platform complexity and federated governance coordination in exchange for domain autonomy, enterprise scalability, and agility.
- Key repository evidence: Presence of data product manifests (data-product.yml), explicit schema contract definitions (OpenAPI, Avro, Protobuf, dbt schema YAML), policy-as-code rules, and metadata tags.
- Anti-pattern rejected: Monolithic central data lakes without domain ownership, uncontracted raw database table dumps, centralized data engineering bottleneck teams, and undocumented secret/PII sharing.
- Success criterion: An enterprise ecosystem of autonomous domain data products publishing self-describing, contracted data assets registered in an automated central metadata catalog.

## 4. Source-backed structure
- Theme 1: Shift from Centralized Monoliths to Distributed Scaled Architecture
  - Source: Data Management at Scale
  - Structural locator: Chapter 1, Architecture & Principles / Chapter 2, Domain-Driven Design
  - Spirit locator: Moving from monolithic centralized EDWs and data lakes to domain-driven distributed architectures eliminates central bottlenecks and enforces domain-level data accountability.
- Theme 2: Data Product Specification and Binding Data Contracts
  - Source: Data Management at Scale
  - Structural locator: Chapter 3, Data Products / Chapter 4, Data Mesh & Distribution
  - Spirit locator: Packaging data logic, schema specifications, operational metadata, and access controls into versioned Data Products governed by contracts ensures consumer trust and interoperability.
- Theme 3: Enterprise Metadata Management and Data Cataloging
  - Source: Data Management at Scale
  - Structural locator: Chapter 5, Metadata Management / Chapter 6, Master Data Management
  - Spirit locator: Automating the harvesting of technical, operational, and business metadata provides enterprise-wide discoverability and end-to-end lineage without requiring centralized physical storage.
- Theme 4: Federated Computational Data Governance and Policy-as-Code
  - Source: Data Management at Scale
  - Structural locator: Chapter 7, Data Governance / Chapter 8, Security & Privacy
  - Spirit locator: Embedding policy-as-code security, access controls, and compliance checks directly into self-serve CI/CD pipelines guarantees continuous enterprise governance.
- Theme 5: Self-Serve Platform Infrastructure and Provisioning
  - Source: Data Management at Scale
  - Structural locator: Chapter 9, Platform Architecture & Provisioning
  - Spirit locator: Providing standardized self-serve platform infrastructure as code (IaC) empowers domain teams to deploy and manage data products independently and safely.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. What architectural posture does this source assume?
   - The source assumes a decentralized, domain-driven posture where individual business domains own and operate their data products, connected through a federated computational governance framework.
2. What is the fundamental invariant the system must preserve?
   - The fundamental invariant is that every published domain data asset must be encapsulated as a Data Product with a binding Data Contract specifying its schema, SLA, classification, and access policies.
3. What operational trade-offs does the source explicitly accept?
   - The source accepts higher initial investment in self-serve platform tooling and federated governance standards to eliminate central engineering bottlenecks and enable long-term enterprise scalability.
4. How does the source define failure or risk?
   - The source defines failure as central data teams becoming overwhelmed bottlenecks, uncontracted data lake dumps producing "data swamps", silent schema breaking changes, and unmonitored PII exposure.
5. What repository-level artifacts demonstrate adherence to this spirit?
   - Adherence is demonstrated by repository manifests such as data-product.yml, contract definitions (contract.yaml, avro/proto schemas), security classification tags, and automated quality test suites.
6. What anti-patterns indicate a violation of the source's spirit?
   - Anti-patterns include dumping unformatted raw operational tables into a shared lake folder without metadata, modifying production schemas without versioned contracts, and granting unrestricted global read access to sensitive data.

## 6. Key concepts
- Concept 1: Scaled Data Architecture & Data Mesh
  - Definition: An architectural paradigm that decentralizes data ownership to domain teams, treating data as a product supported by self-serve platform infrastructure and federated governance.
  - Why it matters: Prevents organizational bottlenecks and scales enterprise data management alongside business growth.
  - Related concepts: Domain-Driven Design, Data Product, Self-Serve Platform.
  - Citation: Chapter 1, Architecture & Principles — Principles of scaled data architecture.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 2: Data Product
  - Definition: An autonomous, self-describing node in a data network that encapsulates data logic, underlying storage, metadata, code, and interfaces under explicit domain ownership.
  - Why it matters: Shifts data management from passive historical artifact collection to active product delivery with guaranteed quality SLAs.
  - Related concepts: Data Contract, Bounded Context, Output Port.
  - Citation: Chapter 3, Data Products — Defining data product boundaries and components.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 3: Data Contract
  - Definition: A formal, versioned agreement between a data product producer and consumers specifying schema, semantics, refresh SLAs, quality guarantees, and usage policies.
  - Why it matters: Eliminates silent breaking changes and establishes clear operational expectations between upstream systems and downstream consumers.
  - Related concepts: Schema Evolution, Breaking Change, SLA.
  - Citation: Chapter 4, Data Mesh & Distribution — Data contract specifications.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 4: Enterprise Metadata (Technical, Operational, Business)
  - Definition: Standardized information describing data assets, encompassing technical structure (schemas), operational behavior (lineage, freshness, job status), and business context (definitions, ownership).
  - Why it matters: Enables automated data discovery, lineage tracking, and governance without requiring centralized physical data storage.
  - Related concepts: Data Catalog, Data Lineage, Master Data Management.
  - Citation: Chapter 5, Metadata Management — The three pillars of enterprise metadata.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 5: Federated Computational Governance
  - Definition: A governance model where central guidelines and compliance policies are defined collaboratively but executed automatically via code and infrastructure automation.
  - Why it matters: Ensures continuous security, privacy, and regulatory compliance across decentralized teams without manual gatekeeping delays.
  - Related concepts: Policy-as-Code, Security Classification, Automated Compliance.
  - Citation: Chapter 7, Data Governance — Federated computational governance model.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 6: Master Data Management (MDM) in Domain Architectures
  - Definition: The discipline of maintaining a single, authoritative reference definition for core enterprise entities (e.g., Customer, Product) across distributed domains.
  - Why it matters: Ensures semantic consistency and interoperability when combining data products from distinct domain contexts.
  - Related concepts: Conformed Entities, Reference Data, Golden Record.
  - Citation: Chapter 6, Master Data Management — Integrating MDM into domain-driven architectures.
  - Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
- Pattern 1: Data Product Repository & Contract Pattern
  - Problem solved: Lack of standardized structure and explicit contracts in domain data repositories leading to fragile, undocumented pipelines.
  - Recommended approach: Structure domain data repositories with a root data-product.yml descriptor, a contract/ directory containing versioned schema files, and automated contract test suites.
  - Repository-level manifestation: Presence of data-product.yml, contracts/schema.yaml (or .proto/.avsc), and automated schema validation tests in CI/CD.
  - Limitations: Requires domain software engineers to learn data contract formatting and maintain manifest files.
  - Source citation: Chapter 3, Data Products — Data product repository organization.
- Pattern 2: Policy-as-Code Access & Classification Pattern
  - Problem solved: Manual, slow security access request approvals and unmonitored exposure of PII/sensitive fields in enterprise data assets.
  - Recommended approach: Declare data classification levels (e.g., public, internal, confidential, PII) directly in model YAML files and enforce access policies via automated infrastructure scripts.
  - Repository-level manifestation: Column-level or model-level metadata tags specifying classification: pii or sensitivity: confidential in YAML configs.
  - Limitations: Requires central governance teams to maintain standard classification taxonomies and policy enforcement engines.
  - Source citation: Chapter 8, Security & Privacy — Policy-as-code and data privacy controls.
- Pattern 3: Automated Technical Metadata Registration Pattern
  - Problem solved: Outdated, manually maintained data catalogs that fail to reflect the actual state of production databases and pipelines.
  - Recommended approach: Configure CI/CD deployment pipelines to automatically push compiled schemas, lineage graphs, and model documentation to the enterprise metadata catalog on every release.
  - Repository-level manifestation: Pipeline steps (e.g., in GitHub Actions or CI scripts) executing catalog registration commands (e.g., dbt docs generate && push_metadata).
  - Limitations: Requires active integration endpoints with an enterprise catalog tool (e.g., OpenMetadata, DataHub, Collibra).
  - Source citation: Chapter 5, Metadata Management — Automated metadata harvesting.

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
1. Uncontracted Data Product Releases
   - Why it happens: Domain teams deploy schema modifications or new data models directly to production storage without updating or publishing a data contract.
   - Early warning signs: Downstream analytics pipelines or consumer applications fail unexpectedly with schema parsing or missing column errors.
   - Repository evidence: Data model code repositories lacking data-product.yml or contract definitions under version control.
   - Severity tendency: critical
2. Missing Sensitivity / PII Data Classification
   - Why it happens: Developers add new operational fields containing personal data (e.g., email, SSN) without tagging them for security masking or encryption.
   - Early warning signs: Compliance audit failures, unauthorized user access to sensitive fields, and regulatory privacy violations.
   - Repository evidence: Models containing fields named email, phone, or ssn without explicit sensitivity or classification metadata tags.
   - Severity tendency: critical
3. Centralized Team Bottlenecks ("Fake Decentralization")
   - Why it happens: Organizations claim to adopt domain architecture but still require a central data engineering team to approve and write all transformation pipelines.
   - Early warning signs: Long backlog queues for simple data request changes, domain team frustration, and slow time-to-market.
   - Repository evidence: All data pipeline repositories owned and maintained exclusively by a single central engineering team rather than domain CODEOWNERS.
   - Severity tendency: warning
4. Unregistered Metadata and Silent Lineage Gaps
   - Why it happens: Data transformation jobs execute in isolation without registering pipeline execution status or lineage graphs with the enterprise catalog.
   - Early warning signs: Inability to perform impact analysis when source schemas change, and invisible data flow dependencies across domains.
   - Repository evidence: Deployment pipelines lacking automated catalog integration tasks or metadata publishing execution steps.
   - Severity tendency: warning

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated)
- Absence of `data-product.yml` or `data-product.yaml` manifest in a domain data repository root.
- Absence of a version-controlled contract directory (`contracts/` or `schemas/`) specifying published output schemas.
- Detection of common sensitive field name patterns (e.g., `email`, `ssn`, `phone_number`) lacking explicit `classification` or `sensitivity` metadata tags in YAML models.
- Absence of automated contract validation or test scripts in the CI pipeline configuration (`.github/workflows/`).

### Needs-review candidates
- Verification that schema evolution changes in data contracts follow semantic versioning rules (e.g., major version increments for column deletions).
- Verification that domain ownership in `CODEOWNERS` maps directly to business domain teams rather than a central monolith team.
- Inspection of self-serve IaC scripts (`terraform/`, `pulumi/`) to ensure environment isolation between domain products.

### Human review (contextual & organizational)
- Evaluating whether data product boundaries align cleanly with domain Bounded Contexts.
- Assessing whether cross-domain data access requests adhere to enterprise regulatory and privacy policies.
- Reviewing whether domain teams possess adequate engineering capability to maintain their published data products.

### Organizational artifacts check
- Root `data-product.yml` manifest declaring domain ownership, SLA, contact channels, and published output ports.
- Enterprise data catalog registration workflows defined in repository CI/CD pipeline scripts.

## 10. Candidate rules

- Rule ID: dataops-scale-001
- Title: Missing Data Product Manifest File
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: A domain data repository without a data-product.yml manifest cannot be indexed by self-serve platforms or governed by federated policies.
- Source-backed basis: Data products must be self-describing nodes encapsulating metadata, owner specifications, and operational boundaries.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Presence of data-product.yml or data-product.yaml at the repository root.
- Pass condition: data-product.yml or data-product.yaml exists at the root of the domain data repository.
- Fail condition: No data product manifest file is present in the repository root.
- Exceptions or limitations: Utility or helper libraries not serving as domain data products.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Chapter 3, Data Products — Data product specifications and manifests.
- Source: Data Management at Scale
- Structural locator: Chapter 3, Data Products
- Spirit locator: Encapsulating domain data into self-describing data products requires a standardized manifest file at the repository root.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-scale-002
- Title: Missing Sensitive Field Classification Tag
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Deploying data assets containing sensitive field names (e.g., email, ssn) without classification tags prevents automated policy-as-code masking and risks privacy violations.
- Source-backed basis: Federated computational governance requires security classifications declared directly in model metadata.
- Repository evidence to inspect: YAML schema or model definition files containing sensitive field names (e.g., email, phone, ssn, address).
- Pass condition: Sensitive fields in schema YAML files include classification or sensitivity metadata tags.
- Fail condition: Sensitive field names exist in schema definitions without corresponding classification metadata tags.
- Exceptions or limitations: Fully anonymized test datasets.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Requires structural YAML-aware parser to evaluate column-level metadata tag properties.
- Source citation: Chapter 8, Security & Privacy — Policy-as-code and data classification.
- Source: Data Management at Scale
- Structural locator: Chapter 8, Security & Privacy
- Spirit locator: Policy-as-code security enforcement relies on explicit column-level metadata classification tags in model definitions.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-scale-003
- Title: Missing Version-Controlled Data Contract Directory
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Publishing domain data assets without a version-controlled contract directory leads to unannounced breaking changes for downstream consumers.
- Source-backed basis: Data products must expose explicit, versioned data contracts defining output schemas and semantic rules.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Presence of a contracts/ or schemas/ directory containing contract definition files (.yaml, .avsc, .proto).
- Pass condition: A contracts/ or schemas/ directory containing formal contract specifications exists in the repository.
- Fail condition: A domain data product repository lacks a formal contract or schema definition directory.
- Exceptions or limitations: Internal private scratch models not published to external consumers.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Chapter 4, Data Mesh & Distribution — Data contract architecture.
- Source: Data Management at Scale
- Structural locator: Chapter 4, Data Mesh & Distribution
- Spirit locator: Explicit versioned contract directories guarantee schema stability and trust between data producers and consumers.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment
```yaml
rules:
  - id: "dataops-scale-001"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "critical"
    description: "Ensures data-product.yml manifest file exists at the root of domain data repositories."
    check:
      type: "file_exists"
      file_glob: "data-product.y*ml"
      error_when: "file_absent"
      message: "data-product.yml manifest missing. Domain data assets must be encapsulated as self-describing Data Products."
    references:
      - book: "Data Management at Scale"
        concept: "data-product-manifest"

  - id: "dataops-scale-002"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "critical"
    description: "Ensures domain data repositories maintain a version-controlled contracts/ or schemas/ directory."
    check:
      type: "file_exists"
      file_glob: "{contracts,schemas}/"
      error_when: "file_absent"
      message: "Missing contracts/ or schemas/ directory. Data products must declare explicit output data contracts."
    references:
      - book: "Data Management at Scale"
        concept: "data-contracts"

  - id: "dataops-scale-003"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "error"
    description: "Detects unclassified sensitive PII fields in model YAML schema configurations."
    check:
      type: "pattern_match"
      file_glob: "models/**/*.yml"
      pattern: "(?i)(email|ssn|phone|passport|credit_card)"
      error_when: "pattern_present"
      message: "Potential sensitive/PII field detected in model schema. Verify classification or sensitivity tags are configured."
    references:
      - book: "Data Management at Scale"
        concept: "policy-as-code"
```

# Fundamentals of Data Engineering — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source Identity
* **Title:** Fundamentals of Data Engineering: Plan and Build Robust Data Systems
* **Author:** Joe Reis & Matt Housley
* **Branch:** DataOps
* **Profile:** `dataops`
* **Primary File:** `Fundamentals of Data Engineering (Reis, JoeHousley, Matt).pdf`
* **Status:** Verified & Fully Grounded

---

## 2. Scope

### Covered in Primary Source
* The holistic **Data Engineering Lifecycle** (Generation, Storage, Ingestion, Transformation, Serving).
* The 6 foundational **Undercurrents** (Security, Data Management, DataOps, Data Architecture, Orchestration, Software Engineering).
* Evaluation of source systems, storage abstractions (Data Warehouses, Data Lakes, Data Lakehouses), ingestion paradigms (Batch, Streaming, Change Data Capture), query engines, and serving layers (Analytics, ML, Reverse ETL).
* Core principles of cloud-native data architecture, technology selection trade-offs (Build vs. Buy, Monolith vs. Modular, Serverless vs. Servers), FinOps, and data security/privacy (GDPR, CCPA, zero-trust).

### Not Covered in Primary Source
* Vendor-specific code syntax or API specifications (e.g., specific PySpark functions, Snowflake SQL syntax details, or Terraform HCL definitions).
* Low-level database internals like B-Tree page layouts or hardware circuit design.
* Specific machine learning model training algorithms or hyperparameter optimization techniques.

---

## 3. Book Spirit Card

| Field | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Title** | *Fundamentals of Data Engineering* |
| **Author** | Joe Reis & Matt Housley |
| **Core Thesis** | Data engineering must evolve beyond tool-chasing and tech myopia to focus on the end-to-end **Data Engineering Lifecycle**, building resilient, scalable, and secure data foundations through timeless engineering undercurrents. |
| **Operating Model** | Lifecycle-centric architecture where data flows continuously from source generation through storage, ingestion, and transformation to downstream serving, guided by continuous security, DataOps, and governance. |
| **Primary Engineering Object** | End-to-end data pipeline and underlying data storage abstractions. |
| **Architectural Posture** | Cloud-native, loosely coupled, modular, and reversible architectural design prioritizing simplicity, security, and FinOps over complexity. |
| **Preferred Stack Style** | Decoupled compute and storage, managed open-source / SaaS ingestion, pipelines-as-code orchestration (e.g., Airflow, Dagster), and SQL/DataFrame transformations. |
| **Key Invariants** | Data must serve an intentional downstream business purpose; least-privilege security and encryption must be enforced across all lifecycle stages; compute and storage must remain decoupled. |
| **Non-Negotiable Antipatterns** | Technology myopia (choosing tools before understanding requirements); data vanity projects (storing data lakes without serving value); granting admin/root IAM access to all pipeline operators; brittle time-based cron scheduling without dependency DAGs. |
| **Primary Risk If Ignored** | High data downtime, astronomical unmonitored cloud costs, unmanageable data swamps, severe security/privacy breaches, and failed downstream analytics/ML projects. |

---

## 4. Source-Backed Structure

1. **Part I: Foundation and Building Blocks**
   * *Chapter 1: Data Engineering Described* — Defining the data engineer, the evolution from Big Data to Lifecycle Engineering, and interactions with upstream/downstream stakeholders.
   * *Chapter 2: The Data Engineering Lifecycle* — Introducing the 5 lifecycle stages (Generation, Storage, Ingestion, Transformation, Serving) and the 6 Undercurrents.
   * *Chapter 3: Designing Good Data Architecture* — Defining operational vs. technical architecture and establishing 9 core architectural principles (Scalability, Loose Coupling, Reversibility, Security, FinOps).
   * *Chapter 4: Choosing Technologies Across the Data Engineering Lifecycle* — Framework for technology selection: Team size, speed to market, interoperability, build vs. buy, serverless vs. servers, and modular vs. monolith.
2. **Part II: The Data Engineering Lifecycle in Depth**
   * *Chapter 5: Data Generation in Source Systems* — Operational databases, logs, CRUD patterns, CDC, IoT streams, and upstream stakeholder collaboration.
   * *Chapter 6: Storage* — Raw storage ingredients (RAM, SSD, HDD, CPU, networking), storage abstractions (Data Warehouse, Data Lake, Data Lakehouse), storage lifecycles, and retention policies.
   * *Chapter 7: Ingestion* — Engineering considerations for bounded vs. unbounded data, push vs. pull vs. poll, batch vs. streaming ingestion, schema drift, and data connectors.
   * *Chapter 8: Queries, Modeling, and Transformation* — Query engines, batch vs. streaming transformations, data modeling patterns (Normalization, Dimensional Star/Snowflake, Data Vault), and data wrangling.
   * *Chapter 9: Serving Data for Analytics, Machine Learning, and Reverse ETL* — Serving analytics (BI, operational, embedded), machine learning feature stores, reverse ETL feedback loops, and semantic/metrics layers.
3. **Part III: Security, Privacy, and the Future of Data Engineering**
   * *Chapter 10: Security and Privacy* — People, process, and technology aspects of security, encryption at rest/in transit, zero-trust IAM, and compliance with GDPR/CCPA.
   * *Chapter 11: The Future of Data Engineering* — The live data stack, rise of enterprisey data management, decline of complexity, and the enduring nature of the lifecycle.

---

## 5. Source-Backed Spirit

### Q1: What differentiates the "Data Lifecycle" from the "Data Engineering Lifecycle"?
* **Structural Locator:** Part I, Chapter 2 ("The Data Lifecycle Versus the Data Engineering Lifecycle")
* **Spirit Locator:** The general Data Lifecycle describes the high-level life of data within an organization from initial acquisition to eventual deletion or archival. In contrast, the Data Engineering Lifecycle refers specifically to the technical stages and workflows—Generation, Storage, Ingestion, Transformation, and Serving—and their supporting technical undercurrents that data engineers design and operate to turn raw data into consumable assets.

### Q2: Why is Storage depicted as an underlying foundation across all lifecycle stages?
* **Structural Locator:** Part I, Chapter 2 ("Storage") & Part II, Chapter 6 ("Storage")
* **Spirit Locator:** Storage is not merely a single isolated stage; it is the physical and logical bedrock that underpins ingestion, transformation, and serving. Data persists in storage media at rest and during transit between processing steps. Whether using object stores, cloud data warehouses, streaming buffers, or memory caches, storage crosses over every operational boundary of the pipeline.

### Q3: What are the trade-offs between Monolithic and Modular Data Architectures?
* **Structural Locator:** Part I, Chapter 4 ("Monolith Versus Modular")
* **Spirit Locator:** Monolithic data platforms offer all-in-one simplicity and unified governance out of the box but create tight vendor lock-in, rigid scaling limits, and high migration friction. Modular architectures allow teams to assemble best-of-breed components (e.g., decoupled ingestion, storage, query engine, and orchestrator) connected via open standards, maximizing flexibility and reversibility at the cost of higher integration and orchestration complexity.

### Q4: How do the "Undercurrents" support the core lifecycle stages?
* **Structural Locator:** Part I, Chapter 2 ("Major Undercurrents Across the Data Engineering Lifecycle")
* **Spirit Locator:** The six undercurrents—Security, Data Management, DataOps, Data Architecture, Orchestration, and Software Engineering—cut horizontally across all five stages of the lifecycle. No stage can operate in isolation without secure access control, metadata management, automated monitoring/CI-CD, architectural planning, workflow scheduling, and maintainable software code.

### Q5: Why is "Reverse ETL" considered an essential component of the Serving stage?
* **Structural Locator:** Part I, Chapter 2 ("Serving Data") & Part II, Chapter 9 ("Reverse ETL")
* **Spirit Locator:** Serving data is not limited to passive BI reporting. Reverse ETL takes processed, enriched, or scored data from analytics storage and feeds it back into operational systems (CRMs, ad tech platforms, ERPs). This creates an active feedback loop, empowering business tools with intelligence derived downstream.

### Q6: How does the principle of "Reversible Decisions" apply to data system design?
* **Structural Locator:** Part I, Chapter 3 ("Principle 7: Make Reversible Decisions")
* **Spirit Locator:** Data architectures must avoid "one-way door" decisions that lock an organization into proprietary formats, rigid schemas, or monolithic platforms. By utilizing open table formats, standardized APIs, and modular pipelines-as-code, architects preserve the ability to swap individual technologies as requirements or vendor pricing evolve.

---

## 6. Key Concepts

1. **Data Engineering Lifecycle:** The 5-stage conceptual framework (Generation, Storage, Ingestion, Transformation, Serving) describing end-to-end data flow.
2. **Undercurrents:** The 6 fundamental practices (Security, Data Management, DataOps, Data Architecture, Orchestration, Software Engineering) supporting all lifecycle stages.
3. **Data Lifecycle Engineer:** An engineer who operates at a high level of technical abstraction, focusing on end-to-end value, governance, and architecture rather than managing low-level big data cluster infrastructure.
4. **Decoupled Compute and Storage:** An architectural pattern separating storage tiers (e.g., S3/GCS) from compute processing engines (e.g., Snowflake, Spark, BigQuery), enabling independent scaling and FinOps optimization.
5. **Bounded vs. Unbounded Data:** Bounded data represents finite, complete batch datasets; unbounded data represents continuous, infinitely generating event streams requiring windowing and real-time ingestion.
6. **Data Observability Driven Development (DODD):** A DataOps methodology that embeds monitoring and assertion checks directly into pipeline development, observing data health continuously across its lineage.
7. **Principle of Least Privilege:** Security policy restricting user and system access strictly to the resources and data necessary for their specific role, avoiding admin access antipatterns.
8. **Pipelines as Code:** Declaring pipeline DAGs, dependencies, and execution logic using version-controlled software code (e.g., Python) rather than manual GUI configurations.
9. **FinOps in Data Architecture:** Integrating cloud cost visibility and management directly into architectural choices, monitoring resource usage to avoid runaway query/storage expenses.
10. **Reverse ETL:** Pushing enriched analytical results, model scores, or aggregated metrics back into operational source systems (CRMs, marketing automation, SaaS applications).

---

## 7. Practical Patterns and Examples

### Pattern 1: Stream-to-Batch Storage Pattern
* **Context:** Ingesting high-velocity unbounded event data (e.g., clickstream or IoT logs) for downstream analytical querying.
* **Implementation:** High-throughput streaming buffers (e.g., Apache Kafka or AWS Kinesis) receive live events, pass them through a micro-batch streaming engine, and flush compressed columnar files (e.g., Parquet) to cloud object storage. Analytical query engines (e.g., Snowflake or BigQuery) then load or query the object storage in structured batch intervals.
* **Benefit:** Bridges real-time low-latency event capture with cost-effective, high-throughput batch analytical querying.

### Pattern 2: Change Data Capture (CDC) for Low-Impact Ingestion
* **Context:** Ingesting transactional database updates into an analytical warehouse without overloading the production database.
* **Implementation:** Instead of executing heavy SQL `SELECT *` table scans against production OLTP databases, a CDC service reads the database binary transaction log (e.g., Postgres WAL or MySQL binlog). Log events (Inserts, Updates, Deletes) are pushed to a message bus and replayed into the target data warehouse.
* **Benefit:** Eliminates query contention on operational databases while delivering near-real-time synchronization.

### Pattern 3: Pipelines-as-Code Orchestration
* **Context:** Managing complex data transformations across heterogeneous systems with inter-job dependencies.
* **Implementation:** Workflows are defined as Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) in version-controlled Python files using frameworks like Apache Airflow or Dagster. Tasks instantiate containerized tasks or execute SQL commands via APIs, enforcing strict task sequencing, automated retries, and alert notifications.
* **Benefit:** Ensures reproducibility, parameterization, version control, and testability across multi-step data pipelines.

### Pattern 4: Decoupled Staging-to-Mart Transformation Layer
* **Context:** Transforming raw ingested data into clean analytical models without mutating source data.
* **Implementation:** Raw payloads land immutably in a `staging` layer in object storage or data warehouse tables. Transformations read from `staging`, apply cleaning and schema casting in an `intermediate` layer, and materialize business models into dimensional `marts` (Star Schema) via declarative SQL toolsets.
* **Benefit:** Preserves auditability of raw inputs while ensuring clean, modular business logic.

### Pattern 5: Metric / Semantic Layer Serving
* **Context:** Preventing inconsistent metric calculations (e.g., "Active Users" or "MRR") across disparate BI dashboards and ML pipelines.
* **Implementation:** Metric definitions and aggregation formulas are defined once in a centralized metrics repository/YAML layer. Downstream BI tools, notebooks, and reverse ETL applications query the semantic layer API rather than writing raw SQL against warehouse tables.
* **Benefit:** Guarantees a single source of truth across all consumer applications and organizational units.

---

## 8. Common Failure Modes and Risks

| Risk / Failure Mode | Root Cause | Impact | Mitigation Strategy |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Technology Myopia** | Choosing cutting-edge tools based on marketing hype rather than actual business requirements. | Excessive infrastructure complexity, high maintenance overhead, and failed deployments. | Evaluate problems using the Data Engineering Lifecycle framework; prioritize simplicity and business value over tool trends. |
| **Data Vanity Projects (Data Swamps)** | Ingesting massive raw datasets into data lakes without intentional downstream use cases. | Astronomical cloud storage bills, unorganized data swamps, and zero business ROI. | Define explicit downstream analytics/ML requirements before building ingestion pipelines. |
| **Over-Privileged IAM Admin Access** | Granting wildcard (`*`) or admin permissions to pipeline operators and user accounts to bypass IAM configuration complexity. | Catastrophic security vulnerabilities, unauthorized data leaks, and compliance violations. | Enforce zero-trust IAM policies and the Principle of Least Privilege across all storage and compute resources. |
| **Brittle Time-Based Cron Ingestion** | Triggering dependent downstream pipelines on rigid time clocks without checking task status. | Downstream reports generated from stale, incomplete, or corrupted data due to silent upstream delays. | Replace time-based cron jobs with dependency-aware, event-driven pipelines-as-code orchestration. |
| **Unmonitored Schema Drift** | Upstream application developers altering source database schemas without notifying data teams. | Pipeline execution crashes, broken downstream dashboards, and corrupted data models. | Implement automated schema validation, contract checks, and alert notifications at pipeline ingestion entrypoints. |

---

## 9. Repository-Audit Translation

### Static Check Candidates (Automated Repo Verification)
* **Orchestration Verification:** Confirm that workflows are defined as version-controlled code files (`dags/*.py` or `dagster_*.py`) rather than raw shell scripts.
* **SQL Transformation Discipline:** Check that SQL transformation queries do not hardcode production database/schema names and use parameterized refs or variables.
* **Infrastructure as Code (IaC):** Verify that cloud storage buckets, IAM roles, and query warehouses are provisioned via IaC configurations (`*.tf` or `serverless.yml`).
* **CI/CD Integration:** Ensure existence of workflow pipelines (`.github/workflows/*.yml` or `.gitlab-ci.yml`) enforcing automated syntax checking and unit tests.
* **Environment Configuration Separation:** Audit repositories to ensure zero plaintext API keys or database passwords in code files, verifying usage of secret managers or environment variables.

### Human Review Targets (Architectural & Organizational Review)
* **Build vs. Buy Evaluation:** Assessing whether custom-built ingestion scripts should be replaced by managed SaaS connectors.
* **Stakeholder Alignment & Data Contracts:** Reviewing communication channels between upstream application developers and downstream analytics teams regarding schema changes.
* **FinOps Governance:** Reviewing warehouse auto-suspend settings and query performance profiles to optimize monthly cloud bills.
* **Business Metric Consistency:** Evaluating whether business metric calculations in BI tools match definitions in the data dictionary or semantic layer.

---

## 10. Candidate RepoScope Rules

### Rule 1: `dataops-fde-001` — Version-Controlled Orchestration DAGs
* **Title:** Ensure Pipeline Orchestration is Defined as Code
* **Domain:** `dataops`
* **Readiness:** `ready`
* **Severity:** `error`
* **Target Files:** `dags/**/*.py`, `orchestration/**/*.py`, `Dagfile`
* **Rationale:** Time-based cron scripts or manual web-UI pipeline execution violate the fundamental DataOps undercurrent. Orchestration must be declared as version-controlled pipelines-as-code.

### Rule 2: `dataops-fde-002` — Parameterized Environment Configurations
* **Title:** Prohibit Hardcoded Database and Schema Identifiers in SQL
* **Domain:** `dataops`
* **Readiness:** `ready`
* **Severity:** `warning`
* **Target Files:** `models/**/*.sql`, `transformations/**/*.sql`
* **Rationale:** Hardcoding environment-specific database paths prevents proper dev/test/prod environment isolation and breaks pipeline portability across cloud environments.

### Rule 3: `dataops-fde-003` — Infrastructure as Code Declaration
* **Title:** Enforce IaC Declarations for Cloud Storage and Compute
* **Domain:** `dataops`
* **Readiness:** `ready`
* **Severity:** `warning`
* **Target Files:** `terraform/**/*.tf`, `cloudformation/*.yml`, `serverless.yml`
* **Rationale:** Cloud storage buckets, IAM roles, and warehouse compute tiers must be provisioned via version-controlled IaC to guarantee environment reproducibility and security auditing.

### Rule 4: `dataops-fde-004` — Secrets Security Check
* **Title:** Prevent Plaintext Hardcoded Credentials in Data Repositories
* **Domain:** `dataops`
* **Readiness:** `ready`
* **Severity:** `error`
* **Target Files:** `**/*.py`, `**/*.sql`, `**/*.yml`, `**/*.json`
* **Rationale:** Storing plaintext passwords, database URIs with passwords, or cloud provider API keys in code repository files breaches the Security undercurrent and zero-trust guidelines.

---

## 11. Suggested YAML Fragment

```yaml
rules:
  - id: dataops-fde-001
    title: Ensure Pipeline Orchestration is Defined as Code
    profile: dataops
    readiness: ready
    severity: error
    description: >
      Verifies that workflow orchestration pipelines are declared as version-controlled code
      files (e.g., Airflow DAGs or Dagster jobs) rather than unmanaged cron jobs or shell scripts.
    target_files:
      - "dags/**/*.py"
      - "orchestration/**/*.py"
      - "jobs/**/*.py"
    check:
      type: file_exists
      min_count: 1

  - id: dataops-fde-002
    title: Prohibit Hardcoded Production Databases in SQL Transformations
    profile: dataops
    readiness: ready
    severity: warning
    description: >
      Detects hardcoded production database or schema paths in SQL transformation files.
      SQL queries should utilize environment variables or parameterized references.
    target_files:
      - "models/**/*.sql"
      - "transformations/**/*.sql"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      regex: "(?i)FROM\s+[a-zA-Z0-9_]+\.(prod|production|raw_prod)\.[a-zA-Z0-9_]+"
      should_match: false

  - id: dataops-fde-003
    title: Enforce Infrastructure as Code Declarations
    profile: dataops
    readiness: ready
    severity: warning
    description: >
      Ensures the repository contains Infrastructure as Code (IaC) definitions (Terraform or CloudFormation)
      for reproducible storage and compute deployment.
    target_files:
      - "terraform/**/*.tf"
      - "infra/*.yml"
      - "cloudformation/*.yaml"
    check:
      type: file_exists
      min_count: 1

  - id: dataops-fde-004
    title: Prevent Plaintext Secrets and Credentials in Pipeline Code
    profile: dataops
    readiness: ready
    severity: error
    description: >
      Scans repository files for hardcoded database passwords, private keys, or API tokens.
    target_files:
      - "dags/**/*.py"
      - "config/**/*.yml"
      - "scripts/**/*.sh"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      regex: "(?i)(password|secret|api_key|access_key)\s*[:=]\s*['"][A-Za-z0-9_\-]{8,}['"]"
      should_match: false
```

===== FILE: docs/notes/dataops/the-data-warehouse-toolkit.md =====
# The Data Warehouse Toolkit — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** The Data Warehouse Toolkit
- Title: The Data Warehouse Toolkit: The Definitive Guide to Dimensional Modeling (3rd Edition)
- Author(s): Ralph Kimball and Margy Ross
- Branch: dataops
- RepoScope profile: dataops
- Primary source file: Ralph Kimball, Margy Ross - The Data Warehouse Toolkit_ The Definitive Guide to Dimensional Modeling (2013, Wiley.pdf
- Note status: partially-ready-for-YAML

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Fundamental techniques of dimensional modeling: fact tables, dimension tables, and star schemas.
  - Fact table types: transaction, periodic snapshot, and accumulating snapshot fact tables.
  - Advanced dimension modeling: Slowly Changing Dimensions (SCD Types 0-7), conformed dimensions, degenerate dimensions, role-playing dimensions, and junk dimensions.
  - Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Architecture and Bus Matrix design for enterprise integration.
  - Separation of ETL data staging (clean-room extraction/processing) from the user-facing dimensional presentation layer.
- Out of scope:
  - Cloud warehouse pricing and vendor selection (e.g., Snowflake vs. BigQuery billing models).
  - Physical database index creation or storage engine partition tuning for specific proprietary RDBMS engines.
  - Frontend Business Intelligence dashboard color palettes or visual reporting layout styling.

## 3. Source spirit card
- Source title: The Data Warehouse Toolkit: The Definitive Guide to Dimensional Modeling
- Core architectural posture: Structure analytical databases into dimensional star schemas (fact and dimension tables) that mirror business processes and maximize query performance and usability.
- Primary operational invariant: Facts and conformed dimensions must share standardized definitions and surrogate keys across business processes to support enterprise-wide slicing, dicing, and drill-down analysis.
- Default abstraction layer: Star schemas containing atomic fact tables surrounded by denormalized, conformed dimension tables mapped along the Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Matrix.
- System boundary definition: Bounded within the analytical presentation layer and data staging pipeline, downstream of operational OLTP source systems and upstream of BI reporting/OLAP consumption.
- Target failure model: Direct presentation of complex normalized (3NF) schemas to BI tools, isolated non-conformed "data silos" with conflicting attribute definitions, surrogate key allocation collisions, and loss of historical tracking.
- Primary trade-off accepted: Accepts denormalized storage redundancy and controlled ETL transformation complexity in exchange for intuitive BI query models and fast analytical query execution.
- Key repository evidence: Presence of dimensional modeling SQL definitions with clear fact and dimension designations, surrogate key primary keys, conformed dimension references, and SCD Type 2 tracking columns.
- Anti-pattern rejected: Exposing 3NF entity-relationship schemas directly to BI users, snowflake schema over-normalization, mixing grains in fact tables, and using operational natural keys as fact table join keys.
- Success criterion: An integrated dimensional presentation layer supported by a conformed Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Matrix, delivering consistent metrics and high-performance querying across all business domains.

## 4. Source-backed structure
- Theme 1: Fundamental Dimensional Modeling Concepts and Star Schema Architecture
  - Source: The Data Warehouse Toolkit
  - Structural locator: Chapter 1, Essential Concepts
  - Spirit locator: Dividing analytical schemas into fact tables for numeric measurement events and dimension tables for descriptive context optimizes BI query paths and user comprehension.
- Theme 2: Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Architecture and Conformed Dimensions
  - Source: The Data Warehouse Toolkit
  - Structural locator: Chapter 3, Retail Sales / Chapter 4, Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Architecture
  - Spirit locator: Defining conformed dimensions across business process rows in the Enterprise Bus Matrix enables cross-functional data integration without requiring monolithic multi-table joins.
- Theme 3: Fact Table Granularity and Fact Types
  - Source: The Data Warehouse Toolkit
  - Structural locator: Chapter 2, Dimensional Modeling Techniques / Chapter 5, Inventory
  - Spirit locator: Modeling facts at the lowest atomic grain and distinguishing transaction, periodic snapshot, and accumulating snapshot fact tables captures business velocity and process state transitions accurately.
- Theme 4: Dimension Table Techniques and Slowly Changing Dimensions (SCD)
  - Source: The Data Warehouse Toolkit
  - Structural locator: Chapter 5, Inventory / Chapter 6, Procurement
  - Spirit locator: Applying explicit Slowly Changing Dimension techniques (Type 1 overwrite, Type 2 row versioning with effective dates, Type 3 historical columns) maintains point-in-time analytical accuracy.
- Theme 5: Separation of ETL Staging and Dimensional Presentation Layer
  - Source: The Data Warehouse Toolkit
  - Structural locator: Chapter 18, ETL Subsystems & Architecture
  - Spirit locator: Isolating the off-line ETL data staging clean-room from the user-facing dimensional presentation layer prevents unvalidated, dirty, or operational data from polluting analytical models.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. What architectural posture does this source assume?
   - The source assumes a dimensional posture where data is modeled into business process fact tables surrounded by denormalized conformed dimension tables, separated strictly from operational 3NF OLTP databases.
2. What is the fundamental invariant the system must preserve?
   - The fundamental invariant is that all fact tables must be expressed at a single, well-defined atomic grain, utilizing conformed dimensions and surrogate keys to guarantee consistent integration across business processes.
3. What operational trade-offs does the source explicitly accept?
   - The source accepts data storage redundancy and additional ETL processing overhead to generate surrogate keys and denormalized dimensions in order to minimize query complexity and response times for BI tools.
4. How does the source define failure or risk?
   - The source defines failure as exposing normalized operational schemas directly to business analysts, creating isolated data silos with inconsistent metric logic, mixing grains within a fact table, or losing historical fidelity due to unmonitored Type 1 overwrites.
5. What repository-level artifacts demonstrate adherence to this spirit?
   - Adherence is demonstrated by dimensional SQL DDL/DML models clearly named dim_ or fact_, surrogate key allocation logic, explicit effective timestamp columns (effective_at, expiration_at, is_current) for SCD Type 2 dimensions, and Bus Matrix documentation.
6. What anti-patterns indicate a violation of the source's spirit?
   - Anti-patterns include querying 3NF operational tables directly in reporting views, using natural/operational strings as primary/foreign keys in fact tables, over-snowflaking dimension tables into deeply nested hierarchies, and joining facts across different grains without aggregation.

## 6. Key concepts
- Concept 1: Dimensional Modeling & Star Schema
  - Definition: A design technique that structures data into fact tables (containing quantitative metrics) and dimension tables (containing descriptive attributes) connected via foreign keys.
  - Why it matters: Simplifies query structures for end-user BI tools while maximizing data warehouse query execution performance.
  - Related concepts: Fact Table, Dimension Table, Grain.
  - Citation: Chapter 1, Essential Concepts — Dimensional modeling fundamentals.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 2: Fact Table Granularity (Atomic Grain)
  - Definition: The precise level of detail represented by a single row in a fact table (e.g., individual line item on a customer invoice).
  - Why it matters: Preserves maximum analytical flexibility, allowing users to slice and dice metrics at any level of aggregation without losing underlying detail.
  - Related concepts: Transaction Fact Table, Periodic Snapshot, Accumulating Snapshot.
  - Citation: Chapter 2, Dimensional Modeling Techniques — Declaring the grain.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 3: Conformed Dimensions & Bus Matrix
  - Definition: Standardized, shared dimension tables that use identical primary key surrogate values and attribute definitions across multiple business process fact tables.
  - Why it matters: Serves as the architectural backbone of the enterprise data warehouse, enabling cross-process integration and drill-across querying without monolithic joins.
  - Related concepts: Enterprise Bus Architecture, Bus Matrix, Drill-Across.
  - Citation: Chapter 4, Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Architecture — Conformed dimension architecture.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 4: Slowly Changing Dimensions (SCD Types 1, 2, 3, 6)
  - Definition: Techniques used to manage dimensional attribute changes over time (e.g., Type 1 overwrites history, Type 2 adds a new row with effective timestamps, Type 3 adds a historic column).
  - Why it matters: Ensures analytical queries can accurately reflect facts in their historical context at the time the event occurred.
  - Related concepts: Effective Timestamp, Expiration Date, Current Indicator.
  - Citation: Chapter 5, Inventory — Slowly changing dimension strategies.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 5: Surrogate Keys
  - Definition: An integer primary key generated sequentially by the data warehouse ETL pipeline, independent of operational natural keys.
  - Why it matters: Decouples the analytical warehouse from operational system changes, handles unknown/missing values, and enables SCD Type 2 historical row versioning.
  - Related concepts: Natural Key, Durable Key, ETL Staging.
  - Citation: Chapter 1, Essential Concepts — Surrogate key assignment and benefits.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 6: Degenerate Dimension
  - Definition: A dimensional attribute (such as an order number or invoice number) that resides directly in a fact table without joining to a separate dimension table.
  - Why it matters: Allows grouping and transaction tracking without creating superfluous single-column dimension tables.
  - Related concepts: Transaction Fact Table, Line Item Grain.
  - Citation: Chapter 3, Retail Sales — Degenerate dimensions in transaction models.
  - Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
- Pattern 1: SCD Type 2 Dimension Tracking Pattern
  - Problem solved: Loss of historical attribute context when master data changes in upstream operational systems (e.g., customer changes sales region).
  - Recommended approach: Include a surrogate key primary key, operational natural key, effective_start_timestamp, effective_end_timestamp, and is_current_flag in the dimension table schema.
  - Repository-level manifestation: DDL or transformation SQL containing effective_date, expiration_date, and current_flag fields alongside surrogate key generation logic.
  - Limitations: Increases row count in dimension tables and requires strict ETL timestamp maintenance during updates.
  - Source citation: Chapter 5, Inventory — Implementing SCD Type 2 dimension schemas.
- Pattern 2: Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Matrix Pattern
  - Problem solved: Fragmented data engineering projects where teams build isolated, incompatible data marts with duplicate dimensions.
  - Recommended approach: Maintain a version-controlled Bus Matrix document mapping enterprise business processes (rows) against shared conformed dimensions (columns) before writing code.
  - Repository-level manifestation: Markdown or CSV documentation file (e.g., docs/bus_matrix.md) listing processes vs. conformed dimensions.
  - Limitations: Requires cross-team governance and agreement on attribute naming and business rules.
  - Source citation: Chapter 4, Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Architecture — Designing the Bus Matrix.
- Pattern 3: Fact Table Surrogate Key Pipeline Pattern
  - Problem solved: Operational system primary key reassignments or multi-source system natural key collisions corrupting fact table joins.
  - Recommended approach: Join staging fact rows against dimension tables on operational natural keys during ETL/ELT to lookup and write warehouse surrogate keys into the fact table.
  - Repository-level manifestation: SQL model or transformation code joining staging tables with dim_ tables on natural_key to SELECT surrogate_key into the fact table.
  - Limitations: Requires dimensions to be processed and populated before fact table transformation pipelines run.
  - Source citation: Chapter 18, ETL Subsystems & Architecture — Surrogate key lookup processing.

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
1. Natural Keys Used as Fact Foreign Keys
   - Why it happens: Developers copy string or operational integer keys directly into fact tables without mapping them to surrogate dimension keys.
   - Early warning signs: Inability to support SCD Type 2 historical tracking and broken joins when operational systems reset key sequences.
   - Repository evidence: Fact table schemas referencing natural string IDs (e.g., customer_code) instead of warehouse surrogate keys (e.g., customer_sk).
   - Severity tendency: error
2. Missing SCD Type 2 Effective Timestamps
   - Why it happens: Implementing SCD Type 2 versioning without defining standardized expiration timestamps or current row indicators.
   - Early warning signs: Analytical queries return duplicate rows when joining facts to dimensions, or fail to filter for the current attribute state.
   - Repository evidence: Dimension tables marked for historical tracking lacking effective_at / expiration_at or is_current columns.
   - Severity tendency: error
3. Direct Reporting Queries Against Operational 3NF Schemas
   - Why it happens: Teams attempt to build BI dashboards directly on transactional database replicas to avoid building an ELT dimensional model.
   - Early warning signs: Severe database query timeouts, complex multi-table joins in BI models, and unreadable reporting logic.
   - Repository evidence: Analytics queries or BI models querying normalized relational tables (3NF) directly without fact/dimension abstraction layers.
   - Severity tendency: critical
4. Un-Conformed Dimension Silos
   - Why it happens: Independent domain teams build separate customer or product dimensions with inconsistent attribute names and definitions.
   - Early warning signs: Inability to reconcile sales metrics across departments and conflicting KPI reports in leadership meetings.
   - Repository evidence: Multiple dimension models representing the same entity (e.g., dim_cust_marketing vs. dim_customer_sales) with distinct surrogate key logic.
   - Severity tendency: warning

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated)
- Presence of fact model files referencing string natural keys instead of integer surrogate keys in foreign key fields.
- Absence of effective timestamp columns (`effective_at`, `expiration_at`, or `is_current`) in dimension model schemas designated as SCD Type 2.
- Absence of a documented Bus Matrix or conformed dimension definition file in `docs/` or project repository.
- Detection of snowflake schema patterns with deeply nested dimension-to-dimension SQL joins in marts models.

### Needs-review candidates
- Verification that fact table SQL models maintain a single consistent atomic grain throughout all JOIN and GROUP BY clauses.
- Verification that dimension surrogate keys are populated via deterministic lookup pipelines prior to fact table insertion.
- Inspection of accumulating snapshot fact models to ensure milestone timestamp columns are properly initialized.

### Human review (contextual & organizational)
- Evaluating whether business process definitions in the Bus Matrix reflect organizational enterprise boundaries.
- Assessing whether surrogate key collision handling logic is robust across multi-source ingestion systems.
- Reviewing whether business metrics (e.g., Gross Margin, Active Customer) adhere to enterprise governance definitions.

### Organizational artifacts check
- Data dictionary or Bus Matrix documentation file (`docs/bus_matrix.md`) mapping business processes to conformed dimensions.
- Architecture Decision Records (ADRs) establishing surrogate key allocation strategies across staging and presentation layers.

## 10. Candidate rules

- Rule ID: dataops-kimball-001
- Title: Fact Table Natural Key Foreign Key Detection
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Using operational natural keys directly in fact tables prevents historical SCD Type 2 version lookup and risks key collision across disparate source systems.
- Source-backed basis: Fact tables must utilize data warehouse surrogate keys as foreign keys to join with dimension tables.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Fact SQL model column definitions or schema files using operational string codes (e.g., customer_code) as join keys instead of surrogate keys (e.g., customer_sk).
- Pass condition: All foreign key references in fact table schemas join on warehouse surrogate keys (e.g., ending in _sk or _id surrogate integers).
- Fail condition: Fact table schemas reference raw natural string keys in join relationships with dimension tables.
- Exceptions or limitations: Degenerate dimension attributes (such as invoice_number) that reside directly on the fact row without a dimension join.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Chapter 1, Essential Concepts — Surrogate key assignment and usage in fact tables.
- Source: The Data Warehouse Toolkit
- Structural locator: Chapter 1, Essential Concepts
- Spirit locator: Foreign keys in fact tables must be surrogate integers to maintain operational independence and support historical dimensional versioning.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-kimball-002
- Title: Missing Effective Timestamp Columns in SCD Type 2 Dimensions
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: SCD Type 2 dimension tables require explicit effective timestamps and current indicators to resolve point-in-time historical joins correctly.
- Source-backed basis: Managing attribute change over time requires effective date tracking columns to maintain temporal historical fidelity.
- Repository evidence to inspect: SQL or YAML model files for dimension models configured for SCD Type 2 historical tracking.
- Pass condition: Dimension models containing historical tracking include effective date/timestamp columns (e.g., effective_at, expiration_at, or is_current).
- Fail condition: A dimension table with historical row versioning lacks effective date or current indicator columns.
- Exceptions or limitations: SCD Type 1 dimensions where changes overwrite existing attributes without maintaining history.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Chapter 5, Inventory — Slowly changing dimension Type 2 schema requirements.
- Source: The Data Warehouse Toolkit
- Structural locator: Chapter 5, Inventory
- Spirit locator: Preserving point-in-time attribute states requires standardized effective timestamps and current row flags in dimension schemas.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-kimball-003
- Title: Missing Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Matrix Documentation
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Building data warehouse models without an enterprise Bus Matrix risks creating un-conformed data silos and duplicated dimension logic.
- Source-backed basis: The Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Architecture requires a Bus Matrix to plan and govern shared conformed dimensions.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Presence of a Bus Matrix design document (e.g., docs/bus_matrix.md or docs/bus_matrix.csv) in the project repository.
- Pass condition: A Bus Matrix documentation file exists in the repository mapping business processes to conformed dimensions.
- Fail condition: No Bus Matrix or conformed dimension documentation exists in the project repository.
- Exceptions or limitations: Single-process analytics projects with no secondary business domains.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Chapter 4, Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Architecture — Bus Matrix planning.
- Source: The Data Warehouse Toolkit
- Structural locator: Chapter 4, Enterprise Data Warehouse Bus Architecture
- Spirit locator: The Bus Matrix serves as the foundational architectural blueprint for conformed enterprise dimensional modeling.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment
```yaml
rules:
  - id: "dataops-kimball-001"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "error"
    description: "Detects operational natural keys used as foreign keys in fact table schemas instead of surrogate keys."
    check:
      type: "pattern_match"
      file_glob: "models/marts/**/fact_*.sql"
      pattern: "(?i)(join|on)\s+[a-z0-9_]+\.[a-z0-9_]+_code\s*="
      error_when: "pattern_present"
      message: "Natural key string used in fact join. Use warehouse surrogate keys (_sk) for fact-dimension joins."
    references:
      - book: "The Data Warehouse Toolkit"
        concept: "surrogate-keys"

  - id: "dataops-kimball-002"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "error"
    description: "Ensures SCD Type 2 dimension models contain effective timestamp and current indicator columns."
    check:
      type: "pattern_match"
      file_glob: "models/marts/**/dim_*.sql"
      pattern: "(?i)(effective_at|valid_from|dbt_valid_from|is_current)"
      error_when: "pattern_absent"
      message: "Dimension model appears to lack SCD Type 2 effective timestamp tracking columns."
    references:
      - book: "The Data Warehouse Toolkit"
        concept: "slowly-changing-dimensions"

  - id: "dataops-kimball-003"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "warning"
    description: "Ensures Bus Matrix architecture documentation exists in the repository."
    check:
      type: "file_exists"
      file_glob: "docs/*bus_matrix*"
      error_when: "file_absent"
      message: "Bus Matrix documentation is missing. Create docs/bus_matrix.md to track conformed dimensions."
    references:
      - book: "The Data Warehouse Toolkit"
        concept: "bus-architecture"
```
