===== FILE: docs/notes/iiot/industrial-network-security.md =====
# Industrial Network Security — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source Identity

- **Title:** Industrial Network Security

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| **Title** | Industrial Network Security: Securing Critical Infrastructure Networks for Smart Grid, SCADA, and Other Industrial Control Systems |
| **Author(s)** | Eric D. Knapp |
| **Branch** | iiot |
| **RepoScope profile** | iiot |
| **Primary source file** | `Eric D. Knapp - Industrial Network Security_ Securing Critical Infrastructure Networks for Smart Grid, SCADA, and Other Industrial Control Syst (2011, Syngress).pdf` |
| **Note status** | reviewed-with-candidate-rules |

---

## 2. Scope

### In scope

- Technical architecture of industrial control systems (ICS), Distributed Control Systems (DCS), and SCADA networks across business, supervisory, and control tiers.
- Vulnerabilities, protocol structures, and defensive strategies for industrial communication protocols including Modbus TCP, DNP3, ICCP/TASE.2, OPC, Ethernet/IP, and EtherCAT.
- Establishment and perimeter isolation of functional enclaves using firewalls, Intrusion Detection/Prevention Systems (IDS/IPS), data diodes, and application session monitors.
- Anomaly detection, behavioral whitelisting, event correlation across IT and OT boundaries, and log management for situational awareness.
- Alignment of repository and network security configurations with regulatory frameworks including NERC CIP, NRC RG 5.71, NIST SP 800-82, CFATS, and ISA-99.

### Out of scope

- General enterprise IT security practices that lack specific operational technology (OT) or physical safety impacts.
- Detailed physical security, door access controls, fence monitoring, and environmental controls except where intersecting network perimeters.
- Personnel background screening, human resources policy details, and manual site security management.

---

## 3. Source Spirit Card

1. **Core philosophy:** Industrial network security must prioritize operational safety, system availability, and deterministic real-time delivery over IT-centric concepts of high bandwidth, frequent refactoring, or aggressive automated blocking.
2. **Dominant architectural bias:** Defense-in-depth structured around strict functional enclave separation, where traffic across enclave boundaries is denied by default and restricted to explicitly whitelisted protocol paths.
3. **Stance on complexity:** System complexity increases attack surface and causes rule misconfigurations; security mechanisms must remain explicit, minimal, and transparent to prevent unexpected operational failure.
4. **View of developer/operator error:** Human operator or developer misconfiguration is an inevitable risk that must be bounded by automated structural isolation, strict access boundaries, and continuous anomaly auditing.
5. **Highest-priority system virtue:** Deterministic operational reliability and safety, ensuring that security controls never inadvertently disrupt control loops or halt critical physical processes.
6. **Acceptable trade-offs:** Sacrificing remote management convenience, network agility, and real-time enterprise-wide visibility to maintain rigorous perimeter isolation and unidirectional data flows.
7. **Unacceptable trade-offs:** Allowing direct, unmediated corporate-to-controller connectivity or deploying active IPS blocking that generates false positives capable of tripping physical plant operations.
8. **Mental model of repository / system state:** A structured topology of static network enclaves, explicit firewall variables, immutable protocol definitions, and deterministic tag/logic configurations.
9. **Attitude toward automation vs human oversight:** Automated tools provide continuous event collection and anomaly detection, but final operational judgment and risk assessment require experienced human oversight.
10. **Defining failure mode according to source:** Loss of situational awareness leading to undetected lateral compromise, untracked controller logic injection, or unexpected physical process disruption.

---

## 4. Source-Backed Structure

### Theme 1: Enclave Architecture and Boundary Isolation

- **Source:** Chapter 2 (About Industrial Networks) and Chapter 7 (Establishing Secure Enclaves)
- **Structural locator:** Chapter 2: Common Industrial Security Recommendations — Network Segmentation / Chapter 7: Establishing Secure Enclaves — Identifying Functional Groups
- **Spirit locator:** Industrial systems must be divided into tightly bounded functional enclaves rather than relying on a flat network or a single corporate firewall, ensuring that compromise within one tier cannot propagate to critical control loops.

### Theme 2: Industrial Protocol Security and Deep Packet Inspection

- **Source:** Chapter 4 (Industrial Network Protocols) and Chapter 7 (Securing Enclave Perimeters)
- **Structural locator:** Chapter 4: Overview of Industrial Network Protocols / Chapter 7: Securing Enclave Perimeters — Implementing Perimeter Security Devices
- **Spirit locator:** Legacy industrial protocols lack native encryption and authentication; securing them requires perimeter filtering, session-level protocol decoding, and restricting write/system function codes to authorized endpoints.

### Theme 3: Exception Detection, Whitelisting, and OT/IT Correlation

- **Source:** Chapter 8 (Exception, Anomaly, and Threat Detection) and Chapter 9 (Monitoring Enclaves)
- **Structural locator:** Chapter 8: Behavioral Whitelisting / Chapter 9: Monitoring Enclaves — Information Management
- **Spirit locator:** Because industrial control communications are highly repetitive and predictable, security monitoring should leverage behavioral whitelisting, exception reporting, and cross-tier event correlation to detect zero-day threats like Stuxnet.

### Theme 4: Regulatory Mapping and Configuration Management

- **Source:** Chapter 6 (Vulnerability and Risk Assessment) and Chapter 10 (Standards and Regulations)
- **Structural locator:** Chapter 6: Vulnerability Management / Chapter 10: Mapping Industrial Network Security to Compliance
- **Spirit locator:** Security and compliance are complementary when regulatory controls (such as NERC CIP and NRC RG 5.71) are mapped directly to technical configuration artifacts, version-controlled tag bases, and automated perimeter audits.

---

## 5. Source-Backed Spirit

1. **Primary purpose of system architecture:** To establish clear operational enclaves that isolate physical process control loops from untrusted external networks, preventing unauthorized control commands from altering plant state.
2. **Acceptable vs unacceptable risk:** Acceptable risk includes minor delays in enterprise reporting; unacceptable risk includes unauthenticated remote write commands, single points of failure across OT boundaries, and active IPS false positives that shut down production.
3. **Implicit assumptions vs explicit declarations:** All network flows, user credentials, and protocol function codes must be explicitly declared in whitelist configurations; implicit trust or open default-allow rules are strictly prohibited.
4. **Speed/convenience vs safety/correctness:** Safety and correctness unconditionally override speed and convenience; remote access and cross-zone connectivity must pass through jump boxes, VPNs, or unidirectional data diodes regardless of administrative friction.
5. **Human intervention vs automated enforcement:** Automated systems are essential for packet filtering, log aggregation, and anomaly detection, but critical control decisions and threat validations must involve human operators to prevent unintended outages.
6. **Legacy constraints and backward compatibility:** Legacy controllers lacking modern security features must be encapsulated behind physical or logical security proxies and monitored via passive network taps rather than modified directly.

---

## 6. Key Concepts

### 6.1 Electronic Security Perimeter (ESP)

- **Definition:** The logical border surrounding a set of critical cyber assets that enforces strict access control policies via firewalls, IPS devices, or unidirectional gateways.
- **Why it matters:** Establishes a verifiable boundary for auditing and prevents untrusted network traffic from reaching sensitive controllers.
- **Related concepts:** Functional Enclave, Zone and Conduit Model, DMZ.
- **Citation:** Industrial Network Security / electronic-security-perimeter
- **Verification status:** source-backed

### 6.2 Functional Enclave

- **Definition:** A logical grouping of assets, protocols, and authorized users that perform a specific control task and communicate exclusively among themselves.
- **Why it matters:** Reduces attack surface by restricting communications to expected operational pairs and enabling precise behavioral whitelisting.
- **Related concepts:** Segmentation, Least Privilege, Micro-segmentation.
- **Citation:** Industrial Network Security / functional-enclave
- **Verification status:** source-backed

### 6.3 Deep Packet Inspection (DPI)

- **Definition:** The inspection of packet payloads down to the industrial protocol application layer to decode specific function codes, register requests, and data values.
- **Why it matters:** Allows firewalls and IDS units to distinguish benign read commands from potentially dangerous write or system restart commands.
- **Related concepts:** Application Session Inspection, Industrial Protocol Filter, Modbus Function Code.
- **Citation:** Industrial Network Security / deep-packet-inspection
- **Verification status:** source-backed

### 6.4 Behavioral Whitelisting (Smart-Listing)

- **Definition:** A security baseline strategy that explicitly defines allowed IP addresses, ports, protocols, and function codes, alerting or blocking any deviation.
- **Why it matters:** Outperforms signature-based anti-virus against zero-day exploits and targeted attacks like Stuxnet in predictable OT environments.
- **Related concepts:** Anomaly Detection, Exception Reporting, Zero Trust.
- **Citation:** Industrial Network Security / behavioral-whitelisting
- **Verification status:** source-backed

### 6.5 Disjointed Protocol Path

- **Definition:** An architectural pattern where industrial protocols (e.g., Modbus or DNP3) terminate in an intermediate DMZ and are never routed directly from corporate LAN to control loops.
- **Why it matters:** Prevents an attacker who breaches the corporate network from directly traversing to PLCs over open industrial ports.
- **Related concepts:** SCADA DMZ, Protocol Gateway, Multi-Tier Defense.
- **Citation:** Industrial Network Security / disjointed-protocol-path
- **Verification status:** source-backed

### 6.6 Unidirectional Security Gateway (Data Diode)

- **Definition:** A hardware or software gateway that physically or logically enforces one-way data transfer from a high-security enclave to a lower-security network.
- **Why it matters:** Allows historian data replication to business networks while mathematically guaranteeing that no cyber attack can traverse back into the control zone.
- **Related concepts:** NRC RG 5.71, Air Gap, Historian Replication.
- **Citation:** Industrial Network Security / unidirectional-gateway
- **Verification status:** source-backed

---

## 7. Practical Patterns and Examples

### Pattern 1: SCADA DMZ with Disjointed Protocol Gateways

- **Problem solved:** Prevents direct network exposure of PLCs and RTUs to business network compromises.
- **Recommended approach:** Deploy two distinct firewalls surrounding a SCADA DMZ. Terminate corporate connections at the outer firewall and require data historians or HMI servers in the DMZ to re-originate queries to the inner control network over dedicated, non-routable interfaces.
- **Repository-level manifestation:** Configuration files (`firewall.conf`, `network-topology.yaml`) defining distinct subnet variables (`$Enterprise_Net`, `$SCADA_DMZ`, `$Control_Enclave`) with explicitly separated rules.
- **Limitations:** Increases infrastructure cost and introduces minimal latency during data relay.
- **Source citation:** Industrial Network Security / scada-dmz-disjointed-path

### Pattern 2: Unidirectional Historian Data Replication

- **Problem solved:** Allows business intelligence and analytics tools to read plant data without opening inbound network ports into critical control enclaves.
- **Recommended approach:** Place a primary data historian inside the control enclave and configure it to push data outwards through a unidirectional data diode to a replicated historian in the SCADA DMZ or corporate network.
- **Repository-level manifestation:** Deployment manifests (`docker-compose.yml`, `gateway-config.toml`) specifying one-way data transport agents with no listening ports on the internal diode interface.
- **Limitations:** Inbound control or configuration changes cannot be sent back over the diode channel.
- **Source citation:** Industrial Network Security / unidirectional-historian-replication

### Pattern 3: Industrial Protocol Function Code Whitelisting

- **Problem solved:** Blocks unauthorized control commands (such as logic rewrites, coil resets, or device restarts) even if an attacker gains access to an authorized IP address.
- **Recommended approach:** Configure network intrusion prevention devices or application firewalls with DPI rules that permit read-only function codes (e.g., Modbus FC 01-04) while dropping or alerting on write/system codes (e.g., Modbus FC 05, 06, 15, 16) unless originating from designated engineering workstations during approved maintenance windows.
- **Repository-level manifestation:** Snort/Suricata rule files (`scada.rules`) or protocol gateway filters explicitly specifying allowed function code bitmasks.
- **Limitations:** Requires detailed maintenance window scheduling to avoid blocking legitimate administrative updates.
- **Source citation:** Industrial Network Security / function-code-whitelisting

---

## 8. Common Failure Modes and Risks

### Failure 1 — The Air Gap Myth & Soft Perimeter Reliance

- **Why it happens:** Organizations assume that physical separation or basic corporate firewalls make internal OT networks immune to external cyber threats.
- **Early warning signs:** Undocumented dual-homed laptops, temporary maintenance modems, unmonitored USB usage, or shared wireless access points.
- **Repository evidence:** Inbound firewall rules containing `0.0.0.0/0` or wildcard target destinations pointing to internal control subnets.
- **Severity tendency:** Critical

### Failure 2 — Executive Override & Direct Corporate-to-ICS Pass-Through

- **Why it happens:** Executive demand for real-time plant KPIs leads administrators to open direct firewall holes from corporate workstations to internal DBs or HMIs.
- **Early warning signs:** Directly routed SQL queries or OPC connections bypassing the SCADA DMZ.
- **Repository evidence:** Firewall configuration scripts enabling direct port mappings (e.g., port 1433, 4840, 502) between enterprise subnets and PLC addresses.
- **Severity tendency:** Error

### Failure 3 — Aggressive Network Scanning in Latency-Sensitive Enclaves

- **Why it happens:** IT security personnel run standard active vulnerability scanners (e.g., Nmap) against fragile industrial control networks.
- **Early warning signs:** PLC buffer overflows, unexpected controller resets, or missed real-time scan cycles during routine IT vulnerability sweeps.
- **Repository evidence:** CI/CD automation scripts invoking high-rate port scans against subnets containing fieldbus controllers.
- **Severity tendency:** Error

### Failure 4 — Active IPS False Positives Halting Operations

- **Why it happens:** Deploying intrusion prevention systems in active drop/reject mode without exhaustive baseline testing against industrial protocols.
- **Early warning signs:** Spurious packet drops during legitimate, low-frequency operational tasks or unexpected valve shutdowns following IPS deployment.
- **Repository evidence:** IPS rules configured with `drop` or `reject` actions on broad industrial protocol signatures without prior audit mode testing.
- **Severity tendency:** Error

---

## 9. Repository-Audit Translation

### Static checks (automated)

- Verification of repository manifests for explicit protocol port declarations and enclave subnet definitions.
- Detection of hardcoded IP addresses, unencrypted protocol configurations, default credentials, or missing security documentation.
- Validation that configuration files specify explicit source/destination pairs rather than wildcard allow rules.

### Human review (operational & safety-culture)

- Operational review of physical interlock integrity and manual emergency override procedures.
- Inspection of plant maintenance logs and jump box jump-host usage protocols.
- Evaluation of site-level physical lockout/tagout procedures and operator security awareness training.

### Safety-critical exclusions

- Physical safety interlocks, mechanical relief valve settings, and hardwired Master Control Relays (MCR) must never be evaluated or modified by static software repository checks.
- Human safety culture, emergency shutdown readiness, and site physical access controls are explicitly excluded from static automated auditing.

---

## 10. Candidate Rules

### Rule 1 — `iiot-network-001`

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| **Rule ID** | `iiot-network-001` |
| **Rule Title** | Unencrypted Default Industrial Protocol Telemetry Exposure |
| **Profile** | iiot |
| **Severity tendency** | error |
| **Rationale** | Modbus TCP (port 502) and standard DNP3 (port 20000) transmit cleartext control commands and telemetries without built-in authentication or encryption. Referencing these ports in unsegmented or default configuration manifests exposes control loops to unauthorized command injection. |
| **Source-backed basis** | Knapp Chapter 4 & Chapter 7 emphasize that legacy Modbus and DNP3 lack native encryption and authentication, requiring explicit enclave perimeter filtering and protocol-aware encapsulation or proxying. |
| **Repository evidence to inspect** | Configuration files (`*.yaml`, `*.yml`, `*.json`, `*.toml`, `*.ini`, `*.env`, `*.conf`) containing exposed industrial port definitions. |
| **Pass condition** | Industrial protocol ports are either absent or bound strictly within designated enclave variables and isolated subnets. |
| **Fail condition** | Industrial protocol ports (502, 20000) are defined with unrestricted listening interfaces or direct corporate routing. |
| **Exceptions or limitations** | Test environment files explicitly marked as local mocks or virtual simulators. |
| **YAML readiness** | ready |
| **Source citation** | Industrial Network Security / electronic-security-perimeter |
| **Source** | Eric D. Knapp - Industrial Network Security |
| **Structural locator** | Chapter 4: Industrial Network Protocols — Modbus / Chapter 7: Establishing Secure Enclaves — Securing Enclave Perimeters |
| **Spirit locator** | Industrial protocols lacking native security controls must be strictly bounded within isolated enclaves and explicitly filtered at perimeters. |
| **Verification status** | source-backed |

---

### Rule 2 — `iiot-credential-001`

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| **Rule ID** | `iiot-credential-001` |
| **Rule Title** | Hardcoded OT/SCADA Passwords or Credentials |
| **Profile** | iiot |
| **Severity tendency** | critical |
| **Rationale** | Storing plaintext passwords or default vendor credentials in deployment scripts, PLC configuration dumps, or gateway configuration files enables unauthorized enclave entry and privilege escalation. |
| **Source-backed basis** | Knapp Chapter 6 & Chapter 11 identify hardcoded credentials and default administrative passwords as primary vulnerability vectors enabling unauthorized enclave entry and Stuxnet-like lateral movement. |
| **Repository evidence to inspect** | Manifests, scripts, and configuration files (`*.yaml`, `*.yml`, `*.json`, `*.toml`, `*.ini`, `*.st`, `*.scl`, `*.env`) for hardcoded password strings. |
| **Pass condition** | No plaintext passwords or default credential strings exist in repository configuration files. |
| **Fail condition** | Password fields contain obvious default or hardcoded strings (e.g., `admin`, `password`, `123456`, `root`, `modbus`, `scada`). |
| **Exceptions or limitations** | Public example files (`.env.example`) containing explicitly non-functional placeholder values. |
| **YAML readiness** | ready |
| **Source citation** | Industrial Network Security / default-accounts-and-passwords |
| **Source** | Eric D. Knapp - Industrial Network Security |
| **Structural locator** | Chapter 6: Vulnerability and Risk Assessment — Common Vulnerabilities / Chapter 11: Common Pitfalls and Mistakes — Misconfigurations |
| **Spirit locator** | Default passwords and hardcoded credentials represent severe configuration weaknesses that bypass perimeter controls. |
| **Verification status** | source-backed |

---

### Rule 3 — `iiot-change-001`

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| **Rule ID** | `iiot-change-001` |
| **Rule Title** | Missing Controller Configuration Changelog or Deployment Manifest |
| **Profile** | iiot |
| **Severity tendency** | warning |
| **Rationale** | Failure to maintain a version-controlled change record for controller tags, logic modifications, or PLC configurations prevents auditability and obscures unauthorized logic modifications. |
| **Source-backed basis** | Knapp Chapter 10 (mapping NERC CIP-003-4 R6 and CIP-007-4) mandates change control and configuration documentation for all Critical Cyber Assets. |
| **Repository evidence to inspect** | Repository root and controller configuration directories for documentation files (`CHANGELOG*`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `MANIFEST*`). |
| **Pass condition** | A valid changelog or modification manifest exists in repository controller configuration paths. |
| **Fail condition** | Repository lacks a CHANGELOG or deployment manifest file in controller source directories. |
| **Exceptions or limitations** | None. |
| **YAML readiness** | ready |
| **Source citation** | Industrial Network Security / change-control-and-configuration-management |
| **Source** | Eric D. Knapp - Industrial Network Security |
| **Structural locator** | Chapter 10: Standards and Regulations — Mapping Industrial Network Security to Compliance |
| **Spirit locator** | Automated change identification and documentation are mandatory controls for maintaining configuration assurance in critical cyber assets. |
| **Verification status** | source-backed |

---

### Rule 4 — `iiot-safety-001`

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| **Rule ID** | `iiot-safety-001` |
| **Rule Title** | Unvalidated Industrial Write Function Code Execution Across Enclave Perimeters |
| **Profile** | iiot |
| **Severity tendency** | critical |
| **Rationale** | Executing Modbus write function codes or DNP3 operate commands across enclave boundaries without deep packet inspection or secondary authorization can cause unsafe state transitions. |
| **Source-backed basis** | Knapp Chapter 7 & Chapter 8 recommend restricting industrial write function codes across enclaves using DPI and application session monitors. |
| **Repository evidence to inspect** | Network filter configurations, gateway scripts, and Snort/Suricata rules. |
| **Pass condition** | Deep packet inspection rules explicitly filter write function codes at perimeter gateways. |
| **Fail condition** | Generic permit rules allow uninspected industrial write commands across enclave perimeters. |
| **Exceptions or limitations** | Temporary maintenance window exceptions with explicit time-to-live restrictions. |
| **YAML readiness** | needs-review |
| **Reason if not ready** | Static regex cannot reliably perform stateful session reassembly or verify whether an inline SCADA IPS or application monitor is performing deep packet inspection. |
| **Source citation** | Industrial Network Security / function-code-whitelisting |
| **Source** | Eric D. Knapp - Industrial Network Security |
| **Structural locator** | Chapter 7: Establishing Secure Enclaves — Securing Enclave Perimeters / Chapter 8: Exception, Anomaly, and Threat Detection — Behavioral Whitelisting |
| **Spirit locator** | Protocol function codes must be inspected and bounded to prevent unauthorized physical state changes. |
| **Verification status** | source-backed |

## 11. Suggested YAML Fragment

```yaml
rules:
  - id: iiot-network-001
    title: Unencrypted Default Industrial Protocol Telemetry Exposure
    profile: iiot
    severity: error
    message: "Industrial protocol ports (Modbus TCP 502 / DNP3 20000) detected in configuration without documented enclave boundary separation."
    references:
      - book: "Industrial Network Security: Securing Critical Infrastructure Networks for Smart Grid, SCADA, and Other Industrial Control Systems"
        concept: "electronic-security-perimeter"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{yaml,yml,json,toml,ini,env,conf}"
      pattern: "(502|20000)"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_present

  - id: iiot-credential-001
    title: Hardcoded OT/SCADA Passwords or Credentials
    profile: iiot
    severity: critical
    message: "Hardcoded or default credential pattern detected in industrial control repository configuration."
    references:
      - book: "Industrial Network Security: Securing Critical Infrastructure Networks for Smart Grid, SCADA, and Other Industrial Control Systems"
        concept: "default-accounts-and-passwords"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{yaml,yml,json,toml,ini,st,scl,env}"
      pattern: "(?i)(password|passwd|secret)\\s*[:=]\\s*[\"']?(admin|password|123456|root|modbus|scada)[\"']?"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_present

  - id: iiot-change-001
    title: Missing Controller Configuration Changelog or Deployment Manifest
    profile: iiot
    severity: warning
    message: "Repository lacks a top-level CHANGELOG or modification audit manifest for industrial controller configurations."
    references:
      - book: "Industrial Network Security: Securing Critical Infrastructure Networks for Smart Grid, SCADA, and Other Industrial Control Systems"
        concept: "change-control-and-configuration-management"
    check:
      type: file_absent
      file_glob: "**/CHANGELOG*"
      error_when: file_absent

- [x] All emitted rule IDs are unique.
- [x] All emitted severities match Section 10.
- [x] All emitted references use `{book|standard, concept}`.
- [x] All emitted check types are in `{pattern_match, file_exists, file_absent}`.
- [x] All "ready" rules appear in Section 11 YAML.
- [x] All "needs-review" / "blocked" rules are absent from Section 11 YAML.

- [x] No page numbers anywhere in the note.
- [x] No verbatim quotes longer than 15 words.
- [x] Every supplied rule ID is either used with a source-backed meaning or explicitly marked "Meaning to be confirmed."
- [x] "Note status" reflects the weakest readiness in Section 10.
- [x] Section 9 does not smuggle non-static advice into the automated list.

# Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
- Title: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt: Building Meaningful Data Models at Scale
- Author(s): Rui Machado and Hélder Russa
- Branch: dataops
- RepoScope profile: dataops
- Primary source file: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt_ Building Meaningful Data Models at Scale.pdf
- Note status: partially-ready-for-YAML

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Transformation layer architecture within the Extract-Load-Transform (ELT) paradigm.
  - Modular SQL data modeling conventions, including staging, intermediate, and marts layers.
  - dbt project organization, Jinja templating, SQL macros, and dbt packages.
  - Automated testing (singular and generic), source freshness monitoring, and Markdown doc blocks.
- Out of scope:
  - Data ingestion engine configurations (e.g., Fivetran or Airbyte connector setup).
  - Cloud data warehouse infrastructure provisioning (e.g., IAM roles, BigQuery dataset creation commands).
  - Business Intelligence (BI) visualization dashboard building in external tools (e.g., Tableau, Looker).

## 3. Source spirit card
- Source title: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt: Building Meaningful Data Models at Scale
- Core architectural posture: Treat data transformations as a version-controlled, modular, and testable software platform within an ELT cloud warehouse paradigm.
- Primary operational invariant: Every analytics transformation must be modular, reproducible, testable, and documented in code before deployment to production.
- Default abstraction layer: Modular SQL SELECT models organized across staging, intermediate, and marts layers, connected via explicit DAG references and sources.
- System boundary definition: Bounded strictly within the data transformation and serving layer of the cloud data warehouse platform, downstream of raw ingestion and upstream of BI consumption.
- Target failure model: Undetected schema drift, breaking downstream models, unverified primary key constraints, duplicate metric logic, and stale source data landing silently in production.
- Primary trade-off accepted: Accepts higher storage and compute consumption in cloud data platforms in exchange for modular SQL, rapid iteration, and maintainable transformations.
- Key repository evidence: Presence of dbt_project.yml, structured models directory (staging/intermediate/marts), packages.yml, SQL model files using ref functions, and schema YAML test assertions.
- Anti-pattern rejected: Monolithic multi-thousand-line SQL queries, hardcoded database/schema/table string references, un-versioned stored procedures, and undocumented transformation logic.
- Success criterion: A fully version-controlled, self-documenting dbt project with automated test execution on every build and clear lineage tracking across raw sources to business marts.

## 4. Source-backed structure
- Theme 1: Evolution from ETL to ELT and the Analytics Engineer Role
  - Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
  - Structural locator: Chapter 1, Analytics Engineering
  - Spirit locator: Modern data architectures separate raw ingestion from transformation, establishing the analytics engineer role to apply software engineering discipline to SQL modeling.
- Theme 2: Modular Data Modeling Layering
  - Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
  - Structural locator: Chapter 2, Data Modeling for Analytics
  - Spirit locator: Decomposing complex queries into staging, intermediate, and dimensional marts layers using the DRY principle isolates source changes and optimizes model reuse.
- Theme 3: dbt Project Structure and Model Execution
  - Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
  - Structural locator: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt
  - Spirit locator: Standardizing file system layout, YAML configurations, and Jinja referencing ensures transparent DAG compilation, reproducible builds, and version-controlled transformations.
- Theme 4: Materialization Strategies, Macros, and Semantic Layer
  - Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
  - Structural locator: Chapter 5, dbt Advanced Topics
  - Spirit locator: Applying targeted materialization strategies (views, tables, incremental, ephemeral) alongside reusable SQL macros and semantic metrics optimizes query performance and enforces business consistency.
- Theme 5: End-to-End Analytics Engineering Pipeline Implementation
  - Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
  - Structural locator: Chapter 6, Building an End-to-End Analytics Engineering Use Case
  - Spirit locator: Integrating data staging, dimensional modeling, generic and singular test execution, and automated documentation generation establishes a resilient production data product pipeline.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. What architectural posture does this source assume?
   - The source assumes an ELT posture where transformation logic occurs directly within cloud data warehouses using code-centric, version-controlled SQL and Jinja templating rather than GUI-based ETL tools or opaque stored procedures.
2. What is the fundamental invariant the system must preserve?
   - The fundamental invariant is that all transformations must be deterministic, modular, fully testable, and traceable through a directed acyclic graph (DAG) where every downstream model references upstream dependencies via explicit dbt ref constructs.
3. What operational trade-offs does the source explicitly accept?
   - The source accepts increased warehouse compute costs and temporary storage overhead from staging and intermediate materializations to maximize developer productivity, code reusability, and automated quality verification.
4. How does the source define failure or risk?
   - The source defines failure as unmonitored data quality degradation, circular or broken dependency lineages, hardcoded database paths that prevent multi-environment deployment, silent primary key duplication, and stale data reaching business consumers.
5. What repository-level artifacts demonstrate adherence to this spirit?
   - Adherence is demonstrated by a well-structured repository containing dbt_project.yml, modular SQL models in models/staging/, models/intermediate/, and models/marts/, packages.yml dependency manifests, schema testing YAML files, and singular test SQL queries.
6. What anti-patterns indicate a violation of the source's spirit?
   - Anti-patterns include writing monolithic queries with nested subqueries across thousands of lines, referencing raw target tables directly using hardcoded strings, committing macro directories without package declarations, and running dbt projects without generic schema tests or documentation blocks.

## 6. Key concepts
- Concept 1: Analytics Engineer
  - Definition: A technical role bridging data engineering and data science, responsible for writing clean, version-controlled, testable SQL transformations.
  - Why it matters: Prevents organizational bottlenecks by empowering data teams to deliver self-service data products with software engineering rigor.
  - Related concepts: ELT, Data Mesh, Data Product.
  - Citation: Chapter 1, Analytics Engineering — Analytics engineer role as the bridge between engineering and analytics.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 2: Modular Data Modeling (Staging, Intermediate, Marts)
  - Definition: Structuring SQL models into distinct layers where staging cleans raw sources, intermediate applies complex joins/business logic, and marts serve domain dimensions and facts.
  - Why it matters: Adheres to the DRY principle, reduces duplicate logic, and isolates downstream models from upstream source schema changes.
  - Related concepts: Staging Layer, Marts Layer, DAG.
  - Citation: Chapter 2, Data Modeling for Analytics — Building modular data models using dbt.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 3: Model Referencing (ref function)
  - Definition: A Jinja templating function that replaces hardcoded table names with dynamic references, compiling environment-specific database relations and building the project DAG.
  - Why it matters: Enables seamless deployment across development, staging, and production environments while auto-generating lineage dependency graphs.
  - Related concepts: Directed Acyclic Graph, Jinja, Dynamic SQL.
  - Citation: Chapter 2, Data Modeling for Analytics — Modular data models referencing syntax.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 4: Materialization Strategies (View, Table, Ephemeral, Incremental)
  - Definition: Configuration directives determining how dbt models are persisted in the data warehouse, balancing query runtime against compute build time.
  - Why it matters: Prevents performance bottlenecks and drastically reduces warehouse processing costs for large, high-volume datasets.
  - Related concepts: Incremental Model, Ephemeral Model, Materialized View.
  - Citation: Chapter 5, dbt Advanced Topics — Model materializations and performance optimization.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 5: Generic and Singular Testing
  - Definition: Generic tests are reusable parameter-driven assertions defined in YAML (e.g., unique, not_null), whereas singular tests are custom SQL queries returning failing rows.
  - Why it matters: Catches data quality anomalies early in the transformation pipeline before flawed data reaches reporting dashboards.
  - Related concepts: Source Freshness, Schema Validation, Data Quality.
  - Citation: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt — Tests and quality verification in dbt.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 6: Source Freshness Monitoring
  - Definition: An automated audit check comparing a source table's max timestamp against defined warning and error time thresholds.
  - Why it matters: Alerts data operators when upstream ingestion pipelines stall or fail, preventing decisions based on stale information.
  - Related concepts: loaded_at_field, SLA, Data Observability.
  - Citation: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt — Source freshness testing configuration.
  - Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
- Pattern 1: Folder-Based Layering and Configuration Pattern
  - Problem solved: Messy, unstructured dbt projects where model materialization, owner, and access settings are hard to manage and audit.
  - Recommended approach: Organize models into clear subdirectories (staging/, intermediate/, marts/) and configure default materialization hierarchies in dbt_project.yml using layer-level config blocks.
  - Repository-level manifestation: models/ directory containing staging/, intermediate/, and marts/ subfolders, with matching configuration blocks in dbt_project.yml.
  - Limitations: Requires disciplined adherence across the data team; mislocated SQL files bypass folder-level configuration defaults.
  - Source citation: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt — Structure of a dbt project and directory organization.
- Pattern 2: Dynamic Incremental Filtering Pattern
  - Problem solved: Exorbitant query compute costs and long build times caused by repeatedly re-transforming full historical tables on every run.
  - Recommended approach: Configure materialized='incremental' in the model config block and append an is_incremental() Jinja conditional filter against the target table's max timestamp.
  - Repository-level manifestation: SQL files in models/ containing config materialized='incremental' and Jinja conditional checks against the target table max timestamp.
  - Limitations: Requires a reliable, monotonically increasing timestamp column in the source table and handling of late-arriving data.
  - Source citation: Chapter 5, dbt Advanced Topics — Incremental models and optimization.
- Pattern 3: Reusable SQL Macro Abstraction Pattern
  - Problem solved: Duplicated SQL transformation snippet logic (such as monetary conversions or custom surrogate key hashing) repeated across multiple models.
  - Recommended approach: Extract repeated SQL constructs into Jinja macro files saved in the macros/ folder, parameterize variables, and invoke them across models.
  - Repository-level manifestation: .sql files in macros/ defining custom macro functions and model SQL files invoking those macros.
  - Limitations: Over-macroizing SQL queries can decrease readability for analysts unfamiliar with Jinja templating.
  - Source citation: Chapter 5, dbt Advanced Topics — Dynamic SQL with Jinja and SQL macros.

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
1. Hardcoded Relation References
   - Why it happens: Developers copy-paste legacy SQL queries containing explicit database, schema, and table strings directly into dbt models.
   - Early warning signs: dbt run fails in development or staging environments due to cross-database permission errors or writing to production tables.
   - Repository evidence: SQL model files containing raw string literals in FROM or JOIN clauses instead of ref or source constructs.
   - Severity tendency: critical
2. Unvalidated Primary Key Constraints
   - Why it happens: Assuming raw source data is unique without configuring automated uniqueness and non-null schema assertions in dbt YAML files.
   - Early warning signs: Downstream mart models produce inflated metrics or duplicate rows when joined against unvalidated dimension keys.
   - Repository evidence: Absence of unique and not_null test declarations under columns in model or source YAML schema files.
   - Severity tendency: error
3. Missing Dependency Declarations for Packages/Macros
   - Why it happens: Team members use third-party packages or custom macros without maintaining a versioned packages.yml file.
   - Early warning signs: CI/CD build pipelines fail with compilation errors stating a macro or function cannot be resolved.
   - Repository evidence: A project with files in macros/ or custom package function calls lacking a packages.yml file or missing package installation steps.
   - Severity tendency: warning
4. Silent Source Data Stagnation
   - Why it happens: Ingestion jobs crash or pause without throwing explicit database errors, leaving upstream source tables frozen in time while dbt models continue running successfully.
   - Early warning signs: Business stakeholders notice reporting dashboards displaying static daily figures.
   - Repository evidence: Source definition YAML files omitting loaded_at_field and freshness threshold configurations.
   - Severity tendency: warning

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated)
- Absence of `dbt_project.yml` at the repository root.
- Absence of `models/` directory when `dbt_project.yml` is present.
- Presence of hardcoded 3-part database table references (e.g., `FROM database.schema.table`) in model SQL files instead of `{{ ref() }}` or `{{ source() }}`.
- Absence of `packages.yml` when `macros/` contains custom macro definitions calling external package functions.
- Absence of `tests/` directory when `models/` directory exists.

### Needs-review candidates
- Verification of generic test configurations (`unique`, `not_null`) across all dimension key columns in schema YAML files.
- Verification of source freshness threshold settings (`loaded_at_field`, `warn_after`, `error_after`) in source YAML declarations.
- Verification that materialization strategies (`view`, `table`, `incremental`) in `dbt_project.yml` align with folder-level performance expectations.

### Human review (contextual & organizational)
- Reviewing whether dimensional model grain definitions match business requirement metrics.
- Evaluating whether surrogate key generation logic in `marts` models correctly handles null business keys.
- Assessing whether dbt semantic layer metric definitions accurately reflect organizational financial KPIs.

### Organizational artifacts check
- CODEOWNERS file asserting data engineering team ownership over `models/marts/core/`.
- Data product contract documentation or README files describing model SLAs and refresh schedules.

## 10. Candidate rules

- Rule ID: dataops-dbt-001
- Title: Hardcoded Relation Reference Detection
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Hardcoded database relation strings break multi-environment compilation and prevent dbt from constructing accurate lineage graphs.
- Source-backed basis: Models must reference upstream relations via ref or source functions to enable environment-agnostic compilation.
- Repository evidence to inspect: SQL files under models/ containing hardcoded database, schema, or table identifiers in FROM or JOIN clauses.
- Pass condition: All relation targets in SQL model queries are accessed exclusively via {{ ref(...) }} or {{ source(...) }}.
- Fail condition: A model SQL file contains a hardcoded three-part relation string like FROM `project.dataset.table`.
- Exceptions or limitations: None.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Chapter 2, Data Modeling for Analytics — Model referencing mechanism.
- Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
- Structural locator: Chapter 2, Data Modeling for Analytics
- Spirit locator: Referencing relations dynamically via ref Jinja constructs ensures environment flexibility and accurate lineage DAG generation.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-dbt-002
- Title: Missing dbt Project Configuration File
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: A repository containing dbt models without dbt_project.yml cannot be parsed or executed by dbt CLI or CI tools.
- Source-backed basis: dbt_project.yml is the required root file that identifies a directory as a dbt project and configures execution parameters.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Presence of dbt_project.yml at repository root.
- Pass condition: dbt_project.yml exists at the repository root.
- Fail condition: dbt_project.yml is absent from the repository root.
- Exceptions or limitations: Non-dbt repositories.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt — Structure of a dbt project.
- Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
- Structural locator: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt
- Spirit locator: The dbt_project.yml file serves as the core manifest defining project boundaries and configuration settings.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-dbt-003
- Title: Missing Models Directory Structure
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: A dbt project without a models directory lacks transformation logic and cannot execute data pipelines.
- Source-backed basis: The models folder is a mandatory directory containing all SQL transformation models.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Presence of models/ directory when dbt_project.yml exists.
- Pass condition: The models/ directory exists in the repository.
- Fail condition: The models/ directory is absent while dbt_project.yml is present.
- Exceptions or limitations: None.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt — Structure of a dbt project.
- Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
- Structural locator: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt
- Spirit locator: Models are the primary artifact where analytics engineers encapsulate transformation logic.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-dbt-004
- Title: Missing Package Manifest for Macros Directory
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Utilizing custom macros or third-party package macros without a packages.yml manifest causes compilation failures in CI/CD.
- Source-backed basis: Packages and macros provide shared logic and must be declared in packages.yml for dependency resolution.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Presence of packages.yml when macros/ folder is present and non-empty.
- Pass condition: packages.yml exists when custom macros or package functions are declared in macros/.
- Fail condition: macros/ directory is non-empty but packages.yml is missing from the repository root.
- Exceptions or limitations: Projects using purely native Jinja without external package dependencies.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Chapter 5, dbt Advanced Topics — dbt packages and macros.
- Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
- Structural locator: Chapter 5, dbt Advanced Topics
- Spirit locator: Explicit package dependency manifest files ensure reproducible macro compilation across environments.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-dbt-005
- Title: Unconfigured dbt Model Testing Schema
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Models deployed without generic tests (unique, not_null) risk passing duplicate or null primary key records to downstream marts.
- Source-backed basis: Testing is an essential component of analytics engineering to ensure accuracy and data reliability.
- Repository evidence to inspect: YAML schema files in models/ specifying test assertions under column definitions.
- Pass condition: Every model defined in schema YAML files includes unique and not_null test assertions on key columns.
- Fail condition: Model schema YAML files lack test declarations on primary key columns.
- Exceptions or limitations: Staging views on non-unique raw event logs.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Requires YAML-aware structural checker to inspect nested schema test declarations.
- Source citation: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt — Generic and singular tests.
- Source: Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt
- Structural locator: Chapter 4, Data Transformation with dbt
- Spirit locator: Automated assertion testing on primary keys prevents data quality degradation in production marts.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment
```yaml
rules:
  - id: "dataops-dbt-001"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "critical"
    description: "Detects hardcoded database.schema.table relation strings in dbt model SQL files."
    check:
      type: "pattern_match"
      file_glob: "models/**/*.sql"
      pattern: "(?i)(from|join)\s+[`'"]?[a-z0-9_]+\.[a-z0-9_]+\.[a-z0-9_]+[`'"]?"
      error_when: "pattern_present"
      message: "Hardcoded relation reference found in dbt model. Use {{ ref(...) }} or {{ source(...) }} instead."
    references:
      - book: "Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt"
        concept: "model-referencing"

  - id: "dataops-dbt-002"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "critical"
    description: "Ensures dbt_project.yml exists at the repository root."
    check:
      type: "file_exists"
      file_glob: "dbt_project.yml"
      error_when: "file_absent"
      message: "dbt_project.yml is missing from the repository root."
    references:
      - book: "Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt"
        concept: "dbt-project-structure"

  - id: "dataops-dbt-003"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "error"
    description: "Ensures models/ directory exists when dbt_project.yml is present."
    check:
      type: "file_exists"
      file_glob: "models/"
      error_when: "file_absent"
      message: "models/ directory is missing from the dbt repository."
    references:
      - book: "Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt"
        concept: "modular-data-modeling"

  - id: "dataops-dbt-004"
    profile: "dataops"
    severity: "warning"
    description: "Ensures packages.yml exists when macros directory is present."
    check:
      type: "file_exists"
      file_glob: "packages.yml"
      error_when: "file_absent"
      message: "packages.yml is missing from repository containing custom macros."
    references:
      - book: "Analytics Engineering with SQL and dbt"
        concept: "dbt-packages"
```

### Candidates not emitted
- `dataops-dbt-005` (Unconfigured dbt Model Testing Schema): Marked `needs-review` because validating nested column test declarations requires a YAML-aware structural parser beyond `pattern_match` or `file_exists`.

# Data Quality Fundamentals — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Data Quality Fundamentals
- Title: Data Quality Fundamentals: A Practitioner's Guide to Building Trustworthy Data Pipelines
- Author(s): Barr Moses, Lior Gavish, Molly Vorwerck
- Branch: dataops
- RepoScope profile: dataops
- Primary source file: Data Quality Fundamentals_ A Practitioner's Guide to Building Trustworthy Data Pipelines.pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - End-to-end data pipeline reliability, observability, and data downtime prevention across data warehouses, lakes, and orchestration layers.
  - The 5 pillars of data observability: Freshness, Volume, Schema, Distribution, and Lineage.
  - Operationalizing SRE principles for data: setting SLAs, SLIs, and SLOs for data assets.
  - Data quality testing assertions, automated anomaly detection, field-level lineage, and data incident management workflows.
- Out of scope:
  - Specific internal implementation details of commercial software platforms beyond standard open-source and architectural integrations.
  - Low-level database storage engine kernel development or hardware tuning.
  - Generic organizational management theories unrelated to version-controlled repository evidence or data product engineering.

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Central thesis: Modern analytical data pipelines require the same operational diligence, automated observability, and structured incident management frameworks as production software applications to eliminate data downtime and maintain trust.
2. Primary problem addressed: Unannounced and undetected "data downtime"—periods where data is missing, erroneous, stale, or structurally broken—resulting in broken downstream executive dashboards, faulty machine learning models, and eroded business confidence.
3. Architectural posture: Proactive, metadata-driven end-to-end observability that combines automated anomaly detection across storage assets with pre-commit and post-load quality assertion gates.
4. Core unit of abstraction: The observable data pipeline node (table, view, or stream) characterized by its freshness, volume metrics, schema contract, value distribution parameters, and field-level lineage graph.
5. Operating model: Continuous automated monitoring and alerting integrated with version-controlled quality tests, automated field-level lineage, and SRE-style incident response workflows.
6. Safety / failure philosophy: Fail-safe containment and early detection; data issues must be caught at ingestion or transformation via circuit breakers and automated alerts before bad data propagates downstream into consumer-facing artifacts.
7. Primary trade-off embraced: Investing engineering resources into automated metadata monitoring, test maintenance, and incident management overhead in exchange for dramatic reductions in Time to Detection (TTD), Time to Resolution (TTR), and data downtime.
8. Invariant / non-negotiable principle: Data trust must be objectively measured through quantifiable Service Level Indicators (SLIs) and Service Level Objectives (SLOs) rather than subjective stakeholder sentiment or anecdotal bug reports.
9. Anti-pattern targeted: Reactive "firefighting" driven by executive Slack messages, reliance on tribal knowledge for debugging, and treating data pipelines as one-off static scripts without observability or versioned quality gates.
10. Target repository profile / ecosystem: DataOps repositories containing dbt projects, data orchestration DAGs (Airflow, Dagster, Prefect), data validation configurations (Great Expectations, Soda, dbt tests), CI/CD pipelines, and versioned runbooks.

## 4. Source-backed structure
### Theme 1: The 5 Pillars of Data Observability
- Source: Data Quality Fundamentals
- Structural locator: Chapter 5: Architecting for Data Reliability -> Measuring and Maintaining Data Quality in the Pipeline
- Spirit locator: Defines the foundational architectural criteria required to evaluate data asset health continuously without extracting or compromising underlying payload data.

### Theme 2: SRE Principles Applied to Data (SLAs, SLOs, SLIs)
- Source: Data Quality Fundamentals
- Structural locator: Chapter 5: Architecting for Data Reliability -> How to Set SLAs, SLOs, and SLIs for Your Data
- Spirit locator: Establishes quantifiable standards for data downtime, incident tracking, and performance targets agreed upon between data producers and business consumers.

### Theme 3: Data Incident Management & The Data Reliability Lifecycle
- Source: Data Quality Fundamentals
- Structural locator: Chapter 6: Fixing Data Quality Issues at Scale -> Data Incident Management
- Spirit locator: Provides a structured, multi-phase feedback loop (Detect, Resolve, Prevent) for triaging pipeline breakages using lineage, code inspection, and blameless postmortems.

### Theme 4: Field-Level Lineage & Root Cause Analysis
- Source: Data Quality Fundamentals
- Structural locator: Chapter 7: Building End-to-End Lineage -> Building End-to-End Field-Level Lineage for Modern Data Systems
- Spirit locator: Maps upstream source dependencies and downstream blast radii down to individual table columns to enable rapid root cause analysis and impact communication.

### Theme 5: Data Quality Testing & Anomaly Detection
- Source: Data Quality Fundamentals
- Structural locator: Chapter 4: Monitoring and Anomaly Detection for Your Data Pipelines -> Knowing Your Known Unknowns and Unknown Unknowns
- Spirit locator: Differentiates between predictable static contract violations (tested via explicit assertions) and unpredictable statistical anomalies (monitored via dynamic ML detectors).

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. What problem is this source trying to solve?
It solves the pervasive issue of silent data failure and data downtime where advanced data stacks process incorrect, missing, or stale data without warning, wasting engineering hours and damaging business trust.

2. What is its core architectural posture?
A metadata-first, end-to-end data observability posture that integrates passive monitoring across storage systems with active testing at pipeline handoffs.

3. What does it consider a fatal mistake or anti-pattern?
Relying solely on manual stakeholder bug reports ("firefighting"), operating without field-level lineage, or treating data quality as a one-time project rather than an ongoing operational discipline.

4. What does it consider non-negotiable?
Establishing clear, version-controlled definitions for data quality tests, source freshness thresholds, and explicit ownership/SLA commitments for critical data products.

5. How does it balance trade-offs?
It balances absolute precision against false positive alert fatigue by combining deterministic threshold testing for known contract constraints with machine learning anomaly detection for unknown statistical variations.

6. How should an engineer demonstrate compliance in a repository?
By committing dbt/Soda/Great Expectations quality test files, configuring freshness SLA thresholds in YAML, maintaining automated lineage configurations, establishing version-controlled incident runbooks, and maintaining CODEOWNERS mapping for data assets.

## 6. Key concepts
### Concept 1: Data Downtime
- Definition: The total period during which data is missing, erroneous, stale, or otherwise inaccurate across the data lifecycle.
- Why it matters: Quantifies the total financial and operational impact of broken data on the business.
- Related concepts: Time to Detection (TTD), Time to Resolution (TTR), Data Reliability.
- Citation: Chapter 1 -> Understanding the Rise of Data Downtime.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 2: Freshness (Observability Pillar)
- Definition: The measure of how up-to-date a data asset is relative to expected arrival intervals.
- Why it matters: Prevents downstream analytics from consuming stale or delayed operational records.
- Related concepts: Source Freshness, SLA, `loaded_at_field`.
- Citation: Chapter 5 -> Measuring and Maintaining Data Quality in the Pipeline.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 3: Distribution & Volume (Observability Pillars)
- Definition: Statistical metrics measuring whether field data falls within expected value ranges, null-rate bounds, and expected row counts.
- Why it matters: Detects silent corruptions where pipeline execution succeeds but the payload contains invalid or truncated data.
- Related concepts: Anomaly Detection, Null-rate Spikes, Z-score.
- Citation: Chapter 4 -> Monitoring and Anomaly Detection for Your Data Pipelines.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 4: Field-Level Lineage
- Definition: A detailed graph mapping dependencies and transformations between specific fields/columns across upstream sources and downstream consumers.
- Why it matters: Enables instant root cause analysis upstream and precise blast radius calculation downstream during incidents.
- Related concepts: Root Cause Analysis, Dependency Mapping, Operational Analytics.
- Citation: Chapter 7 -> Building End-to-End Field-Level Lineage for Modern Data Systems.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 5: Data SLAs, SLOs, and SLIs
- Definition: Service Level Agreements (formal commitments), Objectives (target reliability thresholds), and Indicators (quantitative measurements) adapted from SRE to govern data pipeline health.
- Why it matters: Aligns technical data teams and business stakeholders on explicit expectations for data availability and freshness.
- Related concepts: TTD, TTR, Data Certification.
- Citation: Chapter 5 -> How to Set SLAs, SLOs, and SLIs for Your Data.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 6: Data Circuit Breaker
- Definition: Automated pipeline logic that halts downstream processing or DAG execution when upstream quality assertions fail.
- Why it matters: Prevents bad or corrupted data from contaminating downstream gold/marts tables and business dashboards.
- Related concepts: Quality Gate, Airflow Operator, Quality Screen.
- Citation: Chapter 3 -> Collecting, Cleaning, Transforming, and Testing Data.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
### Pattern 1: In-Pipeline Quality Gate (Circuit Breaker)
- Problem solved: Contamination of analytical marts when raw or staging data fails quality assertions.
- Recommended approach: Execute deterministic test assertions (e.g. unique, non-null, referential integrity) prior to materializing downstream transformations.
- Repository-level manifestation: dbt test step in CI/CD pipeline or orchestration DAG blocking step (`airflow.operators` executing data tests before model build).
- Limitations: Adds execution latency to pipeline runs and requires maintaining explicit test definitions.
- Source citation: Chapter 3 -> Collecting, Cleaning, Transforming, and Testing Data.

### Pattern 2: Source Freshness Threshold SLA Declaration
- Problem solved: Silent ingestion halts where source database syncs freeze without raising pipeline execution exceptions.
- Recommended approach: Explicitly declare `loaded_at_field` and `freshness` warning/error time periods inside version-controlled dbt source YAML configurations.
- Repository-level manifestation: `warn_after` and `error_after` blocks inside `_sources.yml` or `schema.yml` files.
- Limitations: Requires an accessible timestamp column on source tables.
- Source citation: Chapter 4 -> Monitoring and Anomaly Detection for Your Data Pipelines -> Monitoring for Freshness.

### Pattern 3: Version-Controlled Data Incident Runbooks
- Problem solved: Ad-hoc, frantic debugging dependent on single engineers during high-severity data outages.
- Recommended approach: Document standardized incident response steps, escalation paths, and communication templates directly within the code repository.
- Repository-level manifestation: `RUNBOOK.md` or `docs/runbooks/` files referenced in pipeline repository structures.
- Limitations: Requires periodic human reviews to ensure runbook steps reflect evolving architecture.
- Source citation: Chapter 6 -> Fixing Data Quality Issues at Scale -> Data Incident Management.

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
### Failure Mode 1: Silent Upstream Schema Drift
- Why it happens: Source database migrations add, rename, or drop columns without notifying downstream data engineering teams.
- Early warning signs: Unexplained NULL spikes in downstream marts or query failures in strict SELECT expressions.
- Repository evidence: Missing schema validation tests, raw `SELECT *` usages without explicit column contracts or schema drift assertions.
- Severity tendency: critical

### Failure Mode 2: Pipeline Ingestion Freeze (Freshness Outage)
- Why it happens: Upstream extract jobs or third-party API webhooks fail silently while orchestrators continue reporting success.
- Early warning signs: Stale timestamp values across core transactional tables; zero new rows loaded in daily ingestion windows.
- Repository evidence: Sources configured without `loaded_at_field` declarations or missing freshness SLA checks in orchestration configurations.
- Severity tendency: error

### Failure Mode 3: Alert Fatigue & Noise Overload
- Why it happens: Excessive un-tuned alerting on minor distribution shifts without precision filtering or severity classification.
- Early warning signs: Data engineers ignoring Slack alert channels or silencing automated monitoring notifications.
- Repository evidence: Blanket threshold checks applied without anomaly tuning, missing severity tagging on quality assertions.
- Severity tendency: warning

### Failure Mode 4: Tribal Knowledge Dependence in Root Cause Analysis
- Why it happens: Lack of version-controlled documentation, missing lineage mapping, and absence of standardized triage procedures.
- Early warning signs: High Time to Resolution (TTR) when primary data engineers are unavailable; repeated manual SQL debugging.
- Repository evidence: Absence of `CODEOWNERS` files, missing field-level lineage configurations, and missing repository runbooks.
- Severity tendency: error

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated)
- Absence of data validation frameworks or quality test configurations (`models/**/_*.yml`, `great_expectations/`, `soda/`).
- Absence of version-controlled incident runbooks or escalation documentation (`RUNBOOK.md` or `docs/runbooks/*.md`).
- Hardcoded non-parameterized connection strings or static schema references in model SQL.

### Needs-review candidates
- Missing source freshness SLA configurations (`loaded_at_field`, `freshness` thresholds in dbt source YAML).
- Missing unique and non-null column assertions on primary keys in dbt model schema YAMLs.
- Missing CODEOWNERS or metadata ownership declarations for critical data products.

### Human review (contextual & organizational)
- Assessing whether chosen SLO target percentages (e.g. 99.9% vs 95%) align with business stakeholder tolerance.
- Evaluating the qualitative effectiveness of blameless postmortems and team communication channels.
- Reviewing whether dimensional model grain choices match business decision-making requirements.

### Organizational artifacts check
- Verifying presence of `CODEOWNERS`, `OWNERS`, `data_product.yml`, or ownership metadata tags within repo configuration files.

## 10. Candidate rules
- Rule ID: dataops-quality-001
- Title: Require Data Quality Testing Configuration
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Repositories lacking automated data quality test configurations risk silent data corruption in production pipelines.
- Source-backed basis: Data Quality Fundamentals emphasizes that data pipelines must incorporate automated quality checks and circuit breakers at every transformation stage.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Look for presence of `models/**/_*.yml`, `great_expectations.yml`, `soda.yml`, or `tests/` directories.
- Pass condition: Repository contains at least one data quality test file or directory configuration.
- Fail condition: No dbt YAML test files, Great Expectations configs, or Soda YAML files are found.
- Exceptions or limitations: Applies only to repositories containing analytical or data transformation logic.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation:
  - Source: Data Quality Fundamentals
  - Structural locator: Chapter 3: Collecting, Cleaning, Transforming, and Testing Data
  - Spirit locator: Automated quality testing assertions must be version-controlled alongside transformation code to prevent bad data propagation.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-runbook-001
- Title: Require Version-Controlled Incident Runbook
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: info
- Rationale: Standardized incident response documentation reduces Time to Resolution (TTR) and eliminates dependence on tribal knowledge during data outages.
- Source-backed basis: Data Quality Fundamentals states that data incident management requires documented response protocols, escalation paths, and blameless postmortems.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Look for presence of `RUNBOOK.md`, `docs/RUNBOOK.md`, or files under `docs/runbooks/`.
- Pass condition: An incident runbook markdown file exists in the repository.
- Fail condition: No runbook markdown file is present.
- Exceptions or limitations: None.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation:
  - Source: Data Quality Fundamentals
  - Structural locator: Chapter 6: Fixing Data Quality Issues at Scale -> Data Incident Management
  - Spirit locator: Structured incident management requires version-controlled runbooks to guide detection, response, and postmortem workflows.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-freshness-001
- Title: Require Source Freshness SLA Declaration
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Source declarations without freshness tracking cannot detect silent ingestion freezes before bad data reaches downstream consumers.
- Source-backed basis: Data Quality Fundamentals highlights Freshness as a core pillar of data observability, requiring explicit `loaded_at_field` monitoring.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Inspect dbt source YAML files (`*_sources.yml` or `schema.yml`) for `loaded_at_field` and `freshness` blocks.
- Pass condition: Source definitions include `loaded_at_field` and `freshness` SLA thresholds.
- Fail condition: Source definitions omit `loaded_at_field` or `freshness` declarations.
- Exceptions or limitations: Requires nested YAML-aware structural parsing.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Requires YAML-aware structural checker to inspect nested properties inside source YAML definitions.
- Source citation:
  - Source: Data Quality Fundamentals
  - Structural locator: Chapter 4: Monitoring and Anomaly Detection for Your Data Pipelines -> Monitoring for Freshness
  - Spirit locator: Source data freshness must be explicitly tracked using timestamp fields and warning/error SLA thresholds.
- Verification status: source-backed
