===== FILE: docs/notes/dataops/designing-data-intensive-applications.md =====
# Designing Data-Intensive Applications — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Designing Data-Intensive Applications
- Title: Designing Data-Intensive Applications: The Big Ideas Behind Reliable, Scalable, and Maintainable Systems
- Author(s): Martin Kleppmann
- Branch: dataops
- RepoScope profile: dataops
- Primary source file: Kleppmann, Martin.pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - System architecture trade-offs across storage engines (LSM-trees, B-trees), encoding formats (Avro, Protobuf, JSON), and query models (OLTP vs OLAP).
  - Distributed data system invariants including replication (single-leader, multi-leader, leaderless), partition rebalancing, linearizability, and consensus algorithms.
  - Unifying batch and event stream processing via immutable event logs, Event Sourcing, Change Data Capture (CDC), and Command Query Responsibility Segregation (CQRS).
  - Derived data architectures, end-to-end correctness, auditable state generation, and schema evolution rules.
- Out of scope:
  - High-level business organization hierarchy design without technical system evidence.
  - GUI frontend styling or human-computer interaction user interface guidelines.
  - Manual database administrator operational runbooks unrepresented in automation or repository configs.

## 3. Source spirit card
- Central thesis: Data-intensive applications must be built by combining specialized storage, processing, and streaming building blocks using immutable event logs and verifiable abstractions to achieve reliability, scalability, and maintainability.
- Operating model: State changes are captured as an immutable, append-only stream of facts from a canonical system of record, from which downstream read-optimized views and indexes are derived asynchronously.
- Primary architectural posture: Decoupled, event-driven dataflow systems where state computation is deterministic, verifiable, and resilient to hardware or network faults.
- Primary risk focus: Silent data corruption, unhandled network partitions, race conditions under weak isolation levels, and breaking schema changes across service boundaries.
- Primary audit objective: Verifying that data schemas, serializations, replication settings, and stateful transformation logic are strictly declared, versioned, and tested.
- Primary evidence boundary: Schema definition files, serialization IDLs, container orchestration configs, database migration scripts, and consumer idempotency logic.
- Core design philosophy: Embracing immutability, explicit schema evolution, and end-to-end correctness over blind trust in individual infrastructure components.
- Key technical abstractions: Immutable event log, System of Record, Derived Data System, LSM-tree / SSTable, Change Data Capture, and Write-Ahead Log (WAL).
- Primary trade-offs/tensions: Balancing strong consistency and linearizability against fault tolerance, lower latency, and high availability in distributed networks.
- Canonical failure mode: Modifying state in-place without write-ahead logging or versioning, leading to unrecoverable data drift and inability to audit or rebuild system state.

## 4. Source-backed structure
- Theme 1: Foundations of Data Systems: Reliability, Scalability, Maintainability
  - Source: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications
  - Structural locator: Chapter 1: Reliable, Scalable, and Maintainable Applications
  - Spirit locator: Engineering data systems to withstand hardware faults, load spikes, and software evolution through fault tolerance, abstraction, and evolvability.
- Theme 2: Storage Engines and Data Models
  - Source: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications
  - Structural locator: Chapter 3: Storage and Retrieval
  - Spirit locator: Selecting storage formats (LSM-trees vs B-trees, row vs column storage) based on read/write workload characteristics and access patterns.
- Theme 3: Encoding, Schema Evolution, and Distributed Replication
  - Source: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications
  - Structural locator: Chapter 5: Encoding and Evolution
  - Spirit locator: Maintaining backward and forward compatibility across service boundaries using explicit schema definitions and versioned binary encodings.
- Theme 4: Distributed Consistency, Consensus, and Correctness
  - Source: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications
  - Structural locator: Chapter 9: Consistency and Consensus
  - Spirit locator: Navigating consensus, linearizability, and atomic commit protocols to prevent data loss and split-brain states in distributed stores.
- Theme 5: Stream Processing, Event Sourcing, and Derived Data Systems
  - Source: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications
  - Structural locator: Chapter 11: Stream Processing
  - Spirit locator: Unifying data landscapes by treating log-based event streams as the immutable source of truth for deriving state and read views.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
- Q1: How does this source define data system architecture?
  - Answer: The source defines data system architecture as the composition of heterogeneous storage, index, and processing modules bound together by application code and messaging layers to meet functional and non-functional requirements.
- Q2: What primary architectural invariants or principles govern pipeline execution?
  - Answer: Raw inputs must be captured immutably, processing steps must be deterministic and repeatable, and schemas must explicitly manage evolution without breaking existing consumers.
- Q3: How should data quality and trustworthiness be verified across lifecycle transitions?
  - Answer: Trust is established by performing end-to-end integrity checks, validating schemas at boundary entry points, and auditing transformations against append-only event streams.
- Q4: What is the source's approach to failure handling, observability, and incident recovery?
  - Answer: Systems must assume network delays and node crashes, employing crash-recovery logs, idempotent consumer execution, self-auditing integrity checks, and deterministic simulation.
- Q5: How are operational governance, metadata, and data ownership enforced?
  - Answer: Ownership is mapped by designating canonical Systems of Record for authoritative data writes and deriving downstream read models via explicit Change Data Capture pipelines.
- Q6: What repository evidence or software artifacts prove adherence to the source's philosophy?
  - Answer: Presence of explicit schema IDLs (Avro, Protobuf), version-controlled migration scripts, container orchestration configs, and idempotent stream consumer code.

## 6. Key concepts
- Concept 1: System of Record vs. Derived Data System
  - Definition: A System of Record holds the canonical, authoritative state written first; Derived Data Systems are read-optimized representations computed asynchronously from the System of Record.
  - Why it matters: Prevents dual-writing inconsistencies and enables rebuilding read views from raw immutable history.
  - Related concepts: Event Sourcing, CQRS, Change Data Capture (CDC).
  - Citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 1: Trade-Offs in Data Systems Architecture, Spirit: Authoritative data lives in systems of record; derived data is regenerated from events.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 2: Immutable Event Log
  - Definition: An append-only sequence of timestamped records representing immutable historical facts.
  - Why it matters: Serves as the ultimate source of truth, enabling time-travel debugging, auditability, and replayability.
  - Related concepts: Write-Ahead Log (WAL), Event Sourcing, Stream-Table Duality.
  - Citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 11: Stream Processing, Spirit: Append-only logs capture facts permanently and drive state derivations.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 3: Schema Evolution (Forward and Backward Compatibility)
  - Definition: Designing data encodings so new code can read data written by old code (backward compatibility) and old code can read data written by new code (forward compatibility).
  - Why it matters: Enables rolling deployments and decoupled service upgrades without system-wide downtime.
  - Related concepts: Schema Registry, Avro, Protocol Buffers, Thrift.
  - Citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 5: Encoding and Evolution, Spirit: Explicit schemas with evolution rules prevent breaking serialization changes.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 4: Idempotence
  - Definition: An operational property where executing a processing step multiple times produces the exact same system state as executing it once.
  - Why it matters: Guarantees exact-once semantics and fault recovery in distributed stream and batch processing pipelines.
  - Related concepts: At-Least-Once Processing, Deduplication, Deterministic Execution.
  - Citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 11: Stream Processing, Spirit: Idempotent operations allow safe retries during network or node failures.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 5: Stream-Table Duality
  - Definition: The fundamental relationship where a stream of events represents the changelog of a table, and a table represents the current aggregated state of a stream.
  - Why it matters: Provides the theoretical foundation for materializing stateful views from continuous real-time streams.
  - Related concepts: Change Data Capture (CDC), Materialized View, State Management.
  - Citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 11: Stream Processing, Spirit: Streams are tables in motion; tables are streams at rest.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 6: LSM-Tree vs. B-Tree Storage
  - Definition: Storage engine designs optimized either for write-heavy workloads via sequential append-only SSTables (LSM-Tree) or read-heavy random access via in-place page updates (B-Tree).
  - Why it matters: Dictates database I/O performance, write amplification, and hardware wear characteristics.
  - Related concepts: Write-Ahead Log (WAL), SSTable, Memtable, Compaction.
  - Citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 3: Storage and Retrieval, Spirit: Storage structures must align with write vs. read access patterns.
  - Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
- Pattern 1: Schema-First API and Stream Interfaces
  - Problem solved: Prevents runtime serialization errors and breaking data type mismatches across distributed microservices.
  - Recommended approach: Define interface payloads using explicit IDL schemas (`.proto`, `.avsc`) stored in repository code and validated during build time.
  - Repository-level manifestation: Schema files inside `schemas/`, `proto/`, or `avro/` directories.
  - Limitations: Requires schema registry deployment and discipline in managing field default values.
  - Source citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 5: Encoding and Evolution, Spirit: IDL schema specifications guarantee interface safety across releases.
- Pattern 2: Log-Based Change Data Capture (CDC)
  - Problem solved: Synchronizes search indexes, caches, and analytical warehouses without risky dual-writing in application code.
  - Recommended approach: Capture raw database transaction log mutations and stream them into message brokers for asynchronous consumption.
  - Repository-level manifestation: CDC connector configs (e.g., Debezium YAML files) and stream consumer handlers.
  - Limitations: Requires database transaction log retention and ordering guarantees.
  - Source citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 11: Stream Processing, Spirit: CDC extracts state changes directly from transaction logs cleanly.
- Pattern 3: Idempotent Consumer with Unique Message Keys
  - Problem solved: Eliminates duplicate record creation when network retries replay event messages.
  - Recommended approach: Assign deterministic unique IDs to incoming events and use atomic database conditional inserts or deduplication keys.
  - Repository-level manifestation: SQL statements utilizing `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` or unique key constraints in stream consumer code.
  - Limitations: Requires primary key index overhead on destination storage tables.
  - Source citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 11: Stream Processing, Spirit: Deterministic keying guarantees idempotent record updates.

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
- Failure 1: Dual Writing Across Storage Systems
  - Why it happens: Developers write code that updates two databases sequentially in an application layer without distributed transactions or event logs.
  - Early warning signs: Data inconsistency and drift between relational primary databases and search/cache indexes.
  - Repository evidence: Application handlers invoking sequential `db.save()` and `cache.put()` calls without transaction wrappers or CDC.
  - Severity tendency: error
- Failure 2: Unversioned In-Place Schema Modifications
  - Why it happens: Fields are deleted or renamed in data structures without managing backward/forward compatibility.
  - Early warning signs: Serialization crashes in old consumer services when parsing new payload messages.
  - Repository evidence: Removing fields from `.proto` or `.avsc` files without assigning deprecated tag numbers or default values.
  - Severity tendency: critical
- Failure 3: Plaintext Unencrypted Connection Strings in Stream Configs
  - Why it happens: Database or message broker URIs containing plaintext credentials are hardcoded into deployment scripts.
  - Early warning signs: Exposed credentials in repository search results or git logs.
  - Repository evidence: Connection strings containing `amqp://user:pass@host` or `postgres://user:pass@host` in config files.
  - Severity tendency: critical
- Failure 4: Non-Idempotent Stream Processing Handlers
  - Why it happens: Consumers perform side-effects (e.g., sending emails, incrementing counters) without checking if an event was already processed.
  - Early warning signs: Duplicate records or inflated aggregation totals after network restarts or pod rebalances.
  - Repository evidence: Consumer execution loops lacking state deduplication or atomic unique key assertions.
  - Severity tendency: error

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated)
- Detecting hardcoded plaintext database connection strings or unencrypted connection URIs in configuration files.
- Verifying the presence of explicit schema specification files (`.proto`, `.avsc`, `.thrift`, `.json`) in event-driven streaming repositories.
- Checking for the existence of database migration tooling configuration (e.g., Flyway, Liquibase, Alembic) in relational database projects.
- Ensuring that environment credential configurations are excluded via `.gitignore`.
### Needs-review candidates
- Checking whether schema evolution settings in Schema Registry configurations enforce `BACKWARD` or `FULL` compatibility modes.
- Inspecting stream consumer code for idempotent retry and deduplication mechanisms.
### Human review (contextual & organizational)
- Evaluating whether system boundaries correctly isolate Systems of Record from Derived Data Systems.
- Reviewing distributed system partition tolerance and consistency trade-off decisions under fault scenarios.
### Organizational artifacts check
- Verifying code ownership and repository domain boundary assignments via `CODEOWNERS` files.

## 10. Candidate rules

- Rule ID: dataops-kleppmann-001
  Title: Hardcoded unencrypted connection URIs in configuration files
  Profile: dataops
  Severity tendency: critical
  Rationale: Hardcoding connection strings with embedded plaintext passwords in code or config files violates basic security and secret separation principles.
  Source-backed basis: Source highlights the necessity of secure credential handling and environment decoupling in data systems.
  Repository evidence to inspect: `.py`, `.yaml`, `.json`, `.properties`, or `.env.example` files.
  Pass condition: Connection strings utilize environment variable substitution rather than embedded credentials.
  Fail condition: Connection strings contain hardcoded `user:pass@host` credentials.
  Exceptions or limitations: Test configuration files using local dummy credentials.
  YAML readiness: ready
  Source citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 5: Encoding and Evolution, Spirit: Environment parameters must be decoupled from code.
  Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-kleppmann-002
  Title: Missing schema definition files in event-driven streaming repositories
  Profile: dataops
  Severity tendency: error
  Rationale: Operating event-driven messaging without explicit schema definition files leads to untyped payloads, silent data corruption, and breaking changes.
  Source-backed basis: Source emphasizes schema-first design and IDLs as essential for data encoding and evolution.
  Repository evidence to inspect: Presence of `.proto`, `.avsc`, `.thrift`, or `.json` schema files in repositories containing stream consumer/producer code.
  Pass condition: Repository contains at least one formal schema IDL file.
  Fail condition: Stream producers/consumers exist without any formal schema definition files.
  Exceptions or limitations: Pure analytical SQL query repositories without custom event serializers.
  YAML readiness: ready
  Source citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 5: Encoding and Evolution, Spirit: IDL schema specifications guarantee interface safety across releases.
  Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-kleppmann-003
  Title: Missing database migration tool configuration
  Profile: dataops
  Severity tendency: warning
  Rationale: Modifying database schemas manually without version-controlled migration scripts causes environment schema drift and deployment failures.
  Source-backed basis: Source mandates explicit versioning and evolvability for database storage schemas.
  Repository evidence to inspect: Directories for migration tools (`migrations/`, `db/migrate/`, `flyway/`, `alembic/`).
  Pass condition: Repository includes a recognized database migration directory and configuration.
  Fail condition: Relational database application repository lacks database migration scripts.
  Exceptions or limitations: NoSQL schema-on-read database repositories.
  YAML readiness: ready
  Source citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 5: Encoding and Evolution, Spirit: Schema evolution must be systematically versioned and executed.
  Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-kleppmann-004
  Title: Unverified Schema Registry compatibility mode
  Profile: dataops
  Severity tendency: warning
  Rationale: Disabling compatibility checks in schema registries allows breaking schema changes to be published, crashing downstream event consumers.
  Source-backed basis: Source identifies backward and forward compatibility as mandatory for independent service evolvability.
  Repository evidence to inspect: Schema registry configuration files or deployment manifests.
  Pass condition: Compatibility mode is explicitly set to `BACKWARD`, `FORWARD`, or `FULL`.
  Fail condition: Compatibility mode is set to `NONE` or omitted.
  Exceptions or limitations: Local development test registries.
  YAML readiness: needs-review
  Reason if not ready: Requires YAML-aware or API-level structural configuration checking.
  Source citation: Martin Kleppmann - Designing Data-Intensive Applications, Chapter 5: Encoding and Evolution, Spirit: Compatibility enforcement prevents serialization breakage.
  Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment
```yaml
rules:
  - id: dataops-kleppmann-001
    title: Hardcoded unencrypted connection URIs in configuration files
    severity: critical
    references:
      - book: "Designing Data-Intensive Applications"
        concept: "secret-separation"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{py,yaml,yml,json,properties,ini}"
      pattern: "(?i)(postgres|mysql|amqp|mongodb|redis)://[^:]+:[^@]+@"
      error_when: pattern_present
      message: "Hardcoded connection string containing plaintext password detected in configuration file."

  - id: dataops-kleppmann-002
    title: Missing schema definition files in event-driven streaming repositories
    severity: error
    references:
      - book: "Designing Data-Intensive Applications"
        concept: "schema-evolution"
    check:
      type: file_absent
      file_glob: "**/*.{proto,avsc,thrift}"
      error_when: file_absent
      message: "Explicit schema definition files (.proto, .avsc, .thrift) are missing from event-driven project."

  - id: dataops-kleppmann-003
    title: Missing database migration tool configuration
    severity: warning
    references:
      - book: "Designing Data-Intensive Applications"
        concept: "schema-evolution"
    check:
      type: file_absent
      file_glob: "{migrations,db/migrate,flyway,alembic,sql}/*"
      error_when: file_absent
      message: "Database migration directory is missing, risking unversioned database schema drift."
```

### Candidates not emitted
- `dataops-kleppmann-004`: Requires YAML-aware structural configuration inspection to verify Schema Registry compatibility parameters (YAML readiness: needs-review).

## 12. Handoff checklist
- [x] All emitted rule IDs are unique.
- [x] All emitted severities match Section 10.
- [x] All emitted references use {book|standard, concept}.
- [x] All emitted check types are in {pattern_match, file_exists, file_absent}.
- [x] All "ready" rules appear in Section 11 YAML.
- [x] All "needs-review" / "blocked" rules are absent from Section 11 YAML.
- [x] Section 11 ends with "### Candidates not emitted".
- [x] No page numbers anywhere in the note.
- [x] No verbatim quotes longer than 15 words.
- [x] Every supplied rule ID is either used with a source-backed meaning or explicitly marked "Meaning to be confirmed."
- [x] "Note status" reflects the weakest readiness in Section 10.
- [x] Section 9 does not smuggle non-static advice into the automated list.
- [x] Section 3 has all 10 fields filled.
- [x] Section 10 has at least 3 rules (or explicit justification).

# Practical DataOps — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Practical DataOps
- Title: Practical DataOps: Delivering Agile Data Science At Scale
- Author(s): Harvinder Atwal
- Branch: dataops
- RepoScope profile: dataops
- Primary source file: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps_ Delivering Agile Data Science At Scale (2020, Apress).pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Application of Lean manufacturing, Agile (Scrum, Kanban, XP), and DevOps practices to data engineering and data science lifecycles.
  - Automated continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) testing pipelines for data validation, code quality, and deployment safety.
  - Operational and business metadata management, lineage tracking, and establishing trust in enterprise data assets.
  - Reproducible pipeline execution through version control, configuration management, containerization, and test data management.
- Out of scope:
  - Manual project management methodologies without repository or software execution evidence.
  - Vendor-specific commercial software purchasing, negotiation, or licensing models.
  - Hardcoded relational database schema designs unlinked to automated pipeline code or version control repository artifacts.

## 3. Source spirit card
- Central thesis: Traditional IT and operational paradigms fail when applied to twenty-first-century data science, requiring DataOps to combine Lean manufacturing, Agile collaboration, and DevOps automation to deliver scalable, reliable data products.
- Operating model: Cross-functional domain teams operate iteratively using automated pipelines, continuous feedback loops, and version-controlled infrastructure to treat data as a first-class production asset.
- Primary architectural posture: Immutable, version-controlled pipelines that separate code from environment configurations and mandate continuous automated validation at every lifecycle transition.
- Primary risk focus: Excessive data downtime, unvalidated production changes, tribal knowledge silos, and poor return on investment stemming from manual, unverified deployment practices.
- Primary audit objective: Confirming that data pipelines, environment dependencies, infrastructure configurations, and test suites are fully declared as code within version control.
- Primary evidence boundary: Repository configuration files, orchestration DAGs, pipeline build scripts, environment lockfiles, test suites, and schema validation manifests.
- Core design philosophy: Eliminating waste and cycle time in data production through reproducible workflows, automated quality gates, and pervasive feedback mechanisms.
- Key technical abstractions: Data product, value stream map, pipeline orchestration DAG, versioned configuration item, and operational metadata store.
- Primary trade-offs/tensions: Balancing rapid exploratory data science experimentation with rigid, production-grade automated deployment controls and governance safeguards.
- Canonical failure mode: Deploying unvalidated pipeline code or manual schema alterations directly into production environments, triggering downstream data corruption and loss of stakeholder trust.

## 4. Source-backed structure
- Theme 1: Agile and Lean Frameworks for Data Delivery
  - Source: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps
  - Structural locator: Chapter 4: Agile Collaboration
  - Spirit locator: Adapting Agile user stories, Kanban WIP limits, and value stream mapping to eliminate bottleneck waste and accelerate iterative data pipeline throughput.
- Theme 2: DevOps Practices for Data Pipelines
  - Source: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps
  - Structural locator: Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps
  - Spirit locator: Applying version control, configuration management, automated deployment, and environment reproducibility to eliminate production release failures.
- Theme 3: Building Trust Through Metadata and Lineage
  - Source: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps
  - Structural locator: Chapter 6: Building Building Trust
  - Spirit locator: Combining business and operational metadata to make data lineage traceable, access controllable, and quality continuously verifiable.
- Theme 4: Architecture and Tool Selection Principles
  - Source: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps
  - Structural locator: Chapter 8: Tools and Architecture
  - Spirit locator: Selecting technologies grounded in open standards, reusability, and automated testing rather than proprietary lock-in or unverified hype.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
- Q1: How does this source define data system architecture?
  - Answer: The source defines data system architecture as an integrated assembly of code, infrastructure, and operational workflows designed to automate the entire data lifecycle from ingestion to consumption while enforcing quality and reproducibility.
- Q2: What primary architectural invariants or principles govern pipeline execution?
  - Answer: Every pipeline asset must be fully reproducible from version control, configuration items must be decoupled from environment secrets, and changes must pass automated testing prior to production promotion.
- Q3: How should data quality and trustworthiness be verified across lifecycle transitions?
  - Answer: Trust is built by embedding automated data validation gates (file integrity, volume checks, schema assertions) into the continuous delivery pipeline and capturing operational metadata for full lineage traceability.
- Q4: What is the source's approach to failure handling, observability, and incident recovery?
  - Answer: The source advocates statistical process control, automated alerting on anomaly detection, blameless retrospectives, and rapid rollback mechanisms enabled by version-controlled pipeline artifacts.
- Q5: How are operational governance, metadata, and data ownership enforced?
  - Answer: Governance is decentralized into product teams using centralized metadata repositories and explicit ownership declarations to manage access permissions, data glossaries, and compliance rules.
- Q6: What repository evidence or software artifacts prove adherence to the source's philosophy?
  - Answer: Presence of version-controlled pipeline code, automated test directories, explicit dependency lockfiles, container build specifications, and environment configuration templates.

## 6. Key concepts
- Concept 1: DataOps Pipeline Reproducibility
  - Definition: The capability to recreate identical execution environments, data transformations, and system states using version-controlled artifacts.
  - Why it matters: Ensures that pipeline bugs or data anomalies can be reliably diagnosed, debugged, and corrected across development, staging, and production.
  - Related concepts: Configuration Management, Environment Parity, Infrastructure as Code.
  - Citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps, Spirit: Complete configuration items committed to version control guarantee reproducible systems.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 2: Operational Metadata
  - Definition: System-generated information capturing data lineage, transformation runtime metrics, ingestion timestamps, and quality validation logs.
  - Why it matters: Provides visibility into dataset provenance and execution health, eliminating tribal knowledge dependencies.
  - Related concepts: Data Lineage, Business Metadata, Data Catalog.
  - Citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 6: Building Trust, Spirit: Operational metadata encapsulates provenance and data processing results.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 3: Value Stream Mapping
  - Definition: A Lean technique used to analyze and map the sequence of processes and lead times required to bring a data asset from raw capture to consumption.
  - Why it matters: Exposes handoff delays, processing bottlenecks, and unnecessary manual interventions across data teams.
  - Related concepts: Kanban, Work in Progress (WIP) Limits, Lead Time.
  - Citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 3: Lean DataOps, Spirit: Mapping value streams identifies waste and accelerates throughput.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 4: Test-Driven Data Development
  - Definition: The practice of authoring automated data assertions and unit/integration tests prior to or alongside pipeline implementation.
  - Why it matters: Prevents silent data corruption from propagating downstream into analytical reports and machine learning models.
  - Related concepts: Continuous Integration, Data Quality Assertion, Test Data Management.
  - Citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps, Spirit: Automated testing guarantees pipeline safety and rapid feedback loops.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 5: Configuration Management
  - Definition: Systematically tracking, controlling, and versioning all configuration items necessary to execute software and data pipelines.
  - Why it matters: Prevents environment drift, unapproved hotfixes, and deployment crashes caused by undocumented infrastructure changes.
  - Related concepts: Version Control, Artifact Repository, Immutable Infrastructure.
  - Citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps, Spirit: Versioning all configuration items maintains total system integrity.
  - Verification status: source-backed
- Concept 6: Data Product Orientation
  - Definition: Structuring data deliverables as long-lived products managed by cross-functional teams rather than one-off project initiatives.
  - Why it matters: Promotes continuous operational maintenance, documentation quality, and customer-aligned feature enhancements.
  - Related concepts: Domain Orientation, Cross-Functional Teams, Product Owner.
  - Citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 8: Tools and Architecture, Spirit: Transitioning from project mindsets to product ownership drives long-term asset value.
  - Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
- Pattern 1: Continuous Integration Pipeline for Data Code
  - Problem solved: Eliminates manual deployment errors and untested code merges in production data environments.
  - Recommended approach: Implement automated CI triggers on git push to run linter checks, unit tests, SQL syntax validation, and dummy dry runs before merging.
  - Repository-level manifestation: CI workflow files (`.github/workflows/*.yml` or `.gitlab-ci.yml`) executing test runners (`pytest`, `dbt test`).
  - Limitations: Requires maintainable mock datasets and well-isolated test execution environments.
  - Source citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps, Spirit: CI checks enforce quality standards on every commit.
- Pattern 2: Secret Separation and Environment Configuration
  - Problem solved: Prevents plaintext credentials, database passwords, and API keys from leaking into public or shared version control repositories.
  - Recommended approach: Store credentials in secure secret vaults or environment variables, referencing template files (`.env.example`) in version control.
  - Repository-level manifestation: Presence of `.env.example`, `.gitignore` entries for `.env`, and secret fetching code (`os.getenv()`).
  - Limitations: Requires secure credential management infrastructure outside the repository.
  - Source citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps, Spirit: Credentials must never appear in version control code.
- Pattern 3: Automated Test Data Management (TDM)
  - Problem solved: Prevents developers from using sensitive production data or non-deterministic datasets during testing.
  - Recommended approach: Version control sanitized seed datasets or synthetic data generation scripts to supply reliable test fixtures.
  - Repository-level manifestation: Dedicated test fixture directories (`tests/fixtures/`, `seeds/`) containing anonymized CSV/JSON files.
  - Limitations: Synthetic fixtures may fail to replicate edge-case data anomalies found in live production feeds.
  - Source citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps, Spirit: Test data management provides safe, reproducible testing inputs.

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
- Failure 1: Plaintext Credentials Committed to Version Control
  - Why it happens: Developers hardcode API tokens or database passwords into scripts for quick testing and accidentally commit them.
  - Early warning signs: Plaintext passwords visible in code reviews or commit history logs.
  - Repository evidence: String patterns like `password = "..."` or `api_key = "..."` inside Python, SQL, or YAML scripts.
  - Severity tendency: critical
- Failure 2: Unverified Pipeline Code Promoted Without Automated Tests
  - Why it happens: Teams rush feature deliveries and bypass creating automated unit or integration tests for new pipeline modules.
  - Early warning signs: Frequent production data outages following pull request merges; complete lack of a `tests/` directory.
  - Repository evidence: Missing test files or CI workflow definitions that contain no invocation of test frameworks.
  - Severity tendency: error
- Failure 3: Dependency Version Drift Across Execution Environments
  - Why it happens: Package dependencies are specified without exact version pins, causing different environments to pull incompatible package versions.
  - Early warning signs: Pipeline scripts execute successfully in local development but fail in production deployment.
  - Repository evidence: Unpinned dependency manifests (`requirements.txt` without `==` versions) or missing lockfiles (`Pipfile.lock`, `poetry.lock`).
  - Severity tendency: warning
- Failure 4: Monolithic Unversioned Pipeline Orchestration
  - Why it happens: Orchestration DAGs are written as massive, unstructured monolithic scripts with hardcoded system paths.
  - Early warning signs: Inability to run modular sub-sections of a pipeline; failure logs that do not isolate broken tasks.
  - Repository evidence: Monolithic Python orchestration files exceeding hundreds of lines without modular function imports or parameterization.
  - Severity tendency: warning

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated)
- Detecting hardcoded plaintext credentials, passwords, or access tokens in pipeline scripts.
- Verifying the existence of an automated test directory (`tests/` or `test/`) or framework invocation in CI configurations.
- Checking for dependency lockfiles (`requirements.txt`, `Pipfile.lock`, `poetry.lock`, `package-lock.json`) alongside manifest files.
- Verifying that local environment secret files (`.env`, `credentials.json`) are listed in `.gitignore`.
### Needs-review candidates
- Validating the structural coverage and assertion types of data validation frameworks (e.g., Great Expectations, Soda, dbt tests).
- Checking whether orchestration DAG definitions (e.g., Airflow, Dagster) include proper retry and failure notification handlers.
### Human review (contextual & organizational)
- Evaluating whether cross-functional team roles (Data Product Owners, Data Engineers, Analysts) are effectively aligned around value streams.
- Assessing the quality and business relevance of data glossary definitions and user documentation.
### Organizational artifacts check
- Verifying repository ownership configuration via `CODEOWNERS` or `OWNERS` files against organizational team registries.

## 10. Candidate rules

- Rule ID: dataops-atwal-001
  Title: Hardcoded plaintext credentials in pipeline code
  Profile: dataops
  Severity tendency: critical
  Rationale: Hardcoding credentials or secret keys in version control introduces severe security vulnerabilities and violates DataOps secret separation principles.
  Source-backed basis: Source mandates storing credentials securely outside version control using environment variables or secret vaults.
  Repository evidence to inspect: `.py`, `.sql`, `.yaml`, `.json`, or `.sh` files in the repository.
  Pass condition: No hardcoded API keys, passwords, or access tokens are present in source files.
  Fail condition: Plaintext credentials match regex patterns in code files.
  Exceptions or limitations: Mock test credentials inside test fixture files are exempt.
  YAML readiness: ready
  Source citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps, Spirit: Credentials must never appear in version control code.
  Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-atwal-002
  Title: Missing automated test directory or test execution
  Profile: dataops
  Severity tendency: error
  Rationale: Automated testing is essential to DataOps continuous delivery; omitting tests risks releasing corrupt data pipelines into production.
  Source-backed basis: Source highlights automated test-driven development as a core requirement for building trust and reliability.
  Repository evidence to inspect: Presence of `tests/`, `test/`, `models/schema.yml`, or test execution steps in CI workflows.
  Pass condition: At least one recognized test directory, test manifest, or test step is present.
  Fail condition: Neither a test directory nor an automated test invocation is found in the repository.
  Exceptions or limitations: Pure documentation repositories are exempt.
  YAML readiness: ready
  Source citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps, Spirit: Automated testing guarantees pipeline safety and rapid feedback loops.
  Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-atwal-003
  Title: Missing dependency lockfile for reproducible builds
  Profile: dataops
  Severity tendency: warning
  Rationale: Unpinned dependencies cause environment drift, resulting in non-reproducible pipeline builds across development and production.
  Source-backed basis: Source emphasizes configuration management and environment parity as mandatory for system reproducibility.
  Repository evidence to inspect: Dependency manifest files (`requirements.txt`, `Pipfile`, `pyproject.toml`).
  Pass condition: A corresponding lockfile (`requirements.txt` with pinned versions, `Pipfile.lock`, `poetry.lock`) is present.
  Fail condition: Dependency manifest exists without a corresponding lockfile or pinned version declarations.
  Exceptions or limitations: Standalone scripts with no external package dependencies.
  YAML readiness: ready
  Source citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 7: DevOps for DataOps, Spirit: Versioning all configuration items maintains total system integrity.
  Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-atwal-004
  Title: Incomplete metadata tracking in pipeline DAGs
  Profile: dataops
  Severity tendency: warning
  Rationale: Pipelines executed without operational metadata logging prevent lineage tracing and delay root cause analysis during failures.
  Source-backed basis: Source highlights operational metadata as essential for capturing execution provenance and data lineage.
  Repository evidence to inspect: Orchestration DAG files inside `dags/` or `orchestration/`.
  Pass condition: DAG tasks configure explicit metadata logging or audit callbacks.
  Fail condition: DAG tasks omit metadata collection and logging configurations.
  Exceptions or limitations: Simple single-step scripts.
  YAML readiness: needs-review
  Reason if not ready: Requires AST or structural Python parsing to inspect DAG task definitions accurately.
  Source citation: Harvinder Atwal - Practical DataOps, Chapter 6: Building Trust, Spirit: Operational metadata encapsulates provenance and data processing results.
  Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment
```yaml

```

# Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs
- Title: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs
- Author(s): Hugh Jack
- Branch: iiot
- RepoScope profile: iiot
- Primary source file: Jack, H - Automating Manufacturing Systems with Plcs (2010).pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

---

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Fundamental PLC operations including the continuous scan cycle (self test, input scan, logic solve, output scan), watchdog timers, and memory structures.
  - Ladder logic design paradigms, including relay-style contact/coil logic, latching/unlatching instructions, state-based block logic, and state transition equations.
  - Advanced discrete control concepts including Sequential Function Charts (SFC), Structured Text (ST), timers (TON, TOF, RTO), counters (CTU, CTD), and subroutines.
  - Hardwired emergency safety circuits, Master Control Relays (MCR), Safety Control Relays (SCR), and independent hardware interlocks.
  - Verification and audit translation of control logic, tag declarations, state initializations, and scan-time safety considerations.
- Out of scope:
  - Non-deterministic high-level software development or general IT server networking unrelated to PLC fieldbus or control loops.
  - Mechanical component fabrication, structural CAD design, and fluid power piping schematics except where directly actuated by PLC outputs.
  - Enterprise ERP software integration, business intelligence analytics, and non-operational database administration.

---

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Core philosophy: Control logic must be deterministic, highly structured, and strictly cyclical, maintaining predictable real-time response to physical inputs across every scan cycle.
2. Dominant architectural bias: Sequential and state-driven execution patterns where state memory is explicitly latched, unlatched, or tracked in structured blocks to prevent ambiguous active states.
3. Stance on complexity: Complex logic should be modularized using subroutines, state equations, or Sequential Function Charts (SFC) rather than unstructured, monolithic ladder rungs.
4. View of developer/operator error: Programmer logic errors and edge cases (such as missing first-scan initializations or race conditions during input changes) are inevitable risks that must be bounded by systematic state design and hardwired safety interlocks.
5. Highest-priority system virtue: Deterministic operational safety and scan execution integrity, ensuring physical machinery cannot enter unsafe or uncoordinated physical states.
6. Acceptable trade-offs: Sacrificing program elegance, high-level abstractions, or execution speed to maintain transparent, easily debuggable, and deterministic ladder logic.
7. Unacceptable trade-offs: Relying solely on software control logic or PLC outputs for emergency stops without independent hardwired Safety Control Relays (SCR) and physical interlocks.
8. Mental model of repository / system state: A cyclic, snapshot-based input/output table driven by deterministic ladder logic equations and state transition blocks.
9. Attitude toward automation vs human oversight: Automated scan cycles drive low-level real-time control, but manual emergency stop hardware and operator overrides must always supersede software execution.
10. Defining failure mode according to source: Program logic errors causing unhandled state transitions, missed input signals due to scan timing issues, or software-only emergency stops failing during PLC hardware lockup.

---

## 4. Source-backed structure

### Theme 1: Deterministic PLC Scan Cycle and Memory Snapshots
- Source: Chapter 8 (PLC Operation)
- Structural locator: Chapter 8: Operation Sequence — The Scan Cycle
- Spirit locator: PLCs evaluate logic against a frozen input snapshot in memory, ensuring that input updates mid-scan do not create race conditions or inconsistent logic evaluations across rungs.

### Theme 2: Hardwired Safety Control Relays and Emergency Stop Architecture
- Source: Chapter 31 (Safety)
- Structural locator: Chapter 31: Hardware Safety — Emergency Stops and Master Control Relays
- Spirit locator: Emergency stops and primary safety shutoffs must remain physically hardwired to disconnect actuator power independently of PLC hardware and software execution state.

### Theme 3: State-Based Control and Equation-Driven Logic
- Source: Chapter 12 (State Based Design)
- Structural locator: Chapter 12: Introduction to State Based Design — State Equations and Block Logic
- Spirit locator: Machine operations must be modeled as discrete, mutually exclusive states using state equations or structured blocks to prevent uncoordinated actuator movements.

### Theme 4: Sequential Function Charts (SFC) and Concurrent Execution
- Source: Chapter 20 (Sequential Function Charts)
- Structural locator: Chapter 20: SFC Implementation in Ladder Logic — Step and Transition Execution Order
- Spirit locator: Complex concurrent processes require strict separation of steps, transitions, and action executions, initialized explicitly on the first scan to avoid orphan states.

---

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. Primary purpose of system architecture: To govern physical process machinery deterministically through structured PLC control cycles while enforcing hardwired physical safety baselines.
2. Acceptable vs unacceptable risk: Acceptable risk includes increased logic program length for explicit state blocks; unacceptable risk includes software-only emergency stops or uninitialized state registers during PLC startup.
3. Implicit assumptions vs explicit declarations: All state variables, timer presets, state transitions, and first-scan initialization routines must be explicitly declared in code; implicit state retention is strictly prohibited.
4. Speed/convenience vs safety/correctness: Safety and physical deterministic correctness unconditionally take precedence over programming brevity or remote execution convenience.
5. Human intervention vs automated enforcement: Automated logic handles routine scan-based sequencing, but physical operator emergency stop buttons must immediately cut relay power to outputs regardless of PLC software status.
6. Legacy constraints and backward compatibility: Legacy relay ladder concepts (contacts, coils, interlocks) provide the visual and logical safety model for modern software PLCs and structured text equivalents.

---

## 6. Key concepts

### 1. PLC Scan Cycle
- Definition: The continuous four-stage execution loop consisting of self-test (sanity check/watchdog reset), input scan (snapshot to memory), logic solve, and output scan.
- Why it matters: Ensures input values remain fixed throughout logic evaluation, preventing race conditions within a single cycle.
- Related concepts: Input Table, Logic Scan, Output Table, Watchdog Timer.
- Citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / plc-scan-cycle
- Verification status: source-backed

### 2. Safety Control Relay (SCR)
- Definition: A hardwired electromechanical relay circuit connected in series with emergency stop buttons that removes main power from output modules independently of the PLC.
- Why it matters: Guarantees that a PLC fault or software lockup cannot prevent an emergency stop from de-energizing actuators.
- Related concepts: Master Control Relay (MCR), Emergency Stop (E-Stop), Hardwired Safety.
- Citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / safety-control-relay
- Verification status: source-backed

### 3. First Scan Bit
- Definition: A specialized system memory flag (e.g., S2:1/15 or FS) that is true for exactly one scan cycle after the PLC powers up or enters RUN mode.
- Why it matters: Used to force initial state bits, clear accumulators, and set default parameters before normal state machine execution begins.
- Related concepts: System Status Memory, State Initialization, Startup Routine.
- Citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / first-scan-bit
- Verification status: source-backed

### 4. State Equation Method
- Definition: A mathematical design approach where Boolean logic expressions represent state memory retention and transition conditions for ladder logic translation.
- Why it matters: Converts state transition diagrams directly into verified, deterministic ladder logic rungs without relying on unlatching bugs.
- Related concepts: Block Logic, Latch/Unlatch, Sequential Control.
- Citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / state-equation-method
- Verification status: source-backed

### 5. Sequential Function Chart (SFC)
- Definition: An IEC 61131-3 graphical programming language that organizes control into steps, transitions, and actions for sequential or parallel processes.
- Why it matters: Simplifies complex multi-state machine programming and enables concurrent step execution while maintaining clear transition logic.
- Related concepts: Steps, Transitions, End of Transition (EOT).
- Citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / sequential-function-chart
- Verification status: source-backed

### 6. Retentive Timer (RTO)
- Definition: A PLC timer instruction that retains its accumulated time value even when the rung condition goes false or power is cycled, requiring an explicit RESET instruction.
- Why it matters: Prevents loss of accumulated process timing during temporary power interruptions or cycle pauses.
- Related concepts: TON Timer, TOF Timer, Preset Value, Accumulator Value.
- Citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / retentive-timer
- Verification status: source-backed

---

## 7. Practical patterns and examples

### Pattern 1: Hardwired Emergency Stop with PLC Fault Contacts
- Problem solved: Prevents catastrophic machine overrun or injury when a PLC crashes, freezes, or experiences internal hardware fault.
- Recommended approach: Wire emergency stop buttons in series with a hardwired Safety Control Relay (SCR) coil and a normally-open PLC Fault Contact. The SCR contacts switch main power to all digital output cards, while the PLC opens its fault contact if the watchdog timer expires.
- Repository-level manifestation: Hardwired wiring diagrams (`wiring-schematic.pdf`) and PLC configuration manifests explicitly defining watchdog timeout thresholds and fault output contacts (`rack-config.yaml`).
- Limitations: Requires physical electromechanical wiring outside the software repository.
- Source citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / hardwired-estop-pattern

### Pattern 2: First-Scan State Machine Initialization
- Problem solved: Prevents random or multi-state active conditions upon PLC startup or mode change.
- Recommended approach: Examine the First Scan system bit (`FS` / `S2:1/15`) on the initial rung of the state control program to unconditionally LATCH the initial state (`Step_1` / `STA`) and UNLATCH all remaining state bits (`Step_2` through `Step_N`).
- Repository-level manifestation: Ladder logic source files (`*.st`, `*.scl`, `*.lad`) containing explicit `IF FirstScan THEN State := STEP_1; END_IF;` or first-scan latch rungs.
- Limitations: Must be executed before any state transition logic rungs in the scan cycle.
- Source citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / first-scan-initialization-pattern

### Pattern 3: Dual-Hand Palm Button Press Safety Logic
- Problem solved: Ensures an operator's hands are clear of hazardous stamping or cutting machinery before initiating a cycle.
- Recommended approach: Require two independent palm button inputs (`P1` and `P2`) to be pressed simultaneously within a tight time window (e.g., < 0.5 seconds) using a dedicated timer instruction before setting the cycle start latch.
- Repository-level manifestation: Structured text or ladder logic routines (`press_control.st`) enforcing concurrent input verification and timer checks prior to output coil energization.
- Limitations: Software dual-hand control must complement, not replace, physical light curtains or two-hand control safety relays where mandated.
- Source citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / dual-hand-safety-pattern

---

## 8. Common failure modes and risks

### 1. Software-Only Emergency Stop Implementation
- Why it happens: Programmers route emergency stop pushbuttons solely as digital inputs to PLC logic rungs without hardwired power disconnection.
- Early warning signs: E-stop buttons wired directly to PLC input terminals while output card power rails bypass Safety Control Relays.
- Repository evidence: Tag lists mapping `E_Stop_PB` solely to internal logic coils (`M0.0`) without physical SCR feedback signals.
- Severity tendency: Critical

### 2. Missing First-Scan State Reset Logic
- Why it happens: State machine variables rely on default memory persistence across power cycles without explicit first-scan initialization.
- Early warning signs: Machine powers up with multiple state bits active simultaneously or skips the homing step after a power outage.
- Repository evidence: State machine routines (`*.st`, `*.scl`) lacking references to first-scan system flags (`S2:1/15`, `FirstScan`, `FS`).
- Severity tendency: Error

### 3. Unlatched Retentive Timers Causing Process Jumps
- Why it happens: Developers use retentive timer instructions (`RTO`) for cyclic operations without adding explicit reset (`RES`) instructions upon cycle completion.
- Early warning signs: Process cycles complete prematurely or trigger immediate timeout faults upon restart.
- Repository evidence: Code containing `RTO` instructions without corresponding `RES` instructions bound to cycle-reset conditions.
- Severity tendency: Warning

### 4. Race Conditions from Mid-Scan Memory Writes
- Why it happens: Direct I/O memory manipulation or immediate I/O update commands alter input/output images during logic solving rather than respecting scan boundary snapshots.
- Early warning signs: Intermittent, non-reproducible logic glitches where identical inputs evaluate differently across consecutive rungs in the same scan.
- Repository evidence: Unbounded usage of immediate read/write instructions (`IIR`, `IIW`) inside routine control loops.
- Severity tendency: Error

---

## 9. Repository-audit translation

### Static checks (automated)
- Verification of PLC structured text, ladder logic exports, and configuration files for first-scan state initializations, hardcoded password parameters, and required changelog manifests.
- Detection of unreferenced retentive timers (`RTO` without `RES`) or improper state machine variable initialization.
- Validation that tag declarations and I/O address mappings follow structured naming and type definitions.

### Human review (operational & safety-culture)
- Operational audit of physical Safety Control Relay (SCR) wiring diagrams and emergency stop physical loop test logs.
- Inspection of physical mechanical interlocks, guard door limit switches, and hydraulic/pneumatic manual bleed valves.
- Evaluation of operator safety training records and lockout/tagout (LOTO) field procedures.

### Safety-critical exclusions
- Physical Emergency Stop button mechanical switches, hardwired Safety Control Relay contacts, and physical machine guarding must never be evaluated or modified by static repository tools.
- Mechanical relief valves, physical pressure switches, and hardwired Master Control Relays are explicitly excluded from static software auditing.

---

## 10. Candidate rules

### Candidate Rule 1
- Rule ID: iiot-plc-001
- Title: Missing First-Scan Initialization in PLC State Machine Logic
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: PLC state machines that do not initialize default state variables on the first scan (`FirstScan` / `FS` / `S2:1/15`) can power up in ambiguous or dangerous multi-state conditions.
- Source-backed basis: Hugh Jack Chapter 12 & Chapter 20 emphasize that state diagrams and SFCs implemented in ladder logic must explicitly initialize starting steps and reset transition flags during the first scan.
- Repository evidence to inspect: PLC source files (`*.st`, `*.scl`, `*.lad`, `*.xml`, `*.yaml`) defining state variables (`Step`, `State`, `Seq`, `ST1`).
- Pass condition: State machine files contain explicit checks for first scan system flags (`FirstScan`, `FS`, `S2:1/15`, `STEP_0_START`) to initialize state variables.
- Fail condition: State variables are assigned or manipulated without any first-scan initialization routine present in the program file.
- Exceptions or limitations: Single-state or combinational-only logic routines that do not utilize sequential state variables.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / first-scan-bit
- Source: Hugh Jack - Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs
- Structural locator: Chapter 12: State Based Design — State Equations / Chapter 20: Sequential Function Charts — Initialization
- Spirit locator: State variables must be explicitly initialized on the first scan cycle to ensure deterministic startup behavior.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 2
- Rule ID: iiot-plc-002
- Title: Hardcoded Credentials or Default Passwords in PLC Source Code
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Storing plaintext access keys or default vendor passwords in PLC code or configuration manifests allows unauthorized programming access and logic modification.
- Source-backed basis: Hugh Jack Chapter 8 and general IIoT repository safety mandate removing hardcoded credentials from automation project files to prevent unauthorized controller manipulation.
- Repository evidence to inspect: PLC code, structured text, and project manifests (`*.st`, `*.scl`, `*.yaml`, `*.json`, `*.ini`) for hardcoded password strings.
- Pass condition: No plaintext passwords or default access credentials exist in project files.
- Fail condition: Password or authentication strings contain hardcoded default values (e.g., `admin`, `password`, `1234`, `PLC`).
- Exceptions or limitations: Public template files explicitly marked with `.example` extensions.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / plc-security-baselines
- Source: Hugh Jack - Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs
- Structural locator: Chapter 8: PLC Operation — Program Memory and Protection
- Spirit locator: Passwords and access credentials must never be hardcoded into automation control logic.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 3
- Rule ID: iiot-plc-003
- Title: Missing Changelog Manifest for PLC Controller Project
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Modifying PLC logic or tag structures without maintaining a version-controlled changelog prevents auditability and obscures unauthorized changes to machine logic.
- Source-backed basis: Hugh Jack Chapter 31 & Chapter 34 highlight change management and documentation as vital practices for maintaining reliable automated systems.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Project root directory for change documentation (`CHANGELOG*`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `MANIFEST*`).
- Pass condition: A valid changelog file exists in the repository root or documentation path.
- Fail condition: Repository lacks a CHANGELOG or modification audit manifest.
- Exceptions or limitations: None.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / change-documentation
- Source: Hugh Jack - Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs
- Structural locator: Chapter 31: Safety — Documentation / Chapter 34: Engineering Design
- Spirit locator: Automated system logic modifications must be accompanied by version-controlled documentation.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 4
- Rule ID: iiot-plc-004
- Title: Software-Only Emergency Stop Input Mapping Without SCR Feedback
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Routing emergency stop inputs purely to software coils without monitoring hardwired Safety Control Relay (SCR) contacts risks machine overrun if the PLC hangs.
- Source-backed basis: Hugh Jack Chapter 31 requires emergency stops to be physically hardwired to SCR relays, independent of PLC software execution.
- Repository evidence to inspect: PLC tag lists, Structured Text files, and electrical schematic manifests.
- Pass condition: Emergency stop inputs are paired with hardwired SCR feedback contacts.
- Fail condition: E-stop inputs drive software outputs directly without hardwired relay feedback.
- Exceptions or limitations: Cannot fully verify physical wiring continuity from static code regex alone.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Static regex cannot verify physical electromechanical relay wiring or external hardware feedback circuits.
- Source citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / safety-control-relay
- Source: Hugh Jack - Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs
- Structural locator: Chapter 31: Hardware Safety — Emergency Stops
- Spirit locator: Safety shutoffs must be hardwired and independent of software logic.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 5
- Rule ID: iiot-plc-005
- Title: Physical Mechanical Interlock and Guard Door Inspection
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Machine physical safety depends on mechanical interlocks and physical guards that cannot be verified by scanning software source files.
- Source-backed basis: Hugh Jack Chapter 31 emphasizes that hardware safety grounding and physical guards are the primary safety barrier.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Physical plant equipment and site safety logs.
- Pass condition: N/A (Non-static check).
- Fail condition: N/A (Non-static check).
- Exceptions or limitations: Cannot be checked statically.
- YAML readiness: blocked
- Reason if not ready: Physical machine guarding and mechanical interlocks exist entirely outside digital software repositories.
- Source citation: Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs / hardware-safety-rules
- Source: Hugh Jack - Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs
- Structural locator: Chapter 31: Safety — Grounding and Fuses
- Spirit locator: Physical safety hardware is mandatory but non-auditable via static software repository scans.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment

```yaml
rules:
  - id: iiot-plc-001
    title: Missing First-Scan Initialization in PLC State Machine Logic
    profile: iiot
    severity: error
    message: "PLC state machine configuration or code appears to lack a first-scan initialization check (e.g., FirstScan, FS, S2:1/15)."
    references:
      - book: "Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs"
        concept: "first-scan-bit"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{st,scl,lad,xml,yaml}"
      pattern: "((?i)firstscan|\\bFS\\b|S2:1/15|STEP_0_START)"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_absent

  - id: iiot-plc-002
    title: Hardcoded Credentials or Default Passwords in PLC Source Code
    profile: iiot
    severity: critical
    message: "Hardcoded password or default authentication string detected in PLC project files."
    references:
      - book: "Automating Manufacturing Systems with PLCs"
        concept: "plc-security-baselines"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{st,scl,yaml,json,ini}"
      pattern: "(?i)(password|passwd|secret)\\s*[:=]\\s*[\"']?(admin|password|1234|plc)[\"']?"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_present

  - id: iiot-plc-003
    title: Missing Changelog Manifest for PLC Controller Project
    profile: iiot

      error_when: file_absent
```

  - Title: Physical Mechanical Interlock and Guard Door Inspection
  - Readiness: `blocked`
  - Reason: Physical machine guarding and mechanical interlocks exist entirely outside digital software repositories.
