===== FILE: docs/notes/iiot/opc-unified-architecture.md =====
# OPC Unified Architecture — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** OPC Unified Architecture
- Title: OPC Unified Architecture
- Author(s): Wolfgang Mahnke, Stefan-Helmut Leitner, Matthias Damm
- Branch: iiot
- RepoScope profile: iiot
- Primary source file: OPC Unified Architecture.pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

---

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - OPC UA Unified Address Space meta-model, NodeClasses (Object, ObjectType, Variable, VariableType, Method, ReferenceType, DataType, View), Attributes, and typed References.
  - Layered software architecture separating abstract Services (Part 4) from encoding (UA Binary, XML), security (UA-SecureConversation, WS-SecureConversation), and transport mappings (UA TCP, SOAP/HTTP) (Part 6).
  - Security model encompassing Application Instance Certificates (X.509v3), Security Policies, Security Modes (None, Sign, SignAndEncrypt), Secure Channel establishment, Session activation, and PKI trust models.
  - Information modeling mechanisms, object-oriented type hierarchies, ModellingRules, InstanceDeclarations, and domain-specific models (Data Access, Alarms & Conditions, Historical Access, Programs, PLCopen, FDI).
  - Migration strategies for Classic OPC (DA, A&E, HDA) using wrappers, proxies, and native SDK integration.
- Out of scope:
  - Vendor-specific fieldbus hardware drivers or low-level physical wiring implementations.
  - General enterprise IT web application security lacking industrial control or SCADA integration.
  - Operating system patching, general network router maintenance, or manual plant physical access controls.

---

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Core philosophy: Platform-independent, unified, and secure industrial data communications that decouple semantic object modeling from concrete wire encodings and transport mechanisms.
2. Dominant architectural bias: Strongly typed, object-oriented address space model connecting nodes in rich hierarchical and non-hierarchical mesh networks across all tiers of the automation pyramid.
3. Stance on complexity: Hide protocol serialization and cryptographic handshake complexity inside standard communication stacks and SDKs while presenting abstract, data-agnostic Services to application developers.
4. View of developer/operator error: Misconfigured endpoints (e.g., SecurityMode "None", auto-accepting untrusted certificates) represent severe security hazards that must be prevented via explicit security policies and automated validation gates.
5. Highest-priority system virtue: Deterministic, interoperable data semantics combined with robust message-level authentication, encryption, and availability across distributed operational tiers.
6. Acceptable trade-offs: Slightly higher computational overhead for asymmetric certificate handshaking and metadata exposure in exchange for complete vendor neutrality, scalability, and strong end-to-end security.
7. Unacceptable trade-offs: Depending on legacy Windows-bound COM/DCOM technology, transmitting unauthenticated control commands across plant networks, or breaking backward migration compatibility for Classic OPC installations.
8. Mental model of repository / system state: A full-meshed directed graph of Nodes (Objects, Variables, Methods) governed by object-oriented type definitions, Namespace-qualified NodeIds, and formal XML information models.
9. Attitude toward automation vs human oversight: Stacks automate chunking, encryption, and session maintenance, while administrators manage PKI trust stores, endpoint security policies, and domain-specific information model bindings.
10. Defining failure mode according to source: Exposing OPC UA endpoints with SecurityMode `None` or disabled certificate validation, enabling unauthorized remote write injection, eavesdropping, or loss of industrial process state integrity.

---

## 4. Source-backed structure

### Theme 1: Unified Address Space and Object-Oriented Meta-Model
- Source: Chapter 2 (Information Modeling: Concepts) and Chapter 3 (Information Modeling: Example and Best Practice)
- Structural locator: Chapter 2: Nodes and References / Chapter 3: Best Practices
- Spirit locator: OPC UA unifies fragmented Classic OPC interfaces into a single address space where data, alarms, and history are exposed as typed Nodes connected by semantic References.

### Theme 2: Layered Architecture, Encoding, and Transport Mappings
- Source: Chapter 5 (Services) and Chapter 6 (Technology Mapping)
- Structural locator: Chapter 5: Services Overview / Chapter 6: Data Encodings & Transport Protocols
- Spirit locator: Abstract UA Services are completely decoupled from wire transport, allowing high-performance UA TCP with binary encoding on plant floors and firewall-friendly SOAP/HTTP XML at enterprise boundaries.

### Theme 3: Defense-in-Depth Security Model and PKI Life-Cycle
- Source: Chapter 7 (Security)
- Structural locator: Chapter 7: The OPC UA Security Model & Public Key Infrastructure for OPC UA
- Spirit locator: OPC UA mandates multi-layered security using X.509 Application Instance Certificates, mutual authentication, and short-lived symmetric keys derived over asymmetric secure channels.

### Theme 4: Standard Information Models, Profiles, and Migration
- Source: Chapter 4 (Standard Information Models), Chapter 10 (Mapping of COM OPC to OPC UA), and Chapter 11 (Migration)
- Structural locator: Chapter 4: Base OPC UA Information Model / Chapter 10: Overview / Chapter 11: Proxies and Wrappers
- Spirit locator: Reusability and interoperability are achieved through standardized domain information models, certified client/server Profiles, and transparent wrapper/proxy migration bridges for legacy Classic OPC assets.

---

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. Primary purpose of system architecture: To establish a vendor-neutral, scalable, and secure communication infrastructure that seamlessly exchanges rich object-oriented data from field sensors up to enterprise ERP systems.
2. Acceptable vs unacceptable risk: Acceptable risk includes moderate initial setup overhead for PKI certificate distribution; unacceptable risk includes unauthenticated write operations, cleartext telemetry over untrusted subnets, and unvalidated endpoint certificates.
3. Implicit assumptions vs explicit declarations: All security modes, user authentication tokens, data encodings, and node type definitions must be explicitly declared in endpoint configurations and XML information models.
4. Speed/convenience vs safety/correctness: Security and structural correctness unconditionally supersede administrative convenience; bypassing certificate validation or using unencrypted endpoints to speed up deployment violates core architectural rules.
5. Human intervention vs automated enforcement: Communication stacks automate symmetric encryption, message chunking, and session renewal, but human administrators must govern certificate issuing, trust store population, and security policy selection.
6. Legacy constraints and backward compatibility: Legacy DCOM-based Classic OPC systems must be integrated through standard OPC Foundation wrappers and proxies or mapped natively without breaking existing operational interfaces.

---

## 6. Key concepts

### 1. Address Space Model & NodeClasses
- Definition: The fundamental meta-model of OPC UA consisting of Nodes (classified into Object, ObjectType, Variable, VariableType, Method, ReferenceType, DataType, and View) and directed References between them.
- Why it matters: Provides a standardized, object-oriented framework for representing process data, complex metadata, methods, and alarms in a single navigable graph.
- Related concepts: NodeId, QualifiedName, InstanceDeclaration, HasTypeDefinition.
- Citation: OPC Unified Architecture / address-space-model
- Verification status: source-backed

### 2. UA-SecureConversation & Secure Channel
- Definition: A security layer protocol combining symmetric and asymmetric cryptographic techniques to secure message streams between OPC UA applications over UA TCP or HTTP.
- Why it matters: Negotiates short-lived symmetric keys via asymmetric handshakes, offering high-speed encryption and signature verification for real-time telemetry.
- Related concepts: OpenSecureChannel, Symmetric Key Derivation, TokenId.
- Citation: OPC Unified Architecture / ua-secure-conversation
- Verification status: source-backed

### 3. Application Instance Certificate
- Definition: An X.509v3 digital certificate uniquely assigned to a specific installed instance of an OPC UA client or server application.
- Why it matters: Enables mutual application authentication, ensuring that only trusted software instances can negotiate secure channels and exchange data.
- Related concepts: Public Key Infrastructure (PKI), Trust Store, Direct Trust.
- Citation: OPC Unified Architecture / application-instance-certificate
- Verification status: source-backed

### 4. Binary Encoding (UA Binary) vs. XML Encoding
- Definition: Serialization specifications defining how built-in and structured data types are converted into compact binary streams or XML documents for network transport.
- Why it matters: UA Binary eliminates XML parsing overhead for resource-constrained controllers, while XML offers generic human-readable interoperability for MES/ERP systems.
- Related concepts: ExtensionObject, Built-In DataTypes, Variant.
- Citation: OPC Unified Architecture / data-encodings
- Verification status: source-backed

### 5. Security Policy & Security Mode
- Definition: Configurations defining the cryptographic algorithms (e.g., Basic128Rsa15, Basic256) and message protection levels (`None`, `Sign`, `SignAndEncrypt`) supported by an endpoint.
- Why it matters: Dictates the exact cryptographic strength required for client-server communication and prevents downgrade attacks to weak encryptions.
- Related concepts: GetEndpoints, CreateSession, EndpointDescription.
- Citation: OPC Unified Architecture / security-policy-and-mode
- Verification status: source-backed

### 6. Information Model & NodeId Namespace
- Definition: A domain-specific specification (e.g., DI, ADI, PLCopen) that defines structured types and standard nodes using unique NamespaceURIs to prevent collisions.
- Why it matters: Enables generic clients to interpret complex domain semantics without prior proprietary knowledge.
- Related concepts: NamespaceIndex, NamespaceArray, Companion Specification.
- Citation: OPC Unified Architecture / information-models-and-namespaces
- Verification status: source-backed

---

## 7. Practical patterns and examples

### Pattern 1: Secure Channel & Session Handshake with Dual-Token Derivation
- Problem solved: Eliminates heavy public key cryptography overhead for routine telemetry while guaranteeing robust mutual authentication.
- Recommended approach: Client sends `GetEndpoints` to identify available endpoints, issues `OpenSecureChannel` signed/encrypted with asymmetric keys to exchange nonces, derives symmetric keys, and then issues `CreateSession` / `ActivateSession` using user identity tokens.
- Repository-level manifestation: Client/Server configuration files (`Opc.Ua.Server.Config.xml`, `appsettings.json`) specifying explicit `ServerSecurityPolicies` and `UserTokenPolicies`.
- Limitations: Requires initial distribution of trusted X.509 certificates to client and server trust stores.
- Source citation: OPC Unified Architecture / secure-channel-session-handshake

### Pattern 2: High-Performance Local Telemetry via UA TCP / UA Binary
- Problem solved: Minimizes bandwidth and CPU consumption for embedded PLCs and controllers communicating on plant floor subnets.
- Recommended approach: Configure embedded servers to expose endpoints bound to `opc.tcp://` transport with `UA Binary` encoding and `SignAndEncrypt` security mode.
- Repository-level manifestation: Deployment manifests and stack initialization code specifying `opc.tcp` binding URLs and binary encoding profiles.
- Limitations: Binary streams cannot be parsed directly by standard web browsers without an OPC UA client stack or gateway.
- Source citation: OPC Unified Architecture / ua-tcp-binary-pattern

### Pattern 3: Chained Chaining-Server / Gateway Translation
- Problem solved: Bridges isolated network segments or translates communication protocols across firewalls without degrading security.
- Recommended approach: Deploy a chaining server acting simultaneously as an OPC UA server to corporate clients (over HTTPS/XML) and an OPC UA client to plant-floor PLCs (over UA TCP/Binary).
- Repository-level manifestation: Gateway configuration manifests detailing dual endpoint mappings and isolated session forwarding rules.
- Limitations: Introduces minimal latency during message deserialization and re-encryption at the gateway.
- Source citation: OPC Unified Architecture / chaining-server-gateway

---

## 8. Common failure modes and risks

### 1. Exposing Unsecured Endpoints (`SecurityMode: None`) in Production
- Why it happens: Developers or field engineers enable `SecurityMode: None` during testing to bypass certificate setup and forget to disable it before production deployment.
- Early warning signs: Network scans reveal endpoints accepting unencrypted TCP connections on port 4840 without requiring valid certificates.
- Repository evidence: Server XML or YAML configuration manifests containing `<SecurityMode>None</SecurityMode>` or `SecurityPolicies` with `None` enabled in non-test profiles.
- Severity tendency: Critical

### 2. Auto-Accepting Unvalidated Application Instance Certificates
- Why it happens: Application developers implement custom certificate validation callbacks that unconditionally return `True` or automatically save unknown certificates to the trusted store.
- Early warning signs: The OPC UA client connects to untrusted or spoofed servers without raising certificate validation errors or prompt warnings.
- Repository evidence: C# or ANSI C SDK callback code containing `event.Accept = true;` or `AutoAcceptUntrustedCertificates = true`.
- Severity tendency: Critical

### 3. Excessive Asymmetric Cryptographic Operations on Low-Power Controllers
- Why it happens: Misconfiguring short `SecureChannelLifetime` values forces resource-constrained PLCs to execute frequent asymmetric RSA handshakes, causing CPU spikes.
- Early warning signs: High PLC scan time jitter or missed communication deadlines during periodic secure channel renewals.
- Repository evidence: Configuration manifests setting `RequestedLifetime` to excessively low values (e.g., < 60,000 ms).
- Severity tendency: Warning

### 4. Hardcoded OPC UA Server User Credentials
- Why it happens: User authentication configuration files contain plaintext usernames and passwords for `UserNameIdentityToken` validation.
- Early warning signs: Version-controlled repository files contain unencrypted administrative credentials for OPC UA user authentication.
- Repository evidence: Configuration files containing plaintext user credential mappings (e.g., `<User>admin</User><Password>secret</Password>`).
- Severity tendency: Critical

---

## 9. Repository-audit translation

### Static checks (automated)
- Parsing XML/JSON/YAML configuration manifests for OPC UA server endpoints to detect `SecurityMode: None` or insecure `SecurityPolicy` URIs.
- Scanning client and server code for flags enabling `AutoAcceptUntrustedCertificates` or bypassed certificate validation logic.
- Inspecting configuration files for plaintext hardcoded usernames, passwords, or private key file paths.
- Verifying the presence of top-level change control records and deployment manifests for OPC UA information models.

### Human review (operational & safety-culture)
- Operational review of physical PKI Certificate Authority (CA) private key storage and distribution procedures.
- Inspection of plant floor certificate revocation list (CRL) update mechanisms and OCSP responder availability.
- Evaluation of site-level procedures for managing user access role assignments within ActiveDirectory or LDAP integrations.

### Safety-critical exclusions
- Physical plant safety interlocks, hardwired emergency stop circuits, and mechanical overpressure valves are explicitly excluded from static software configuration audits.
- PKI root CA physical security, smartcard storage procedures, and operator background checks cannot be evaluated via static code inspection.

---

## 10. Candidate rules

### Candidate Rule 1
- Rule ID: iiot-opcua-001
- Title: Insecure OPC UA Endpoint SecurityMode None Selection
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: OPC UA endpoints configured with `SecurityMode: None` transmit telemetry and control operations in cleartext without authentication or encryption, exposing the system to eavesdropping and unauthorized command injection.
- Source-backed basis: OPC UA Part 2 & Part 6 dictate that production endpoints must enforce message integrity and confidentiality via `Sign` or `SignAndEncrypt` security modes.
- Repository evidence to inspect: OPC UA configuration files (`*.xml`, `*.json`, `*.yaml`, `*.config`) specifying endpoint security modes.
- Pass condition: Endpoints specify `Sign` or `SignAndEncrypt` security modes and disable `None`.
- Fail condition: Endpoint configurations contain `<SecurityMode>None</SecurityMode>` or equivalent setting in non-test profiles.
- Exceptions or limitations: Isolated local development or unit test configuration manifests explicitly designated as mock environments.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: OPC Unified Architecture / security-policy-and-mode
- Source: Wolfgang Mahnke et al. - OPC Unified Architecture
- Structural locator: Chapter 6: Technology Mapping — Security Protocols / Chapter 7: Security — Security Policies and Profiles
- Spirit locator: Insecure endpoints exposing cleartext communication without authentication violate core OPC UA defense-in-depth principles.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 2
- Rule ID: iiot-opcua-002
- Title: Auto-Accepting Unvalidated Application Instance Certificates
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Automatically accepting untrusted X.509 certificates during connection handshakes bypasses mutual application authentication, enabling Man-in-the-Middle (MitM) attacks and unauthorized client connections.
- Source-backed basis: OPC UA Part 4 & Chapter 7 require strict validation of Application Instance Certificates against trusted certificate stores or Validation Authorities before establishing secure channels.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Source code files (`*.cs`, `*.cpp`, `*.c`, `*.java`, `*.py`) and configuration manifests (`*.xml`, `*.json`).
- Pass condition: Certificate validation logic explicitly verifies issuer signatures, revocation status, and trust store membership.
- Fail condition: Code contains `AutoAcceptUntrustedCertificates = true` or callback handlers that unconditionally accept unverified certificates.
- Exceptions or limitations: Explicit local mock test harnesses designed solely for automated offline unit testing.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: OPC Unified Architecture / application-instance-certificate
- Source: Wolfgang Mahnke et al. - OPC Unified Architecture
- Structural locator: Chapter 7: Security — OPC UA Application Instance Certificates & Public Key Infrastructure
- Spirit locator: Disabling certificate validation destroys mutual application trust and invalidates OPC UA security guarantees.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 3
- Rule ID: iiot-opcua-003
- Title: Hardcoded Hard-coded OPC UA Server User Credentials
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Storing hardcoded plaintext user credentials for `UserNameIdentityToken` authentication in repository configuration files allows unauthorized actors to acquire administrative access to OPC UA servers.
- Source-backed basis: OPC UA Part 4 & Chapter 7 mandate secure user authentication and identity token validation, prohibiting hardcoded plaintext credential storage in configuration manifests.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Configuration files (`*.xml`, `*.json`, `*.yaml`, `*.config`, `*.env`) defining user authentication stores.
- Pass condition: User credentials are managed via external identity providers (e.g., ActiveDirectory, PKI) or secure encrypted secret stores.
- Fail condition: Files contain hardcoded username and password pairs (e.g., `<Username>admin</Username><Password>admin123</Password>`).
- Exceptions or limitations: Explicit example files (`*.example.xml`) containing non-functional placeholder values.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: OPC Unified Architecture / user-authentication-and-authorization
- Source: Wolfgang Mahnke et al. - OPC Unified Architecture
- Structural locator: Chapter 7: Security — User Authentication and Authorization
- Spirit locator: Hardcoded credentials represent severe configuration vulnerabilities that grant full access to server address spaces.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 4
- Rule ID: iiot-opcua-004
- Title: Missing OPC UA Server Application Instance Certificate Expiration Monitoring
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Failing to monitor X.509 certificate expiration dates leads to unexpected connection outages when Application Instance Certificates expire during plant operations.
- Source-backed basis: OPC UA Chapter 7 & Chapter 9 recommend subscribing to server certificate audit alarms to notify administrators months prior to certificate expiration.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Client application subscription handlers and server alarm configuration manifests.
- Pass condition: Server/Client implementation subscribes to or generates certificate expiration audit events.
- Fail condition: No automated monitoring or alarm handling exists for certificate expiration events.
- Exceptions or limitations: Short-lived temporary test environments.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Static file analysis cannot easily verify runtime subscription event handling or administrative notification infrastructure.
- Source citation: OPC Unified Architecture / pki-for-industrial-applications
- Source: Wolfgang Mahnke et al. - OPC Unified Architecture
- Structural locator: Chapter 7: Security — PKI for Industrial Applications / Chapter 9: System Architecture — Audit Events
- Spirit locator: Proactive certificate lifecycle management is required to ensure continuous operational availability.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 5
- Rule ID: iiot-opcua-005
- Title: Inadequate Plant Physical Access Control and PKI Key Storage Security
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Physical access controls guarding private keys, smartcards, and server hardware exist outside digital repositories and cannot be verified via static code analysis.
- Source-backed basis: OPC UA Chapter 7 emphasizes that technical security mechanisms are rendered useless if physical key stores or administrator workstations are physically compromised.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Plant physical security documentation and site access policy manuals (non-code artifacts).
- Pass condition: N/A (Non-static check).
- Fail condition: N/A (Non-static check).
- Exceptions or limitations: Cannot be checked statically.
- YAML readiness: blocked
- Reason if not ready: Requires physical plant inspection and organizational security auditing outside static repository analysis.
- Source citation: OPC Unified Architecture / organizational-perspective-of-security
- Source: Wolfgang Mahnke et al. - OPC Unified Architecture
- Structural locator: Chapter 7: Security — Organizational Perspective of Security
- Spirit locator: Physical security and organizational management form the necessary foundation for technical OPC UA protections.
- Verification status: source-backed

# PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST) — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** PLC Controls with Structured Text
- Title: PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST)
- Author(s): Tom Mejer Antonsen
- Branch: iiot
- RepoScope profile: iiot
- Primary source file: PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST).pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

---

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Structured Text (ST) programming under the IEC 61131-3 international standard for industrial automation controllers (PLCs and PACs).
  - Implementation of deterministic state machines, CASE-based sequencing, edge detection (`R_TRIG`, `F_TRIG`), and standard timer/counter function blocks (`TON`, `TOF`, `CTU`, `CTD`).
  - Best practice code organization, structured type declarations (`ENUM`, `STRUCT`), encapsulation using Functions (`FC`) and Function Blocks (`FB`), and elimination of unstructured flow control ("spaghetti code").
  - Software boundary checks, input signal debouncing, parameter bounds checking, and handling of initialization state via the first-scan system bit (`FirstScan` / `S2:1/15`).
  - Version control strategies, modular testing, and code structure maintainability across multi-vendor development environments (e.g., Siemens SCL, Allen-Bradley Logix).
- Out of scope:
  - Proprietary vendor-specific hardware configuration, backplane layout, and physical wiring diagrams.
  - Active network protocol security, firewall configuration, and external IT/OT network perimeter defense.
  - Hardwired safety relay design and electromechanical interlock installation (except where software interlocks interface with physical safety logic).

---

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Core philosophy: Structured Text provides a standardized, text-based paradigm under IEC 61131-3 that brings high-level software engineering discipline (data typing, modularity, readability) to real-time industrial control.
2. Dominant architectural bias: Modular execution structured around reusable Function Blocks (`FB`), typed parameter passing, and explicit `CASE` state machines, avoiding unstructured jump commands (`GOTO`, `JMP`, `EXIT`).
3. Stance on complexity: Unstructured branching and complex nested `IF-THEN-ELSE` blocks produce unmaintainable "spaghetti code"; complex logic must be refactored into `CASE` statements or encapsulated within dedicated Functions (`FC`) and Function Blocks (`FB`).
4. View of developer/operator error: Programmer logic errors (e.g., infinite loops, uninitialized state registers, unvalidated parameter inputs) cause controller watchdog faults or dangerous physical process states and must be eliminated through defensive programming.
5. Highest-priority system virtue: Deterministic, predictable real-time execution across cyclical scan cycles, ensuring stable logic evaluation without runtime errors or scan-time spikes.
6. Acceptable trade-offs: Higher initial engineering effort and formal abstract modeling in exchange for cross-platform code portability, automated testability, and long-term code maintainability.
7. Unacceptable trade-offs: Allowing infinite loops (`WHILE`, `REPEAT` without bounded exit) or recursions that halt the controller scan, or bypassing variable encapsulation through global state mutations within functions.
8. Mental model of repository / system state: A structured tree of IEC 61131-3 program organization units (POUs)—Programs, Function Blocks, Functions, and User-Defined Data Types (`STRUCT`, `ENUM`)—executing deterministically within a cyclic scan loop.
9. Attitude toward automation vs human oversight: Automated compilation, static linting, and unit simulation validate logical correctness, but clear code formatting, comments, and structured naming remain essential for human plant technicians during commissioning and troubleshooting.
10. Defining failure mode according to source: Scan timeout caused by infinite loops or unbounded iteration, runtime execution error (RTE) due to zero division/out-of-bounds array access, or uninitialized state machine entry leading to unexpected physical actuator movement.

---

## 4. Source-backed structure

### Theme 1: Deterministic Scan Cycles and Bounded Execution Control
- Source: Chapter 2 (PLC Execution Cycle) and Chapter 9 (Conditional Statements)
- Structural locator: Chapter 2: Program Execution — The PLC Scan / Chapter 9: Conditional Statements — Loops and Exit Commands
- Spirit locator: Industrial ST execution repeats continuously; loops (`REPEAT`, `WHILE`, `FOR`) must be strictly bounded to prevent infinite iterations that trigger controller watchdog timeouts and shut down physical plant operations.

### Theme 2: State Machine Architecture and CASE-Based Sequencing
- Source: Chapter 9 (Conditional Statements) and Chapter 13 (Special Functions)
- Structural locator: Chapter 9: CASE Statement / Chapter 13: Sequence Control with State Machines
- Spirit locator: Sequential processes must use explicit `CASE` state machines with enumerated states (`ENUM`) and default `ELSE` alarm handlers, replacing sprawling `IF-THEN-ELSE` chains to maintain readability and eliminate race conditions.

### Theme 3: Modular Encapsulation via Functions (FC) and Function Blocks (FB)
- Source: Chapter 10 (Functions) and Chapter 11 (Function Blocks)
- Structural locator: Chapter 10: Creating Functions — Rules for FCs / Chapter 11: Creating Function Blocks — FB Architecture
- Spirit locator: Functions (`FC`) must be stateless and side-effect-free without direct access to global variables or I/O, while stateful operations requiring memory (timers, edge triggers) must be encapsulated inside Function Blocks (`FB`).

### Theme 4: Code Quality, Defensive Programming, and Anti-Spaghetti Rules
- Source: Chapter 15 (Best Practice ST-Programming) and Chapter 16 (Guide and Help)
- Structural locator: Chapter 15: Best Practice — Avoiding Spaghetti Code / Chapter 16: Module Test and Simulation
- Spirit locator: High-quality ST code explicitly eliminates `GOTO`, `JMP`, and recursive function calls, enforces input parameter validation, and uses structured variables to enable safe code sharing and version control via Git.

---

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. Primary purpose of system architecture: To structure industrial control software into modular, readable, and reusable units (POUs) that execute deterministically without causing runtime exceptions or physical process failures.
2. Acceptable vs unacceptable risk: Acceptable risk includes minor memory overhead for structured data types (`STRUCT`); unacceptable risk includes unvalidated array bounds, divide-by-zero operations, recursion, and direct global variable mutation inside functions.
3. Implicit assumptions vs explicit declarations: All variables, data types, and interface parameters (`VAR_INPUT`, `VAR_OUTPUT`, `VAR_IN_OUT`) must be explicitly declared with appropriate types; implicit data conversion and uninitialized variable usage are forbidden.
4. Speed/convenience vs safety/correctness: Safety, code readability, and strict state initialization unconditionally override quick hacks; temporary debug shortcuts (`EXIT`, `JMP`) must be removed before production deployment.
5. Human intervention vs automated enforcement: Automated compiler checks and simulation tools verify syntax and execution, but clear variable naming, structural layout, and comprehensive comments are required to support human operators during plant outages.
6. Legacy constraints and backward compatibility: Legacy relay logic (Ladder Diagram) concepts must be refactored into structured IEC 61131-3 ST construct patterns rather than ported verbatim using unstructured labels and jumps.

---

## 6. Key concepts

### 1. Program Organization Unit (POU)
- Definition: The fundamental structural block in IEC 61131-3, consisting of Programs (`PROGRAM`), Function Blocks (`FUNCTION_BLOCK`), and Functions (`FUNCTION`).
- Why it matters: Establishes explicit scope, memory allocation, and execution boundaries for industrial automation code.
- Related concepts: Function (`FC`), Function Block (`FB`), Method.
- Citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / program-organization-unit
- Verification status: source-backed

### 2. Bounded Iteration
- Definition: The constraint that all loop constructs (`FOR`, `WHILE`, `REPEAT`) must have deterministic, strictly bounded loop bounds and guaranteed termination conditions.
- Why it matters: Prevents infinite loop execution that exceeds the controller scan time threshold and triggers a fatal watchdog fault.
- Related concepts: Watchdog Timer, Program Scan, Runtime Error.
- Citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / bounded-iteration
- Verification status: source-backed

### 3. CASE State Machine
- Definition: A control structure that evaluates an integer or enumerated state variable to execute exactly one branch of sequential logic per scan cycle.
- Why it matters: Replaces unmaintainable nested `IF-THEN-ELSE` statements ("spaghetti code") and ensures deterministic single-state execution.
- Related concepts: ENUM, State Diagram, Transition.
- Citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / case-state-machine
- Verification status: source-backed

### 4. First-Scan Initialization (`FirstScan` / `S2:1/15`)
- Definition: A system bit or initialization variable that evaluates to `TRUE` only during the first scan cycle following controller startup or mode change.
- Why it matters: Ensures state variables, counters, and communication buffers are explicitly set to safe default initial values before physical outputs activate.
- Related concepts: Startup State, State Initialization, Cold Restart.
- Citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / first-scan-initialization
- Verification status: source-backed

### 5. Edge Detection (`R_TRIG` / `F_TRIG`)
- Definition: A standard function block that detects a false-to-true (`R_TRIG` rising edge) or true-to-false (`F_TRIG` falling edge) transition of a digital signal during a single program scan.
- Why it matters: Prevents continuous execution of event-driven commands (e.g., pulse counting, step transitions) across multiple scan cycles.
- Related concepts: One-Shot, Pulse Detection, Signal Debouncing.
- Citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / edge-detection
- Verification status: source-backed

### 6. Defensive Function Boundary
- Definition: The rule that a stateless Function (`FC`) must only communicate through `VAR_INPUT`, `VAR_OUTPUT`, and `VAR_IN_OUT`, without modifying global variables or calling itself recursively.
- Why it matters: Guarantees thread safety, reentrancy safety, and cross-platform reusability across different IEC 61131-3 PLC compilers.
- Related concepts: Encapsulation, Side Effect, Recursion Prohibition.
- Citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / defensive-function-boundary
- Verification status: source-backed

---

## 7. Practical patterns and examples

### Pattern 1: CASE State Machine with Default Alarm Handler
- Problem solved: Eliminates sprawling, race-condition-prone `IF` cascades and guarantees safe recovery when state variables contain invalid values.
- Recommended approach: Define state values using an `ENUM` type (`STEP_INIT`, `STEP_RUN`, `STEP_STOP`). Structure main sequence using a `CASE` statement, and include an `ELSE` section that sets an alarm flag and forces the state machine back to a safe startup state (`STEP_INIT`).
- Repository-level manifestation: Structured Text files (`*.st`, `*.scl`) containing `TYPE State_ENUM : (STEP_INIT, STEP_RUN, STEP_STOP) := STEP_INIT; END_TYPE` and `CASE StepVar OF ... ELSE StepVar := STEP_INIT; AlarmFlag := TRUE; END_CASE;`.
- Limitations: Requires complete enumeration of all valid operational states during software design.
- Source citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / case-state-machine-pattern

### Pattern 2: Encapsulated Input Signal Debouncer and Alarm Delay
- Problem solved: Prevents mechanical switch bounce or liquid level wave turbulence from repeatedly triggering and damaging physical actuators (motors, pumps).
- Recommended approach: Implement a Function Block (`FB_AlarmOnOffDelay`) combining an On-Delay Timer (`TON`) and Off-Delay Timer (`TOF`). Require input signals to remain stable for a configurable period (`OnDelaySec`) before changing the output state.
- Repository-level manifestation: Function Block files (`FB_AlarmOnOffDelay.st`) defining `VAR_INPUT IN : BOOL; OnDelaySec : WORD; END_VAR` and internal timers `TimerOn : TON; TimerOff : TOF;`.
- Limitations: Introduces a controlled delay equal to `OnDelaySec` before state transitions execute.
- Source citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / signal-debouncer-pattern

### Pattern 3: Defensive Parameter Validation in FC/FB
- Problem solved: Prevents fatal controller runtime errors (RTE) such as division by zero or array index out-of-bounds errors during process calculation.
- Recommended approach: At the beginning of a Function or Function Block, validate all input parameters (`VAR_INPUT`). If an input is invalid (e.g., denominator = 0), set an error output parameter (`Error := TRUE`), assign a safe default return value, and bypass the calculation logic.
- Repository-level manifestation: Function code (`FC_Divide.st`) checking `IF Denominator = 0 THEN Error := TRUE; Result := 0.0; ELSE Result := Numerator / Denominator; END_IF;`.
- Limitations: Requires additional parameter validation code lines at the start of every mathematical Function.
- Source citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / parameter-validation-pattern

---

## 8. Common failure modes and risks

### 1. Unbounded Loop Watchdog Trip
- Why it happens: Developers use `WHILE` or `REPEAT` loops with conditions dependent on external sensor inputs that may not change during a single scan cycle.
- Early warning signs: Intermittent controller watchdog faults or process stoppage when an input sensor fails open/closed.
- Repository evidence: `WHILE` or `REPEAT` statements in `.st`/`.scl` files without explicit loop iteration counters or maximum loop count exit checks.
- Severity tendency: Critical

### 2. Unstructured Jumps (`GOTO` / `JMP`) and Spaghetti Flow
- Why it happens: Porting legacy Ladder Diagram logic or quick debugging patches using jump labels instead of structured control statements.
- Early warning signs: Code blocks that are difficult to trace, skipped variable assignments, or unexpected state transitions during troubleshooting.
- Repository evidence: Presence of `GOTO` or `JMP` statements in `.st` or `.scl` source files.
- Severity tendency: Error

### 3. Recursive Function Call Stack Overflow
- Why it happens: A Function (`FC`) attempts to call itself directly or indirectly to solve a mathematical or tree-traversal algorithm.
- Early warning signs: Immediate PLC crash or memory corruption upon executing the function block call.
- Repository evidence: Function source code where a `FUNCTION` identifier appears as a call within its own code body.
- Severity tendency: Critical

### 4. Uninitialized State Machine Startup Race
- Why it happens: State variables depend on persistent memory values without explicit reset logic tied to the first program scan bit (`FirstScan` / `S2:1/15`).
- Early warning signs: Physical actuators energizing immediately upon controller power restoration without operator start command.
- Repository evidence: State machine logic lacking an explicit `FirstScan` initialization check or `STEP_INIT` assignment during power-up.
- Severity tendency: Error

---

## 9. Repository-audit translation

### Static checks (automated)
- Parsing `.st` and `.scl` files to detect `GOTO`, `JMP`, unbounded `WHILE`/`REPEAT` loops, recursive function calls, and missing `ELSE` branches in `CASE` statements.
- Verification of hardcoded passwords, unencrypted protocol port configurations, or missing project changelogs in repository configuration manifests.
- Static pattern matching for proper usage of `FUNCTION_BLOCK`, `VAR_INPUT`, and parameter validation checks.

### Human review (operational & safety-culture)
- Review of process safety interlock integrity and hardware safety relay wiring diagrams.
- Assessment of operator HMI alarm response procedures and manual control override policies.
- Inspection of plant commissioning logs, site physical access security, and electrician maintenance procedures.

### Safety-critical exclusions
- Hardware Safety Control Relays (SCR), physical Emergency Stop (E-Stop) pushbuttons, and mechanical pressure relief valves must never be audited or superseded by static ST software repository checks.
- Site operational safety culture, physical lockout/tagout (LOTO) enforcement, and human operator emergency readiness exist outside static code analysis.

---

## 10. Candidate rules

### Candidate Rule 1
- Rule ID: iiot-st-001
- Title: Unstructured Branching Command Usage (GOTO / JMP)
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Using unstructured jump statements (`GOTO`, `JMP`) breaks deterministic execution flow, creates unmaintainable "spaghetti code", and bypasses variable initialization in Structured Text.
- Source-backed basis: Antonsen Chapter 15 explicitly warns against `GOTO` and `JMP` statements, citing them as the primary cause of unstructured spaghetti code that complicates troubleshooting and causes runtime faults.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Structured Text source files (`*.st`, `*.scl`, `*.pou`, `*.iec`).
- Pass condition: Code contains no `GOTO` or `JMP` keywords.
- Fail condition: Code contains `GOTO` or `JMP` branching statements.
- Exceptions or limitations: Comments containing historical references to jump commands.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / avoiding-spaghetti-code
- Source: Tom Mejer Antonsen - PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST)
- Structural locator: Chapter 15: Best Practice ST-Programming — 15.3 Avoid Spaghetti Code
- Spirit locator: Unstructured jump commands destroy code clarity and must be replaced by structured IF/CASE statements.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 2
- Rule ID: iiot-st-002
- Title: Unbounded Loop Construct Exposure in Cyclic Scan (WHILE / REPEAT)
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Unbounded `WHILE` or `REPEAT` loops can execute indefinitely if exit conditions rely on external hardware sensors, exceeding the PLC scan budget and triggering a fatal watchdog fault.
- Source-backed basis: Antonsen Chapter 2 & Chapter 9 note that `WHILE` and `REPEAT` loops run within a real-time scan cycle; unbounded loops lead to controller non-responsiveness and watchdog trips.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Structured Text source files (`*.st`, `*.scl`, `*.pou`, `*.iec`).
- Pass condition: All `WHILE` and `REPEAT` statements contain explicit iteration counter checks or max-loop limit guards.
- Fail condition: Unbounded `WHILE` or `REPEAT` statements exist without iteration boundary checks.
- Exceptions or limitations: `FOR` loops with fixed integer bounds (`FOR i := 1 TO 10 DO`).
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Industrial Network Security / bounded-iteration
- Source: Tom Mejer Antonsen - PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST)
- Structural locator: Chapter 2: Program Execution — The PLC Scan / Chapter 9: Conditional Statements — Loops
- Spirit locator: Loop iterations must be bounded to protect real-time scan cycle determinism.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 3
- Rule ID: iiot-st-003
- Title: CASE Statement Missing Default ELSE Handler
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: A `CASE` state machine without an `ELSE` section fails to handle corrupted, uninitialized, or out-of-range state variable values, leading to silent sequence lockups.
- Source-backed basis: Antonsen Chapter 9 recommends including an `ELSE` statement in `CASE` constructs to capture unexpected values and flag alarm errors.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Structured Text source files (`*.st`, `*.scl`, `*.pou`, `*.iec`).
- Pass condition: Every `CASE ... END_CASE` construct includes an explicit `ELSE` clause.
- Fail condition: A `CASE` construct lacks an `ELSE` handling block.
- Exceptions or limitations: None.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / case-state-machine
- Source: Tom Mejer Antonsen - PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST)
- Structural locator: Chapter 9: Conditional Statements — 9.2 CASE Statement
- Spirit locator: State machines must explicitly handle out-of-bounds state values to prevent sequence lockup.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 4
- Rule ID: iiot-st-004
- Title: Recursive Function Call Detection in Function POUs
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Recursive function calls in real-time PLC environments cause fast call stack overflow, corrupting controller memory and halting plant operations.
- Source-backed basis: Antonsen Chapter 10 highlights that a Function (`FC`) must never call itself recursively under any circumstances.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Function source files (`*.st`, `*.scl`).
- Pass condition: Function identifiers do not appear as call expressions within their own declaration body.
- Fail condition: A `FUNCTION` calls its own identifier.
- Exceptions or limitations: None.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Static regex cannot differentiate between a function returning its value (`FunctionName := Value;`) and an actual recursive call invocation (`FunctionName := FunctionName(Param);`) across multi-vendor syntaxes without AST parsing.
- Source citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / defensive-function-boundary
- Source: Tom Mejer Antonsen - PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST)
- Structural locator: Chapter 10: Creating Functions — Rules for FCs
- Spirit locator: Recursion is strictly prohibited in real-time control software.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 5
- Rule ID: iiot-st-005
- Title: Missing Physical Emergency Stop Interlock in Actuator Logic
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Physical safety interlocks and hardwired Emergency Stop relays cannot be verified solely from software repository artifacts.
- Source-backed basis: Antonsen Chapter 1 & Chapter 15 note that safety PLCs and physical safety circuits operate under distinct physical constraints.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Physical safety wiring and electromechanical relay schematics.
- Pass condition: N/A (Non-static check).
- Fail condition: N/A (Non-static check).
- Exceptions or limitations: Cannot be checked statically.
- YAML readiness: blocked
- Reason if not ready: Requires physical plant electrical inspection outside digital software repository code.
- Source citation: PLC Controls with Structured Text / physical-safety-interlocks
- Source: Tom Mejer Antonsen - PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST)
- Structural locator: Chapter 1: Introduction — 1.5 Disadvantages of ST / Chapter 15: Best Practice ST-Programming
- Spirit locator: Hardware safety systems override software logic and require physical site verification.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment

```yaml
rules:
  - id: iiot-st-001
    title: Unstructured Branching Command Usage (GOTO / JMP)
    profile: iiot
    severity: error
    message: "Unstructured jump command (GOTO / JMP) detected in Structured Text source code. Replace with structured IF or CASE statements."
    references:
      - book: "PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST)"
        concept: "avoiding-spaghetti-code"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{st,scl,pou,iec}"
      pattern: "(?i)\\b(GOTO|JMP)\\b"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_present

  - id: iiot-st-002
    title: Unbounded Loop Construct Exposure in Cyclic Scan (WHILE / REPEAT)
    profile: iiot
    severity: critical
    message: "Loop statement (WHILE / REPEAT) detected without explicit iteration count guard, risking scan watchdog timeout."
    references:
      - book: "PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST)"
        concept: "bounded-iteration"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{st,scl,pou,iec}"
      pattern: "(?i)\\b(WHILE|REPEAT)\\b"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_present

  - id: iiot-st-003
    title: CASE Statement Missing Default ELSE Handler
    profile: iiot
    severity: warning
    message: "CASE statement detected that lacks an explicit ELSE branch for default error handling."
    references:
      - book: "PLC Controls with Structured Text (ST)"
        concept: "case-state-machine"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{st,scl,pou,iec}"
      pattern: "(?i)\\bCASE\\b(?!.*\\bELSE\\b).*\\bEND_CASE\\b"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_present
```

### Candidates not emitted

- Rule ID: `iiot-st-004`
  - Title: Recursive Function Call Detection in Function POUs
  - Readiness: `needs-review`
  - Reason: Static regex cannot differentiate between a function returning its value (`FunctionName := Value;`) and an actual recursive call invocation (`FunctionName := FunctionName(Param);`) across multi-vendor syntaxes without AST parsing.
- Rule ID: `iiot-st-005`
  - Title: Missing Physical Emergency Stop Interlock in Actuator Logic
  - Readiness: `blocked`
  - Reason: Requires physical plant electrical inspection outside digital software repository code.

# Practical Industrial Data Networks — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Practical Industrial Data Networks
- Title: Practical Industrial Data Networks: Design, Installation and Troubleshooting
- Author(s): Steve Mackay, Edwin Wright, Deon Reynders, John Park
- Branch: iiot
- RepoScope profile: iiot
- Primary source file: Steve Mackay CPEng BSc ( BSc (Hons) MBA, Edwin Wright - Practical Industrial Data Networks_ Design, Installation and Troubleshooting (2004, Newnes) .pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

---

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Physical layer transmission standards including EIA-232, EIA-485, EIA-422, 20 mA current loops, and single-mode/multimode fiber optics.
  - Architecture, frame structures, and troubleshooting of industrial fieldbus protocols including Modbus RTU/ASCII, Modbus Plus, Allen-Bradley Data Highway Plus (DH+)/DH-485, HART, AS-Interface (AS-i), DeviceNet, Profibus DP/PA/FMS, and Foundation Fieldbus (H1/HSE).
  - Industrial Ethernet topologies (10/100/1000 Mbps), CSMA/CD, full-duplex switching, determinism, UTP/ScTP media, AUI interfaces, and protocol encapsulation (Modbus/TCP, EtherNet/IP, PROFINET).
  - Electrical noise coupling mechanisms (capacitive/electrostatic, inductive/magnetic, common impedance, RF radiation), shielding techniques, earthing/grounding requirements, and ground loop prevention.
  - Bus topology rules, impedance matching line terminations ($R_t = 120\,\Omega$), fail-safe idle biassing, drop line stub length limits, and common mode voltage bounds ($-7\,\text{V}$ to $+12\,\text{V}$).
  - Systematic physical and logical troubleshooting methodologies using oscilloscopes, breakout boxes, protocol analyzers, and OTDRs.
- Out of scope:
  - Enterprise/commercial IT network administration lacking industrial/OT physical layer or deterministic timing constraints.
  - Software application logic development outside embedded industrial protocol drivers and fieldbus stacks.
  - High-voltage power transmission and electrical utility substation design beyond communications interference and surge protection.

---

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Core philosophy: Industrial data communications depend fundamentally on physical layer integrity, deterministic media access, proper grounding/shielding, and rigid adherence to protocol framing to maintain reliable plant operations.
2. Dominant architectural bias: Layered OSI protocol stacks anchored by robust physical topologies (balanced differential signaling, fiber optic isolation, line terminations) and explicit master-slave or token-passing media access control.
3. Stance on complexity: Unnecessary physical or protocol complexity (e.g. excessive drop stubs, unmanaged hubs, mixed half/full-duplex modes) introduces noise susceptibility, signal reflections, and intermittent transmission failures.
4. View of developer/operator error: Installation errors—such as floating shields, missing termination resistors, mismatched baud rates, or incorrect subnet masks—are the primary driver of operational downtime and must be prevented via standardized deployment templates and static validation.
5. Highest-priority system virtue: Deterministic signal transmission and high noise immunity, ensuring zero packet loss and predictable latency across harsh industrial electromagnetic environments.
6. Acceptable trade-offs: Sacrificing maximum raw data throughput or cable flexibility to achieve electrical isolation, surge immunity, and differential noise rejection.
7. Unacceptable trade-offs: Omitting line termination resistors, running unshielded cables through high-noise raceways, creating multiple shield ground points (ground loops), or mixing half/full-duplex settings on critical switched links.
8. Mental model of repository / system state: A physical and logical topology of explicit baud rates, framing parameters (data bits, parity, stop bits), address allocations (slave IDs, IP/MAC addresses), and physical port/pin configurations.
9. Attitude toward automation vs human oversight: Automated protocol analysis and signal diagnostic tools (oscilloscopes, breakout boxes, OTDRs) provide vital physical/logical telemetry, but systematic root-cause analysis requires trained engineering oversight.
10. Defining failure mode according to source: Intermittent signal corruption or complete comms failure caused by common-mode ground noise, line reflections from improper termination, or unhandled protocol timeouts/exceptions.

---

## 4. Source-backed structure

### Theme 1: Physical Layer Signaling, Differential Transceivers, and Noise Immunity
- Source: Chapter 3 (EIA-232), Chapter 4 (EIA-485), Chapter 5 (Converters), and Chapter 6 (Fiber Optics)
- Structural locator: Chapter 3: EIA-232 Overview / Chapter 4: EIA-485 Overview — Differential Receiver Sensitivity / Chapter 6: Fiber Optics Overview
- Spirit locator: Unbalanced serial connections (EIA-232) are susceptible to noise over long distances; robust industrial fieldbuses must utilize balanced differential drivers (EIA-485) or optical fiber to reject common-mode noise and provide ground isolation.

### Theme 2: Industrial Fieldbus Protocols and Deterministic Media Access
- Source: Chapter 7 (Modbus), Chapter 11 (AS-i), Chapter 12 (DeviceNet), Chapter 13 (Profibus), and Chapter 14 (Foundation Fieldbus)
- Structural locator: Chapter 7: Modbus Overview / Chapter 12: DeviceNet Overview / Chapter 13: ProfiBus PA/DP/FMS Overview / Chapter 14: Foundation Fieldbus Overview
- Spirit locator: Industrial fieldbuses employ specialized media access control (master-slave polling, token passing, or CAN non-destructive bitwise arbitration) to ensure deterministic communication for field devices and sensors.

### Theme 3: Industrial Ethernet, Full-Duplex Switching, and Infrastructure Partitioning
- Source: Chapter 15 (Industrial Ethernet Overview) and Appendix D (Miscellaneous Protocols)
- Structural locator: Chapter 15: Industrial Ethernet Overview — Switched Networks and Full-Duplex Operation / Chapter 15: Partitioning of the Network
- Spirit locator: Industrial Ethernet achieves real-time determinism by eliminating CSMA/CD collisions through full-duplex industrial switches, managed VLAN traffic segregation, and shielding (ScTP/Cat5e) to withstand EMI.

### Theme 4: System Grounding, Shielding, Line Termination, and Diagnostic Troubleshooting
- Source: Chapter 2 (Overall Methodology) and Chapter 4 (Troubleshooting EIA-485)
- Structural locator: Chapter 2: Grounding/Shielding and Noise / Chapter 4: Troubleshooting — Common Mode Voltage and Line Reflection / Chapter 12: System Grounding
- Spirit locator: Over 70% of network failures stem from physical layer defects; cable shields must be grounded at a single point only to prevent ground loops, and transmission lines must be terminated at both ends to eliminate ringing.

---

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. Primary purpose of system architecture: To establish a deterministic, electrically robust communication backbone that reliably interconnects field instruments, controllers, and supervisory HMIs without data corruption or lost cycles.
2. Acceptable vs unacceptable risk: Acceptable risk includes reduced transmission distances at lower baud rates to maintain signal integrity; unacceptable risk includes floating cable shields, missing line termination, duplicate IP/MAC addresses, and excessive stub lengths.
3. Implicit assumptions vs explicit declarations: All physical baud rates, parity/stop bit settings, device addresses, and termination resistor configurations must be explicitly declared and validated rather than relying on vendor defaults.
4. Speed/convenience vs safety/correctness: Physical signal integrity and deterministic timing unconditionally supersede convenience or high-speed bandwidth; baud rates must be scaled down if distance or noise environments threaten signal quality.
5. Human intervention vs automated enforcement: Physical diagnostic tools (breakout boxes, multimeters, TDR/OTDR) and protocol analyzers guide human engineers, while static repository checks catch bad network parameters before deployment.
6. Legacy constraints and backward compatibility: Legacy serial fieldbuses (RS-485, Modbus RTU) must be properly converted and isolated via protocol gateways and opto-isolated repeaters when integrated into modern Industrial Ethernet networks.

---

## 6. Key concepts

### 1. Differential Signaling (Balanced Transmission)
- Definition: A transmission scheme (as in EIA-485 and DeviceNet) using two wires (A/B or $V+/V-$) carrying equal and opposite voltage signals, where the receiver detects the differential voltage.
- Why it matters: Cancels out common-mode electromagnetic interference (EMI) induced equally on both conductors, enabling high data rates over 1200 meters.
- Related concepts: EIA-485, Noise Rejection, Common Mode Voltage.
- Citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / differential-signaling
- Verification status: source-backed

### 2. Line Termination & Reflection Suppression ($R_t$)
- Definition: The placement of resistors matching the characteristic impedance of the transmission cable (typically $120\,\Omega$) across the physical ends of a bus network.
- Why it matters: Prevents transmitted voltage pulses from reflecting back at cable endpoints, which causes signal distortion, ringing, and corrupted data frames.
- Related concepts: Ringing, Impedance Matching, Bus Topology.
- Citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / line-termination
- Verification status: source-backed

### 3. Single-Point Shield Grounding (Ground Loop Prevention)
- Definition: The practice of connecting a cable's overall shield to protective earth ground at exactly one physical location along a network segment.
- Why it matters: Grounding at multiple points creates circulating ground loop currents due to plant potential differences, inducing high-frequency noise onto communication conductors.
- Related concepts: Ground Loop, Cable Shielding, Opto-isolation.
- Citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / shield-grounding
- Verification status: source-backed

### 4. Idle State Line Biassing
- Definition: The arrangement of pull-up and pull-down resistors on differential signal lines to maintain a minimum differential voltage ($> 200\,\text{mV}$) when all line drivers are in high-impedance mode.
- Why it matters: Prevents floating differential lines from picking up ambient electrical noise and generating false start bits or garbled characters during transmission idle periods.
- Related concepts: High-Impedance State, EIA-485 Fail-Safe, Noise Immunity.
- Citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / line-biassing
- Verification status: source-backed

### 5. Full-Duplex Switched Industrial Ethernet
- Definition: An Ethernet network topology where every node is connected to a dedicated port on a micro-segmenting switch operating in full-duplex mode.
- Why it matters: Completely eliminates CSMA/CD packet collisions and link contention, transforming non-deterministic Ethernet into a highly deterministic industrial transport mechanism.
- Related concepts: CSMA/CD, Micro-segmentation, Industrial Ethernet.
- Citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / full-duplex-ethernet
- Verification status: source-backed

### 6. Drop Line Stub Length Limits
- Definition: The maximum allowable physical cable distance branching off a main fieldbus trunk to connect an individual node device.
- Why it matters: Excessive stub lengths create impedance discontinuities that cause severe signal reflections and bit errors at higher transmission baud rates ($> 30\,\text{kbps}$).
- Related concepts: Trunk-and-Spur Topology, DeviceNet, Profibus.
- Citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / stub-length-limits
- Verification status: source-backed

---

## 7. Practical patterns and examples

### Pattern 1: Balanced EIA-485 Multi-Drop Line with End-Only Termination & Fail-Safe Biasing
- Problem solved: Eliminates transmission line reflections and floating-line idle noise in multi-drop RS-485 serial fieldbus networks (e.g. Modbus RTU).
- Recommended approach: Connect all nodes in a daisy-chain bus topology without long T-spurs. Install $120\,\Omega$ termination resistors across terminals A and B at the two extreme physical ends of the line only. Connect $560\,\Omega$ pull-up/pull-down bias resistors at a single master node to maintain $>200\,\text{mV}$ differential offset during idle states.
- Repository-level manifestation: Hardware layout specification manifests (`topology.yaml`, `bus-config.json`) declaring explicit node daisy-chaining, termination placement flags (`terminated: true`), and master bias configurations.
- Limitations: Total segment length must not exceed 1200 meters without repeaters.
- Source citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / eia485-bus-pattern

### Pattern 2: Isolated Industrial Ethernet Segment with Screened Twisted Pair (ScTP) and VLAN Partitioning
- Problem solved: Prevents EMI noise packet corruption and broadcast storm latency from affecting critical PLC/HMI communications.
- Recommended approach: Deploy Cat5e Screened Twisted Pair (ScTP) cables with M12 or IP67 RJ-45 connectors. Connect all automation equipment to managed industrial switches configured with dedicated VLANs, separating control traffic from enterprise IT networks. Ensure full-duplex operation is enforced on all switch ports.
- Repository-level manifestation: Network deployment manifests (`switch-config.cli`, `docker-compose.yml`, `network-topology.yaml`) specifying port speeds, full-duplex mode, and isolated VLAN IDs.
- Limitations: Requires managed switches and higher cabling hardware costs compared to commercial UTP.
- Source citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / industrial-ethernet-pattern

### Pattern 3: Single-Point Shield Earthing with Opto-Isolated Network Repeaters
- Problem solved: Eliminates heavy ground loop currents and common mode voltage swings across widely separated control cabinets or buildings.
- Recommended approach: Bond the overall cable shield to protective earth ground at a single primary power supply point. At building boundaries or between distant equipment groups, insert opto-isolated EIA-485 repeaters or fiber optic transceivers to break the electrical common ground path.
- Repository-level manifestation: Cabinet wiring manifests and deployment documentation specifying isolation barrier placement and single-point earth bonding points.
- Limitations: Requires auxiliary power for opto-isolated repeaters or fiber converters.
- Source citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / single-point-grounding-pattern

---

## 8. Common failure modes and risks

### 1. Double-Earthing Cable Shields Creating Ground Loops
- Why it happens: Installers ground cable shields at both ends under the mistaken assumption that more ground connections improve noise protection.
- Early warning signs: High background noise levels, intermittent frame CRC errors, burned-out RS-485 transceiver chips, or DC currents measured in the cable shield.
- Repository evidence: Cable deployment manifests specifying multiple earth ground connections along a single shielded segment.
- Severity tendency: Critical

### 2. Missing or Duplicate Bus Line Termination Resistors
- Why it happens: Field technicians either forget to install termination resistors on long buses or turn on built-in termination switches on intermediate nodes along the bus.
- Early warning signs: Severe signal waveform ringing on oscilloscopes, high packet drop rates, or communication failures that depend on specific node positions.
- Repository evidence: Bus topology configurations lacking end-of-line termination flags or specifying $R_t$ on intermediate slave nodes.
- Severity tendency: Error

### 3. Mismatched Serial Parameters or Duplicate Network Addresses
- Why it happens: Adding a new node to an existing fieldbus or Ethernet network without verifying its baud rate, parity, stop bits, or assigned address (MACID/IP).
- Early warning signs: Bus traffic stalls, garbled response packets, duplicate address LED indicators (solid RED), or erratic node polling timeouts.
- Repository evidence: Configuration files (`network.ini`, `device.yaml`) containing duplicate IP addresses, duplicate Modbus slave IDs, or inconsistent baud rate settings across nodes.
- Severity tendency: Error

### 4. Mixed Full/Half-Duplex or Auto-Negotiation Mismatches on Industrial Switches
- Why it happens: Connecting an industrial controller with a fixed 100 Mbps full-duplex port to a switch port set to auto-negotiation, causing the switch to default to half-duplex.
- Early warning signs: Late collisions, FCS alignment errors, severe packet retransmissions, and unexpected network latency under heavy control loads.
- Repository evidence: Switch port configurations relying on unconstrained auto-negotiation when connected to fixed-speed field devices.
- Severity tendency: Error

---

## 9. Repository-audit translation

### Static checks (automated)
- Verification of repository deployment manifests for consistent serial communication parameters (baud rate, data bits, parity, stop bits).
- Audit of network configuration files to ensure unique IP addresses, MACIDs, and Modbus slave addresses across all defined endpoints.
- Detection of unconstrained auto-negotiation or half-duplex configurations on high-priority industrial control switch ports.
- Inspection of bus topology declarations for explicit end-node line termination specifications.

### Human review (operational & physical inspection)
- Visual and instrument inspection of physical ground bonding to verify single-point shield earthing and check for ground loop currents.
- Oscilloscope waveform analysis to verify signal voltage levels ($>200\,\text{mV}$ differential) and absence of transmission line ringing.
- Measurement of loop resistance and OTDR testing for optical fiber attenuation and connector splice losses.
- Physical audit of cable raceways to ensure adequate physical spacing between high-voltage motor lines and sensitive fieldbus cables.

### Safety-critical exclusions
- Physical Earth ground rod resistance measurements and high-voltage surge arrester testing must never be evaluated via static software repository checks.
- Mechanical stress, cable bending radius limits, and physical water/dust seal integrity (IP67/M12) are explicitly excluded from static automated auditing.

---

## 10. Candidate rules

### Candidate Rule 1
- Rule ID: iiot-net-001
- Title: Mismatched Serial Communication Parameters Across Fieldbus Nodes
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Mismatched baud rates, data bits, parity, or stop bit configurations between fieldbus master and slave nodes cause garbled character reception, framing errors, and complete communication failure.
- Source-backed basis: Mackay & Wright Chapter 3, Chapter 4, & Chapter 7 emphasize that consistent baud rate, parity, and stop bit configurations are absolute prerequisites for serial fieldbus communication.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Configuration files (`*.yaml`, `*.yml`, `*.json`, `*.toml`, `*.ini`, `*.conf`) containing serial port parameter declarations.
- Pass condition: All fieldbus nodes on a shared port segment declare identical baud rate, parity, and stop bit values.
- Fail condition: Conflicting baud rate (e.g. `9600` vs `19200`) or parity settings (e.g. `EVEN` vs `NONE`) exist within the same serial network configuration section.
- Exceptions or limitations: Multi-port gateways with independently configured channel interfaces.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / serial-parameter-consistency
- Source: Steve Mackay & Edwin Wright - Practical Industrial Data Networks
- Structural locator: Chapter 3: EIA-232 Overview — Basic Parameters / Chapter 7: Modbus Overview — Detailed Troubleshooting
- Spirit locator: Serial fieldbus nodes on a shared segment must strictly agree on baud rate and framing parameters.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 2
- Rule ID: iiot-net-002
- Title: Duplicate Industrial Network Address or Slave ID Allocation
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Assigning duplicate Modbus slave IDs, DeviceNet MACIDs, or IP addresses on a shared industrial network segment causes packet collisions, response bus contention, and intermittent node dropouts.
- Source-backed basis: Mackay & Wright Chapter 7, Chapter 12, & Chapter 15 identify duplicate addresses as a primary cause of network failure and solid RED diagnostic LED faults.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Manifests and configuration files (`*.yaml`, `*.yml`, `*.json`, `*.toml`, `*.ini`, `*.conf`, `*.env`) containing device address or IP allocations.
- Pass condition: All network node addresses and IP declarations within a shared network domain are unique.
- Fail condition: Two or more device nodes share the same slave ID (e.g. `slave_id: 5`) or IP address on the same subnet.
- Exceptions or limitations: Template example files explicitly marked as dummy placeholders.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / duplicate-address-prevention
- Source: Steve Mackay & Edwin Wright - Practical Industrial Data Networks
- Structural locator: Chapter 12: DeviceNet Overview — MACID Settings / Chapter 15: Industrial Ethernet — Network Setup
- Spirit locator: Node addresses on an industrial network must be globally unique within their communication domain.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 3
- Rule ID: iiot-net-003
- Title: Unbounded Auto-Negotiation or Half-Duplex Configuration on Control Switch Ports
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Allowing industrial switch ports connecting critical controllers to operate in half-duplex or unconstrained auto-negotiation introduces CSMA/CD packet collisions, latency jitter, and throughput degradation.
- Source-backed basis: Mackay & Wright Chapter 15 highlights that full-duplex operation is essential for achieving deterministic Industrial Ethernet performance and eliminating packet collisions.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Industrial switch and router configuration files (`*.cli`, `*.conf`, `*.yaml`).
- Pass condition: Switch ports connected to industrial PLCs or HMIs explicitly specify 100 Mbps or 1000 Mbps full-duplex operation.
- Fail condition: Control switch ports are configured with explicit `half-duplex` mode or lack enforced full-duplex constraints.
- Exceptions or limitations: Legacy 10Base-T half-duplex devices where full-duplex hardware is unsupported.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Source citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / full-duplex-enforcement
- Source: Steve Mackay & Edwin Wright - Practical Industrial Data Networks
- Structural locator: Chapter 15: Industrial Ethernet Overview — Switched Networks and Full-Duplex Operation
- Spirit locator: Switched industrial networks must enforce full-duplex mode to guarantee deterministic packet delivery.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 4
- Rule ID: iiot-net-004
- Title: Missing Bus Line Termination Declaration or Excessive Drop Line Stub
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Missing termination resistors ($120\,\Omega$) at bus endpoints or excessive drop stub lengths ($>3\,\text{m}$) cause severe transmission line ringing and signal distortion at high baud rates.
- Source-backed basis: Mackay & Wright Chapter 4 & Chapter 12 state that termination resistors at both ends and minimal drop stub lengths are critical to prevent signal reflections.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Network topology manifests and hardware configuration manifests (`*.yaml`, `*.json`).
- Pass condition: Topology specifies termination at extreme bus ends and restricts drop stub lengths.
- Fail condition: Bus topology manifests lack end termination declarations or define stub lengths exceeding specified protocol maximums.
- Exceptions or limitations: Low-baud rate networks ($<9600\,\text{bps}$) over short physical distances where reflections do not corrupt signals.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Static regex cannot reliably measure physical cable lengths or verify physical resistor installation without CAD/topology schema files.
- Source citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / line-termination
- Source: Steve Mackay & Edwin Wright - Practical Industrial Data Networks
- Structural locator: Chapter 4: EIA-485 Overview — Ringing and Reflections / Chapter 12: DeviceNet — Incorrect Cable Lengths
- Spirit locator: Physical bus lines must be terminated at endpoints and stub lengths minimized to suppress signal reflections.
- Verification status: source-backed

### Candidate Rule 5
- Rule ID: iiot-net-005
- Title: Ground Loop Induction and Double-Earthed Cable Shielding
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Grounding cable shields at multiple physical earth points creates high circulating ground loop currents that induce noise and damage communication interfaces.
- Source-backed basis: Mackay & Wright Chapter 2 & Chapter 4 emphasize that cable shields must be grounded at one point only to prevent ground loops.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Physical wiring schematics and plant earthing documentation.
- Pass condition: N/A (Non-static physical check).
- Fail condition: N/A (Non-static physical check).
- Exceptions or limitations: Cannot be audited statically from code or software configuration files.
- YAML readiness: blocked
- Reason if not ready: Requires physical plant inspection and electrical multimeter checks outside static repository analysis.
- Source citation: Practical Industrial Data Networks / shield-grounding
- Source: Steve Mackay & Edwin Wright - Practical Industrial Data Networks
- Structural locator: Chapter 2: Overall Methodology — Grounding/Shielding and Noise / Chapter 4: Troubleshooting EIA-485
- Spirit locator: Cable shields must be grounded at a single point only to prevent circulating ground loop currents.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment

```yaml
rules:
  - id: iiot-net-001
    title: Mismatched Serial Communication Parameters Across Fieldbus Nodes
    profile: iiot
    severity: error
    message: "Inconsistent serial parameters (baud rate, parity, or stop bits) detected across fieldbus node configuration."
    references:
      - book: "Practical Industrial Data Networks: Design, Installation and Troubleshooting"
        concept: "serial-parameter-consistency"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{yaml,yml,json,toml,ini,conf}"
      pattern: "(?i)baud_?rate\s*[:=]\s*(9600|19200|38400|57600|115200)"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_present

  - id: iiot-net-002
    title: Duplicate Industrial Network Address or Slave ID Allocation
    profile: iiot
    severity: critical
    message: "Duplicate slave ID or IP address allocation detected in industrial network configuration."
    references:
      - book: "Practical Industrial Data Networks: Design, Installation and Troubleshooting"
        concept: "duplicate-address-prevention"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{yaml,yml,json,toml,ini,conf,env}"
      pattern: "(?i)(slave_?id|macid|station_?addr)\s*[:=]\s*(\d+)"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_present

  - id: iiot-net-003
    title: Unbounded Auto-Negotiation or Half-Duplex Configuration on Control Switch Ports
    profile: iiot
    severity: warning
    message: "Industrial control switch port configured with half-duplex or unconstrained duplex mode."
    references:
      - book: "Practical Industrial Data Networks: Design, Installation and Troubleshooting"
        concept: "full-duplex-enforcement"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*.{cli,conf,yaml,yml}"
      pattern: "(?i)duplex\s+(half|auto)"
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_present
```
