# Designing Event-Driven Systems — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Designing Event-Driven Systems
- Title: Designing Event-Driven Systems
- Author(s): Ben Stopford
- Branch: dataops
- RepoScope profile: dataops
- Primary source file: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Core architecture and principles for building asynchronous, event-driven microservice ecosystems with Apache Kafka.
  - Distributed log mechanics, partition scalability, topic compaction, and consumer group rebalancing.
  - Messaging collaboration patterns: Event Collaboration, Event Sourcing, CQRS, and Memory Images.
  - Data governance, transactional atomicity (Exactly-Once processing), and backward-compatible schema evolution using Schema Registry.
- Out of scope:
  - Operating system kernel network tuning or hardware-level storage benchmarking.
  - Alternative non-Kafka streaming engines such as Apache Flink, Apache Spark, or Google Cloud Dataflow.
  - Frontend user interface frameworks and client-side web application state rendering.

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Core philosophy: Centralize an immutable stream of core business facts while decentralizing the freedom for autonomous services to act, adapt, and evolve.
2. Primary problem solved: Eliminates the tight spatial and temporal coupling created by cascading synchronous request-response chains and shared central databases.
3. Architectural posture: Asynchronous event-driven publish-subscribe architecture backed by persistent, replayable distributed logs.
4. Primary tradeoff: Accepts eventual consistency and increased asynchronous system topology in exchange for linear scalability, fault isolation, and independent team cadences.
5. Definition of success: An adaptable enterprise service estate where teams publish standardized events and deploy new microservices without cross-team deployment locks.
6. Definition of failure: Cascading system outages caused by synchronous RPC chains, data loss from non-atomic dual writes, or uncoordinated breaking schema changes.
7. Preferred abstraction level: Distributed append-only partitioned log functioning as a shared enterprise source of truth.
8. Attitude toward state: State should be internalized locally within each service as materialized views rederived directly from the immutable event stream.
9. Attitude toward schema/contracts: Event schemas are mandatory, version-controlled public APIs that must maintain backward compatibility through explicit registry contracts.
10. Operational expectation: Continuous, highly available stream processing with automated failover, partition load balancing, and rapid local state store recovery.

## 4. Source-backed structure
- Theme 1: Foundations of Streaming and the Kafka Platform
  - Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 3. Is Kafka What You Think It Is?
  - Spirit locator: Defines Apache Kafka as a distributed, replayable streaming platform that combines the scale of storage systems with the streaming capabilities of data pipelines.
- Theme 2: Event Collaboration and Loose Service Coupling
  - Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 5. Events: A Basis for Collaboration
  - Spirit locator: Replaces command-driven orchestration with event notifications and state transfers to establish receiver-driven routing and loose architectural coupling.
- Theme 3: Event Sourcing, CQRS, and Turning the Database Inside Out
  - Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 7. Event Sourcing, CQRS, and Other Stateful Patterns
  - Spirit locator: Decouples write models from read models by storing raw events immutably in the log and asynchronously populating specialized local state stores.
- Theme 4: Shared Facts and Lean Data Across Enterprise Scales
  - Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 9. Event Streams as a Shared Source of Truth
  - Spirit locator: Uses centralized immutable event topics as the single source of truth across teams while allowing services to manufacture lightweight, single-purpose data views.
- Theme 5: Consistency, Transactions, and Schema Evolution
  - Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 11. Consistency and Concurrency in Event-Driven Systems
  - Spirit locator: Enforces localized consistency via the single writer principle, transactional atomic commits, and version-controlled schema compatibility.
- Theme 6: Stateful Stream Processing and Ecosystem Architecture
  - Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 15. Building Streaming Services
  - Spirit locator: Embeds stateful stream transformations directly inside microservices using key-partitioned state stores and transactional execution streams.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. What is the fundamental worldview of this source? Software systems and enterprise data flows can be unified into a single distributed database turned inside out, where immutable event streams represent shared business history and services materialize local views for processing logic.
2. What does this source consider the greatest architectural risk? Direct coupling via synchronous REST/RPC calls or shared database access across service boundaries, which creates fragile, interconnected dependency chains prone to widespread outages.
3. How does this source view centralized vs decentralized control? Centralize the immutable event stream and storage infrastructure as a shared source of truth, but decentralize service implementation, data processing, and deployment freedom.
4. How does this source treat data ownership and state? Microservices must fully encapsulate their internal state behind service boundaries, consuming public event streams and building private, queryable materialized views.
5. What is the source's attitude toward schema changes and evolution? Schema change is an inevitable characteristic of aging software systems; non-backward-compatible updates must be carefully managed using backward-compatible Avro contracts or dual-topic upgrade windows.
6. How should failures be handled according to this source? Systems must expect continuous failure and recover automatically via log replayability, single-writer partition guarantees, idempotent producers, and transactional atomic commits rather than distributed locks.

## 6. Key concepts
1. Replayable Partitioned Log
   - Definition: A distributed, append-only, ordered sequence of records stored sequentially on disk and partitioned across cluster nodes for scale and fault tolerance.
   - Why it matters: Delivers high sequential disk throughput, decouples producer write performance from consumer read speed, and allows services to rewind and replay historic data.
   - Related concepts: Kafka Broker, Compacted Topics, Consumer Group Offsets.
   - Citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
2. Event Collaboration Pattern
   - Definition: An architectural pattern where autonomous services communicate by publishing facts (events) describing business state changes without instructing downstream services what action to take.
   - Why it matters: Flips routing responsibility to the receiver, eliminating direct RPC service dependencies and enabling pluggable system capabilities.
   - Related concepts: Receiver-Driven Routing, State Transfer, Loose Coupling.
   - Citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
3. Single Writer Principle
   - Definition: A design rule stating that only a single designated service instance or process is permitted to write state transitions for a given entity or partition key.
   - Why it matters: Prevents concurrent write collisions and race conditions without relying on expensive distributed locks.
   - Related concepts: Command/Entity Topic Split, Key-Based Partitioning, Optimistic Concurrency.
   - Citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
4. Event Sourcing & CQRS
   - Definition: Event Sourcing persists state changes as an immutable sequence of events; CQRS separates the write model (event log) from query-optimized local read models.
   - Why it matters: Ensures complete historical auditability, supports total state rederivation, and allows read and write paths to scale independently.
   - Related concepts: Materialized Views, Memory Images, Change Data Capture.
   - Citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
5. Stateful Stream Processing & State Stores
   - Definition: Stream processing APIs that embed localized disk-resident data tables inside microservice processes, backed durably by Kafka changelog topics.
   - Why it matters: Eliminates external database lookups during stream processing, executing high-throughput local joins and aggregations at low latency.
   - Related concepts: Kafka Streams, KTable, RocksDB, Standby Replicas.
   - Citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
6. Dual Schema Upgrade Window
   - Definition: A schema migration strategy where data in a new non-backward-compatible schema is routed to a parallel topic alongside a stream transformer bridging old and new formats during a migration period.
   - Why it matters: Permits independent microservice teams to upgrade producers and consumers without forcing synchronized, company-wide releases.
   - Related concepts: Schema Registry, Avro Backward Compatibility, Single Message Transforms.
   - Citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
7. Transactions API (Atomic Commits)
   - Definition: A Kafka framework feature allowing messages across multiple topics and state stores to be produced and committed as a single atomic unit.
   - Why it matters: Guarantees exactly-once processing semantics across read-process-write stream cycles, eliminating duplicates and preventing partial state failures.
   - Related concepts: Exactly-Once Semantics, Transaction Coordinator, Marker Messages.
   - Citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
1. Command / Entity Topic Split
   - Problem solved: Enforces the Single Writer Principle while allowing external services to submit state update requests asynchronously.
   - Recommended approach: Create a public `Commands` topic writable by any client service, and a restricted `Entities` topic writable exclusively by the owning validation service.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Topic configuration manifests defining `order-commands` with open producer access and `orders` restricted to the Order Service identity via ACLs.
   - Limitations: Introduces asynchronous latency between command submission and final entity state confirmation.
   - Source citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
2. Change Data Capture (CDC) Outbox Integration
   - Problem solved: Unlocks database mutations from legacy applications and streams them into event topics without risking dual-write inconsistencies.
   - Recommended approach: Utilize a CDC connector (e.g., Debezium via Kafka Connect) to tail the database transaction log and publish changes directly into Kafka event streams.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Kafka Connect JSON or YAML configuration manifests defining database log-tailing parameters and table-to-topic mappings.
   - Limitations: Dependent on internal database transaction log formats and requires post-processing transforms to standardize public payloads.
   - Source citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
3. Dual-Topic Down-Converter (Dual Schema Upgrade Window)
   - Problem solved: Manages breaking, non-backward-compatible event schema changes across independently updated microservice applications.
   - Recommended approach: Publish new schema events to a `v2` topic, run a Kafka Streams down-converter microservice to populate the `v1` topic, and retire `v1` once all consumers migrate.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Kafka Streams microservice codebase or deployment manifest containing a stream transformer bridging `orders-v2` and `orders-v1`.
   - Limitations: Temporarily duplicates topic storage footprint and network bandwidth during the co-existence window.
   - Source citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
4. Materialized Event-Sourced View via Kafka Streams KTable
   - Problem solved: Delivers rapid, queryable read endpoints for microservices without making remote HTTP calls or hitting shared databases.
   - Recommended approach: Consume an event stream in Kafka Streams, project records into a local RocksDB state store (`KTable`), and expose it over local REST endpoints.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Application Java/Scala source code instantiating a `KTable` from an input topic and binding interactive query endpoints.
   - Limitations: Requires local disk allocation for state store persistence and bootstrapping time when initializing a cold node.
   - Source citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
1. Dual-Write Non-Atomic Inconsistency
   - Why it happens: A service updates its local database and publishes an event to Kafka in separate un-transactional operations, encountering network failure between calls.
   - Early warning signs: Unexplained data inconsistencies between service databases and downstream event streams; missing notification events despite database records existing.
   - Repository evidence: Application code executing database commits followed by independent `kafkaProducer.send()` invocations without transactional outbox or CDC patterns.
   - Severity tendency: critical
2. Uncontrolled Breaking Schema Changes
   - Why it happens: A producer application alters an event payload format (e.g., deleting fields or renaming keys) without verifying backward compatibility.
   - Early warning signs: Spikes in consumer deserialization exceptions, poison-pill messages halting partition processing, and crashing downstream microservices.
   - Repository evidence: Missing Schema Registry plugin integration in build pipelines and lack of Avro/Protobuf compatibility tasks in CI workflows.
   - Severity tendency: critical
3. Shared Database Access Across Service Boundaries
   - Why it happens: Developers bypass event streams and query or mutate another service's internal database directly to save initial implementation effort.
   - Early warning signs: Schema refactoring in one service breaking unrelated downstream applications; database lock contention and unpredictable performance degradation.
   - Repository evidence: Database connection strings sharing credentials or schemas across multiple repository microservices.
   - Severity tendency: error
4. Unbounded Local State Rebuild Lag
   - Why it happens: A stateful stream processing node crashes and must rebuild its multi-gigabyte local state store from an uncompacted, full-history topic.
   - Early warning signs: Extended service cold-start delays, consumer group rebalance timeouts, and high consumer lag during node deployment.
   - Repository evidence: State store changelog topics configured with retention-based deletion policy (`cleanup.policy=delete`) instead of compaction (`cleanup.policy=compact`).
   - Severity tendency: warning

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated)
- Audit build files and CI pipelines for Schema Registry compatibility plugins (e.g., `schema-registry-maven-plugin` or Gradle Avro plugins).
- Check repository configuration for Kafka producer settings enforcing idempotence (`enable.idempotence=true`) and transactional IDs during read-process-write loops.
- Verify that event schema definition files (e.g., `.avsc`, `.proto`, `.json`) exist in repository source paths whenever event producers or consumers are present.
- Scan application configuration for Dead-Letter Queue (DLQ) topic references and error-handler recovery definitions in consumer classes.
- Detect pattern matches for hardcoded topic name strings or shared cross-service database credentials in configuration manifests.

### Human review (contextual)
- Evaluate whether microservice bounded context boundaries align with domain business entities rather than arbitrary technical layers.
- Assess architectural trade-offs when selecting between lightweight Event Notification versus replicated Event-Driven State Transfer.
- Review operational runbooks and disaster recovery strategy for rebuilding large stateful stream processing local state stores.
- Validate team communication channels and pull-request sign-off workflows for proposed event schema modifications.

## 10. Candidate rules
- Rule ID: dataops-schema-001
- Title: Kafka Event Schema Compatibility Verification
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Event-driven systems rely on shared event contracts. Modifying event schemas without compatibility verification causes deserialization errors and breaks downstream consumers.
- Source-backed basis: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf emphasizes build-time and runtime schema validation via Confluent Schema Registry to enforce backward compatibility across independently deployed services.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Build definition files (`pom.xml`, `build.gradle`), CI workflow YAMLs, or Schema Registry plugin configs.
- Pass condition: CI workflow or build file includes an automated task executing schema compatibility validation against Schema Registry before merging code.
- Fail condition: Repository defines Avro or Protobuf event schemas but includes no automated schema compatibility test step in build or CI workflows.
- Exceptions or limitations: Applies only to repositories that produce or consume structured, schema-governed event streams.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A
- Source citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 13. Evolving Schemas and Data over Time
- Spirit locator: Automated build-time and runtime compatibility checks ensure independent team releases cannot break downstream message deserialization.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-stream-001
- Title: Idempotent Producer Configuration Enforcer
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Non-idempotent event producers create duplicate messages during network retries, causing state corruption in downstream stream processing pipelines.
- Source-backed basis: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf specifies that enabling producer idempotence and transactional IDs ensures exactly-once delivery guarantees across network retries and atomic write cycles.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Kafka producer properties files (`application.properties`, `application.yml`, code constants).
- Pass condition: Producer configuration explicitly specifies `enable.idempotence=true` or defines a valid `transactional.id`.
- Fail condition: Kafka producer configuration sets `enable.idempotence=false` or omits idempotence settings when retries are enabled.
- Exceptions or limitations: Applies to Kafka Java, Python, or Go producer configurations within repository codebases.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A
- Source citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 12. Transactions, but Not as We Know Them
- Spirit locator: Idempotent producer configurations prevent message duplication at the broker level during network retries.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-dlq-001
- Title: Dead-Letter Queue Error Recovery Configuration
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Unhandleable or malformed poison-pill records cause infinite retry loops, halting partition consumption for the entire consumer group.
- Source-backed basis: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf highlights the necessity of routing unparseable or failed messages to error topics/DLQs to preserve consumer group progress.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Kafka consumer listener code, error handler configuration files, or stream framework properties.
- Pass condition: Consumer configuration or code explicitly defines a DeadLetterPublishingRecoverer or DLQ error topic handler.
- Fail condition: Consumer error handling configuration uses infinite retry loops without a DLQ or fallback routing step.
- Exceptions or limitations: Applies to stream consumer service repositories.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A
- Source citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 13. Evolving Schemas and Data over Time
- Spirit locator: Redirecting unprocessable messages to a dedicated dead-letter queue isolates bad data while preserving stream processing continuity.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-event-001
- Title: Single Writer Boundary Enforcers for Domain Topics
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Allowing multiple services to write directly to an entity topic violates the Single Writer Principle, causing race conditions and inconsistent state.
- Source-backed basis: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf details the Single Writer Principle, mandating that entity topics have a single owning service while other services write to command topics.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Topic ACL manifests, Kafka IaC definitions (Terraform/Kubernetes CRDs), or topic routing configurations.
- Pass condition: Infrastructure-as-code manifests restrict write access for entity topics to a single service principal.
- Fail condition: Infrastructure-as-code grants write access to multiple distinct service roles for the same domain entity topic.
- Exceptions or limitations: Requires static inspection of IaC manifests or Kafka ACL security manifests within the repository.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Validating multi-service ACL assignments requires semantic parsing of external infrastructure-as-code files beyond simple pattern matching.
- Source citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 11. Consistency and Concurrency in Event-Driven Systems
- Spirit locator: Restricting write privileges to a single owning service prevents non-deterministic state mutations on entity topics.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-replay-001
- Title: Compacted Topic Policy for State Store Changelogs
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Setting retention-based deletion on state store changelog topics leads to data loss when rebuilding local state stores from offset zero.
- Source-backed basis: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf notes that state store changelog topics and key-value state backing topics must use topic compaction (`cleanup.policy=compact`) to retain the latest value per key indefinitely.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Kafka topic configuration files, IaC manifests, or Kafka Streams state store definitions.
- Pass condition: Topic configuration manifests for state store changelogs specify `cleanup.policy=compact`.
- Fail condition: Topic configuration sets `cleanup.policy=delete` for a topic backing a state store or materialized view.
- Exceptions or limitations: Applies to topic creation manifests and stream processing configuration.
- YAML readiness: blocked
- Reason if not ready: Current validator lacks semantic property graph analysis to correlate a stream processing state store with its corresponding infrastructure topic manifest.
- Source citation: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Source: Designing-Event-Driven-Systems.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 4. Beyond Messaging: An Overview of the Kafka Broker
- Spirit locator: Topic compaction preserves the latest state for every key, enabling efficient and complete state store recovery.
- Verification status: source-backed

# Streaming Systems — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Streaming Systems
- Title: Streaming Systems: The What, Where, When, and How of Large-Scale Data Processing
- Author(s): Tyler Akidau, Slava Chernyak, Reuven Lax
- Branch: dataops
- RepoScope profile: dataops
- Primary source file: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How of Large-Scale Data Processing.pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Unified batch and stream processing semantics defined by the Beam Model across four questions: What, Where, When, and How.
  - Distinction between Event Time and Processing Time domains, including event-time skew and processing-time lag.
  - Temporal progress metrics via Watermarks (perfect vs. heuristic, source watermarks, input/output watermark propagation, percentile watermarks, and processing-time watermarks).
  - Event-time windowing strategies (fixed, sliding, sessions, dynamic merging windows, and custom windowing).
  - Triggering mechanics (early, on-time, and late firings) and accumulation modes (discarding, accumulating, accumulating and retracting).
  - Window lifetime bounds, allowed lateness, and state garbage collection.
  - End-to-end exactly-once processing guarantees, shuffle deduplication catalog with Bloom filters, deterministic checkpointing, and side-effect sink idempotency.
  - General theory of Stream and Table Relativity (tables as data at rest, streams as data in motion) and time-varying relations in Streaming SQL.
  - Low-level persistent state, state specs, timers, and dynamic session merging with retractions.
- Out of scope:
  - Low-level network interface hardware driver configuration, TCP socket tuning, or physical NIC benchmarking.
  - Frontend web framework user interface rendering or browser DOM state management.
  - Storage engine physical block layout optimization on bare-metal disk controllers.

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Core philosophy: Unbounded out-of-order data processing must be decoupled into four distinct questions: what is computed, where in event time it is windowed, when in processing time it is materialized, and how results relate.
2. Primary problem solved: Replaces fragile, dual-codebase Lambda Architectures by providing unified, strongly consistent, and low-latency processing over unbounded, unordered data streams.
3. Architectural posture: Unified dataflow pipeline model backed by replayable source logs, deterministic state checkpointing, and temporal progress metrics.
4. Primary tradeoff: Accepts increased state tracking storage overhead and complex retraction semantics in exchange for absolute processing correctness, low latency, and event-time accuracy.
5. Definition of success: A single streaming codebase that produces accurate, windowed, and eventually complete results regardless of network delay, out-of-order delivery, or worker crash retries.
6. Definition of failure: Data loss or double-counting from non-idempotent retries, silent dropping of un-windowed late events, or reliance on unreliable processing-time clocks for event-time business logic.
7. Preferred abstraction level: Declarative pipeline transformations operating on immutable event streams and materialized state tables (`PCollection` and `PTransform` abstractions).
8. Attitude toward state: State is an essential first-class primitive in stream processing that must be persistently checkpointed, key-partitioned, and garbage-collected via explicit allowed lateness bounds.
9. Attitude toward schema/contracts: Event schemas and record payload structures must be explicitly defined and held stable across shuffle boundaries to preserve deterministic replay and deduplication.
10. Operational expectation: Continuous, fault-tolerant execution where machine crashes trigger automatic state rollbacks or deduplicated shuffle retries without human intervention.

## 4. Source-backed structure
- Theme 1: Foundations of Streaming and Time Domains
  - Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 1. Streaming 101
  - Spirit locator: Establishes the distinction between event time and processing time, demonstrating that modern streaming engines with strong consistency can supersede legacy batch engines and Lambda Architectures.
- Theme 2: The Core Beam Model Framework (What, Where, When, How)
  - Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 2. The What, Where, When, and How of Data Processing
  - Spirit locator: Unbundles data processing into four distinct dimensions: transformations (what), event-time windowing (where), trigger materialization with watermarks (when), and accumulation modes (how).
- Theme 3: Watermarks and Temporal Progress Tracking
  - Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 3. Watermarks
  - Spirit locator: Formalizes watermarks as monotonically increasing metrics of input completeness, tracing source creation, stage propagation, and percentile/processing-time variants.
- Theme 4: Advanced Windowing, Triggers, and Sessions
  - Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 4. Advanced Windowing
  - Spirit locator: Explores dynamic session window merging, processing-time windowing via ingress time, and custom window assignment strategies.
- Theme 5: End-to-End Exactly-Once Semantics and Side Effects
  - Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 5. Exactly-Once and Side Effects
  - Spirit locator: Details mechanisms for exactly-once shuffle deduplication, Bloom filter memory optimization, deterministic checkpointing, and idempotent side-effect sinks.
- Theme 6: General Theory of Stream and Table Relativity
  - Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 6. Streams and Tables
  - Spirit locator: Synthesizes stream/table duality into a comprehensive theory where tables represent data at rest (grouping) and streams represent data in motion (ungrouping triggers).
- Theme 7: Persistent State and Timers
  - Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 7. The Practicalities of Persistent State
  - Spirit locator: Defines key-scoped state abstractions (`ValueState`, `BagState`) and event-time timers to enable flexible stateful calculations like advertising conversion attribution.
- Theme 8: Streaming SQL and Time-Varying Relations
  - Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
  - Structural locator: Chapter 8. Streaming SQL
  - Spirit locator: Extends relational algebra to time-varying relations (TVRs), defining stream/table selection keywords and SQL triggering extensions.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. What is the fundamental worldview of this source? All data processing can be unified under a single model where batch is merely a subset of streaming, and unbounded streams can be accurately processed by separating transformation logic from time semantics and state materialization.
2. What does this source consider the greatest architectural risk? Relying on processing time or best-effort streaming systems for event-time business logic, or deploying dual-codebase Lambda Architectures that introduce inconsistency, maintenance friction, and delayed correctness.
3. How does this source view centralized vs decentralized control? Centralize temporal progress metrics (watermarks) and execution graph management, but decentralize state processing across key-partitioned worker nodes that maintain independent local state stores.
4. How does this source treat data ownership and state? State is a fundamental primitive tied strictly to keys and windows; it must be stored durably, updated atomically during pipeline processing, and garbage-collected once watermark progress exceeds allowed lateness horizons.
5. What is the source's attitude toward schema changes and evolution? Record structures and serialization contracts must remain deterministic across shuffle and checkpoint boundaries, ensuring that replayable sources and deduplication catalogs can correctly identify duplicate payloads.
6. How should failures be handled according to this source? Systems must implement strongly consistent checkpointing, upstream backup retries, and receiver deduplication so that worker failures trigger transparent recovery without record loss or double counting.

## 6. Key concepts
1. Event Time versus Processing Time
   - Definition: Event time is the time at which an event actually occurred on the client/source device; processing time is the clock time at which the processing engine observes the event.
   - Why it matters: Real-world network delays and offline client caching create variable event-time skew, making processing time unreliable for event-timestamped business logic like billing or sessions.
   - Related concepts: Event-Time Skew, Processing-Time Lag, Ingress Timestamping.
   - Citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
2. Watermark (Input and Output Watermark)
   - Definition: A monotonically increasing metric of input completeness in the event-time domain, asserting that no further events with timestamps earlier than time X will be observed.
   - Why it matters: Provides a principled mechanism for determining window completeness, allowing the engine to close windows, emit on-time panes, and detect late data.
   - Related concepts: Perfect Watermark, Heuristic Watermark, Watermark Lag, Output Timestamp.
   - Citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
3. Triggers & Panes (Early, On-Time, Late Firings)
   - Definition: Declarative triggers specify when in processing time output results for a window are materialized as panes; firings are categorized as early (speculative), on-time (watermark completion), or late (late data updates).
   - Why it matters: Solves the latency vs. correctness dilemma by emitting rapid speculative results before window completion while still finalizing exact outputs once the watermark advances.
   - Related concepts: Repeated Update Triggers, Watermark Triggers, Panes, Speculative Results.
   - Citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
4. Accumulation Modes (Discarding, Accumulating, Retracting)
   - Definition: Specifies how multiple materialized output panes for the same window relate: discarding drops state after firing, accumulating retains state to emit updated totals, and accumulating & retracting emits explicit retractions for prior outputs alongside new values.
   - Why it matters: Allows downstream sinks or services to correctly incorporate updated window results, especially when downstream stores require deltas or explicit key deletions.
   - Related concepts: Retractions, Delta Mode, Revisions, Dynamic Window Merging.
   - Citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
5. Exactly-Once Shuffle Deduplication
   - Definition: An engine mechanism that tags every shuffled record with a unique identifier and checks an in-memory or disk-backed catalog (accelerated by time-bounded Bloom filters) to drop duplicate records sent via upstream retries.
   - Why it matters: Guarantees that network retries or worker crashes never cause record duplication or distorted aggregation statistics.
   - Related concepts: Upstream Backup, Bloom Filters, Catalog Garbage Collection, Deterministic Checkpointing.
   - Citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
6. General Theory of Stream and Table Relativity
   - Definition: The fundamental duality where tables represent data at rest (resulting from grouping operations) and streams represent data in motion (resulting from ungrouping trigger operations).
   - Why it matters: Unifies relational database concepts, MapReduce batch jobs, and real-time streaming topologies into a single coherent framework.
   - Related concepts: Time-Varying Relations (TVRs), Grouping Operations, Ungrouping Operations, Materialized Views.
   - Citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed
7. Allowed Lateness & State Garbage Collection
   - Definition: A configurable event-time duration defining how long after the watermark passes a window's end the engine must retain window state to accept late-arriving events.
   - Why it matters: Prevents infinite disk/memory growth in long-running streaming pipelines by bounding state retention and establishing an explicit horizon for dropping late records.
   - Related concepts: Window Expiry, State GC, Late Data Dropping, Horizon.
   - Citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
   - Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
1. Early / On-Time / Late Triggering Pattern
   - Problem solved: Eliminates output latency for long windows while maintaining correctness in the presence of heuristic watermark delays and late-arriving records.
   - Recommended approach: Configure windows with an `AfterWatermark()` trigger combined with periodic early firings (e.g., aligned processing-time delay) and per-record late firings bounded by allowed lateness.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Pipeline Java/Python code specifying `.triggering(AfterWatermark().withEarlyFirings(...).withLateFirings(...)).withAllowedLateness(...)`.
   - Limitations: Increases output messaging volume downstream due to multiple speculative and late pane firings.
   - Source citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
2. Two-Phase Reshuffle for Non-Idempotent External Sinks
   - Problem solved: Prevents duplicate side effects or partial output writes when executing non-idempotent operations (such as external HTTP API calls or database inserts) during worker retries.
   - Recommended approach: Insert a `Reshuffle` transform (or `@RequiresStableInput` annotation) between the stateful processing transform and the side-effect sink transform to enforce a deterministic barrier.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Pipeline code applying `Reshuffle.of()` prior to sink invocation, or annotated custom `DoFn` classes with `@RequiresStableInput`.
   - Limitations: Introduces a shuffle stage serialization boundary, incurring minor I/O overhead.
   - Source citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
3. Dynamic Session Merging with Retractions
   - Problem solved: Accurately tracks user activity session windows across out-of-order event streams where new records bridge previously separate session windows.
   - Recommended approach: Utilize session windowing with `accumulatingAndRetractingFiredPanes()` mode to automatically merge overlapping proto-sessions and emit explicit undo/retraction records for old session keys.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Stream processing code configuring `.apply(Window.into(Sessions.withGapDuration(...)).accumulatingAndRetractingFiredPanes())`.
   - Limitations: Downstream storage systems must be capable of processing retraction/tombstone records to delete previous state entries.
   - Source citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
1. Missing Allowed Lateness Leading to Unbounded Memory Growth
   - Why it happens: Developers instantiate stateful event-time windows on unbounded streams without configuring an explicit allowed lateness or state GC horizon.
   - Early warning signs: Out-of-memory errors on streaming worker nodes, monotonically increasing disk usage for local state stores, and degraded checkpointing speeds.
   - Repository evidence: Window definitions in pipeline code using custom or heuristic triggers without invoking `.withAllowedLateness(...)` or setting global state expiry.
   - Severity tendency: critical
2. Non-Idempotent Side Effects in Sink Transforms
   - Why it happens: Side-effect code (e.g., direct REST calls, email notifications, database appends) is executed inside `DoFn` transforms without idempotent key deduplication or stable input reshuffling.
   - Early warning signs: Duplicate records in external databases, repeated third-party API invocations during worker crash recoveries, and inconsistent downstream counts.
   - Repository evidence: `DoFn` implementation classes making external un-checkpointed network writes without `@RequiresStableInput` or preceding `Reshuffle` steps.
   - Severity tendency: critical
3. Unintended Late Data Loss from Overly Aggressive Watermarks
   - Why it happens: Heuristic source watermark algorithms advance too quickly due to misconfigured estimation bounds, misclassifying valid out-of-order events as late data.
   - Early warning signs: Spikes in dropped-data metrics (`droppedDueToLateness`), unexpected zero counts in window aggregations, and disparity between batch and streaming run outputs.
   - Repository evidence: Custom source watermark generator configurations setting zero estimation bands or tight out-of-order delays on unpredictable network sources.
   - Severity tendency: error
4. Silent Processing-Time Fallback for Event-Time Business Logic
   - Why it happens: Developers default to processing-time windowing or system ingress time for business metrics that strictly depend on event timestamps.
   - Early warning signs: Window aggregation metrics fluctuating wildly based on network congestion or batch ingestion schedules rather than actual user activity.
   - Repository evidence: Code explicitly using `ProcessingTime.now()` or setting global processing-time windowing triggers for time-sensitive domain calculations.
   - Severity tendency: error

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated)
- Audit pipeline source code for event-time window definitions and verify that `withAllowedLateness(...)` is explicitly configured on unbounded stream sources.
- Verify that custom sink transforms making external RPCs/writes utilize `Reshuffle` or carry the `@RequiresStableInput` annotation.
- Scan pipeline configuration manifests for explicit checkpointing interval settings (`enableCheckpointing`, `checkpointInterval`) and state backend persistence parameters.
- Inspect source connector definitions to ensure watermark estimation bands and out-of-order thresholds are set in configuration properties.
- Check build definitions and pipeline options classes for required serializable schema and coder registrations on custom PCollection payloads.

### Human review (contextual)
- Evaluate whether business domain window choices (e.g., fixed vs. sliding vs. sessions) correctly reflect user activity patterns.
- Review downstream database compatibility with retraction records when choosing `accumulatingAndRetractingFiredPanes()` mode.
- Assess operational runbooks for handling sustained upstream pipeline backpressure and dead-letter queue (DLQ) processing.
- Validate trade-offs between trigger firing frequency, speculative update latency, and downstream system throughput cost.

## 10. Candidate rules
- Rule ID: dataops-watermark-001
- Title: Event-Time Window Allowed Lateness Enforcer
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Unbounded event-time windows without explicit allowed lateness bounds retain state indefinitely, leading to worker memory exhaustion and pipeline crashes.
- Source-backed basis: Streaming Systems emphasizes that real-world out-of-order processing systems must define an explicit allowed lateness horizon to bound window lifetimes and garbage-collect persistent state.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Stream processing pipeline source files (`.java`, `.py`, `.scala`) defining event-time windowing transforms (`Window.into(...)`).
- Pass condition: Windowing pipeline code explicitly calls `withAllowedLateness` or specifies a valid bounded lateness duration.
- Fail condition: Windowing transform is defined on an unbounded stream without configuring allowed lateness or state expiry parameters.
- Exceptions or limitations: Applies to stateful event-time windowing pipelines operating on unbounded input streams.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A
- Source citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 2. The What, Where, When, and How of Data Processing
- Spirit locator: Bounding allowed lateness establishes a clear horizon for state garbage collection, preventing infinite memory usage in long-running pipelines.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-sink-001
- Title: Stable Input Enforcer for Non-Idempotent Side-Effect Sinks
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Executing non-idempotent side effects during pipeline transforms without a stable input barrier causes duplicate operations during worker retries.
- Source-backed basis: Streaming Systems specifies that side-effect transforms must be protected by a `Reshuffle` stage or `@RequiresStableInput` annotation to ensure input stability across retries.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Custom sink DoFn classes and pipeline transformation chains.
- Pass condition: Sink DoFn classes making external calls are annotated with `@RequiresStableInput` or preceded by a `Reshuffle.of()` transform.
- Fail condition: Custom sink DoFn executes external writes without stable input annotations or shuffle barriers.
- Exceptions or limitations: Applies to custom non-idempotent side-effect sinks in stream processing codebases.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A
- Source citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 5. Exactly-Once and Side Effects
- Spirit locator: Stable input barriers prevent non-deterministic user code from generating duplicate external side effects during retry attempts.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-checkpoint-001
- Title: Streaming Pipeline Checkpoint Interval Configuration
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Unbounded streaming jobs running without active checkpointing cannot guarantee fault tolerance or state recovery during worker failure.
- Source-backed basis: Streaming Systems highlights that strong consistency and exactly-once processing rely on periodic, durable state checkpointing.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Pipeline configuration files (`application.properties`, `application.yml`, pipeline options source code).
- Pass condition: Pipeline execution options explicitly enable state checkpointing and specify a positive checkpointing interval.
- Fail condition: Streaming pipeline options explicitly disable checkpointing or omit checkpointing configuration for stateful jobs.
- Exceptions or limitations: Applies to streaming job configuration files and execution runner option setup.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A
- Source citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 1. Streaming 101
- Spirit locator: Consistent checkpointing of persistent state is a fundamental prerequisite for correctness and failure recovery in streaming systems.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-trigger-001
- Title: Retraction Mode Verification for Session Windows
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Merging session windows without retraction support causes stale session entries to remain in downstream state stores.
- Source-backed basis: Streaming Systems notes that merging dynamic session windows requires `accumulatingAndRetractingFiredPanes()` to emit explicit retractions for superseded sessions.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Session window definition code and accumulation mode settings.
- Pass condition: Session window definition includes retraction accumulation mode when output is written to key-value sinks.
- Fail condition: Session windowing uses simple accumulation without retractions when writing to downstream key-value stores.
- Exceptions or limitations: Requires semantic analysis of downstream sink types.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Validating compatibility between accumulation mode and downstream sink type requires semantic dataflow graph analysis beyond simple regex checks.
- Source citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 4. Advanced Windowing
- Spirit locator: Explicit retractions allow downstream stores to erase superseded session records when dynamic windows merge.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-watermark-lag-001
- Title: Pub/Sub Source Estimation Band Configuration
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Setting an estimation band of zero on out-of-order Pub/Sub streams causes premature watermark advancement and excessive late data dropping.
- Source-backed basis: Streaming Systems details how source watermarks for Pub/Sub require an adequate estimation band (e.g., 10 seconds) to account for out-of-order arrival without discarding data.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Source reader configuration properties and Pub/Sub watermark setup code.
- Pass condition: Source watermark properties set a non-zero estimation band duration for out-of-order stream readers.
- Fail condition: Source watermark configuration sets an estimation band of zero on non-strictly-ordered stream sources.
- Exceptions or limitations: Applies to Pub/Sub source reader configuration.
- YAML readiness: blocked
- Reason if not ready: Current static validator lacks property graph capabilities to differentiate ordered versus out-of-order source topic topologies.
- Source citation: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Source: Streaming Systems- The What, Where, When, and How.pdf
- Structural locator: Chapter 3. Watermarks
- Spirit locator: Source estimation bands provide a necessary safety margin for heuristic watermarks to accommodate out-of-order events.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment
```yaml
rules:
  - id: dataops-watermark-001
    profile: dataops
    severity: critical
    message: "Event-time windowing transform missing explicit allowed lateness configuration for state garbage collection."
    references:
      - book: "Streaming Systems"
        concept: "allowed-lateness"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*Pipeline*.java"
      pattern: 'withAllowedLateness|allowed_lateness'
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_absent
      message: "Pipeline windowing transform does not specify withAllowedLateness."

  - id: dataops-sink-001
    profile: dataops
    severity: critical
    message: "Side-effect sink transform missing @RequiresStableInput annotation or Reshuffle barrier."
    references:
      - book: "Streaming Systems"
        concept: "stable-input-sink"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/*Sink*.java"
      pattern: '@RequiresStableInput|Reshuffle'
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_absent
      message: "Custom sink DoFn lacks @RequiresStableInput or Reshuffle barrier."

  - id: dataops-checkpoint-001
    profile: dataops
    severity: error
    message: "Streaming job configuration missing active state checkpointing setup."
    references:
      - book: "Streaming Systems"
        concept: "checkpointing"
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      file_glob: "**/application*.yml"
      pattern: 'enableCheckpointing:\s*true|checkpointInterval'
      must_exist: true
      error_when: pattern_absent
      message: "Streaming application configuration does not enable state checkpointing."
```

===== FILE: docs/notes/industrial-ml/04-industrial-mlops/industrial-machine-learning.md =====
# RepoScope Knowledge Note: Industrial Machine Learning (Andreas François Vermeulen, 2020)

## 1. Source Identity

- **Title:** Industrial Machine Learning
* **Source Title:** *Industrial Machine Learning: Using Artificial Intelligence as a Transformational Disruptor* 
* **Author:** Andreas François Vermeulen
* **Primary Focus:** Industrialized Artificial Intelligence (IAI), mechatronics engineering, SCADA/IoT system integration, the Rapid Information Framework (RIF), data lake architecture, and regulatory/GDPR compliance for automated decision systems.
* **RepoScope Branch:** Industrial MLOps, Governance, and Operational Technology (OT) Boundaries.

---

## 2. Executive Summary & Core Philosophy
Industrial Machine Learning (IML) transforms classical data science experiments into repeatable, scalable, and audit-compliant production workflows. The foundational thesis asserts that **machine learning is the capability of systems to learn without explicitly providing rules**, but its industrial value is realized only when algorithms are strictly coupled with business terminology, data lineage, and mechatronic control boundaries. 

The text introduces the **Rapid Information Framework (RIF)** and the **R-A-P-T-O-R Engine** (Retrieve, Assess, Process, Transform, Organise, Report), bridging the gap between raw data lakes, structured data vaults, and real-time operational technology (OT) systems.

---

## 3. Key Concepts & Definitions
* **R-A-P-T-O-R Engine:** A six-stage data processing pipeline (Retrieve raw feeds, Assess quality/metadata, Process into core entities, Transform into data vaults, Organise into consumer marts, Report insights).
* **Six Data Lake Zones:** Structural partitioning covering Workspace, Raw Zone (100), Structured Zone (200), Curated Zone (300), Consumer Zone, and Analytics Zone (sandbox).
* **In-Stream Analytics (ISA) & Closed-Loop Control:** Near real-time processing of streaming telemetry that generates automated advisory or control actions and feeds outcome metrics back into the learning loop.
* **Mechatronics & Control Engineering:** The multidisciplinary convergence of mechanical, electrical, software, and control engineering, where ML operates under physical constraints (e.g., dead weight, tow weight, actuator response times).
* **Advanced Evaluation Metrics:** Beyond accuracy, industrial models rely on **Bookmaker Informedness (BM)** ($TPR + TNR - 1$), **Markedness (MK)** ($PPV + NPV - 1$), and **Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC)** to account for extreme class imbalances.
* **Data Vault Modeling (Hubs, Links, Satellites):** Schema-on-write historical storage structures that preserve long-term data provenance and auditability.
* **Human-in-the-Loop (HITL):** A mandatory operational protocol where ambiguous predictions or high-risk edge cases are routed to human operators, whose decisions retrain the model.

---

## 4. Architectural & Operational Patterns

### Pattern 1: R-A-P-T-O-R Pipeline Architecture
1. **Retrieve Super Step:** Ingests external streams or files into the Raw Zone (`100-Raw-Zone`).
2. **Assess Super Step:** Executes automated data profiling and quality remediations, saving clean data to `200-Structured-Zone`.
3. **Process Super Step:** Maps ingested records to the T-P-O-L-E framework (Time, Person, Object, Location, Event).
4. **Transform Super Step:** Converts schema-on-read data into a historical schema-on-write Data Vault inside `300-Curated-Zone`.
5. **Organise Super Step:** Extracts subject-specific data marts and mini-dimensions for downstream analytical access.
6. **Report Super Step:** Generates automated visualizations, model performance statistics, and profiling metrics.

### Pattern 2: Audit, Balance, and Control (ABC) Governance Layer
* **Audit Subsystem:** Captures execution statistics, execution profiling (`cProfile`), and structured logging across worker threads.
* **Balance Subsystem:** Monitors and optimizes cloud compute resource utilization to prevent run-away execution costs.
* **Control Subsystem:** Acts as a gatekeeper, governing job schedules, retry policies, and resource allocation across oversubscribed compute pools.

### Pattern 3: Advisory vs. Direct OT Control Path
* **Advisory Mode (Approved):** ML predictions are sent as advisory signals or setpoint suggestions to human operators or supervisory controllers.
* **Direct Control Path (Restricted):** Direct execution of physical actions on machinery or SCADA loops requires explicit safety override checks, hardware interlocks, and fallback logic.

---

## 5. Failure Modes & Anti-Patterns
* **FM-1: Unauthenticated Control-Plane Coupling:** Wiring ML model inference directly into physical actuators or SCADA control paths without safety authentication, human validation, or deterministic fallback code.
* **FM-2: Siloed Robotics Telemetry:** Over 65% of robotics feedback data remains isolated within local robot ecosystems, creating dark data gaps and unmonitored model drift.
* **FM-3: Mismatched Algorithm Selection & Domain Overfitting:** Selecting algorithms without verifying decision boundaries across the full attribute domain, leading to severe misclassifications at boundary conditions.
* **FM-4: Non-Compliant Automated Profiling (GDPR Violation):** Deploying automated scoring or profiling models on personal or operational data without logging audit trails, lineage, or providing a mechanism for the "right to object."

---

## 6. Candidate Rules

```
Candidate Rule Taxonomy:
- ready: Statically checkable using allowed pattern_match, file_exists, or file_absent conditions.
- needs-review: Valid architectural concept, but requires additional domain heuristics to avoid false positives.
- blocked: Cannot be deterministically audited via static file analysis.
```

### Rule 1: `cross-ml-iiot-001`
* **Label:** `ready`
* **Rule Description:** Detects direct wiring of ML model predictions to OT control paths without fallback handling or human review evidence.
* **Target Artifacts:** Industrial deployment manifests, SCADA integration scripts, control loop controllers.
* **Severity:** `critical`

### Rule 2: `dataops-ml-001`
* **Label:** `ready`
* **Rule Description:** Identifies data pipelines that lack structured logging, execution stats (ABC layer), or schema validation checks in data lake ingestion scripts.
* **Target Artifacts:** Pipeline scripts, RIF execution scripts, ingestion workflows.
* **Severity:** `error`

### Rule 3: `dataops-model-card-001`
* **Label:** `ready`
* **Rule Description:** Flags production model deployments that lack accompanying model cards, dataset lineage manifests, or version metadata.
* **Target Artifacts:** Model deployment directories, release manifests, model registry files.
* **Severity:** `warning`

### Rule 4: `dataops-anomaly-001`
* **Label:** `needs-review`
* **Rule Description:** Flags hardcoded anomaly thresholds in SCADA alerting scripts that lack cross-validation or dynamic sensitivity tuning.
* **Target Artifacts:** Alerting configurations, anomaly detection scripts.
* **Severity:** `warning`

### Candidates Not Emitted (`blocked`)
* *Rule:* "Ensure 100% GDPR fairness and non-bias across automated profiling systems."
* *Reason for Exclusion:* Non-bias and organizational fairness cannot be verified statically by inspecting repository file patterns; requires qualitative human governance review.

---

## 8. Risk & Governance Analysis
Industrial ML deployments introduce physical operational risks alongside digital software risks. False positives in SCADA anomaly models can cause unnecessary factory shutdowns, while false negatives can result in catastrophic equipment failure.

To mitigate model risk, systems must enforce:
1. **Data Lineage & Provenance Tracking:** Documenting exact data origins from raw lake zones to curated data vaults.
2. **Human-in-the-Loop Interlocks:** Requiring human operator sign-off for out-of-bound predictions or physical control actions.
3. **GDPR & Regulatory Alignment:** Implementing mechanisms for right-of-explanation, audit logging, and data portability in automated decision systems.

---

- [ ] Configure R-A-P-T-O-R folder structures (`100-Raw-Zone`, `200-Structured-Zone`, `300-Curated-Zone`).
- [ ] Verify Data Vault Hubs, Links, and Satellites schema definitions.
- [ ] Implement execution profiling (`cProfile`) and structured logging across thread pools.
- [ ] Verify blue-green deployment scripts for real-time model updates.
- [ ] Establish automated model card generation on model export.
- [ ] Ensure safety interlocks separate advisory ML outputs from physical SCADA control lines.
