# Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Outlier Analysis
- Title: Outlier Analysis
- Author(s): Charu C. Aggarwal
- Branch: dataops
- RepoScope profile: dataops
- Primary source file: Charu C. Aggarwal (auth.) - Outlier analysis (2013, Springer).pdf
- Note status: partially-ready-for-YAML

## 2. Scope
- In scope:
  - Fundamental outlier definitions, distinction between noise and anomalies, point vs. contextual vs. collective anomalies, and the foundational principle that "The Data Model is Everything".
  - Core algorithmic frameworks: Extreme value analysis, probabilistic mixture models, linear regression and Principal Component Analysis (PCA), proximity-based models (LOF, LOCI, distance and density methods), and information-theoretic compression models.
  - High-dimensional outlier detection techniques: projected outliers, grid-based evolutionary search, Subspace Outlier Degree (SOD), OUTRES, and random subspace ensembles.
  - Complex data domains: time-series sensor streams, discrete sequences (Markovian, rule-based, and HMM models), categorical data, spatial data, and network graphs.
  - Evaluation methodologies (Precision-Recall curves, ROC analysis) and rare-class cost-sensitive classification.
- Out of scope:
  - Source code implementation details in specific proprietary languages or vendor frameworks.
  - Non-static runtime behavioral performance benchmarking and hardware-level execution profiling.
  - Broad organizational governance and business ROI management metrics.

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Primary architectural thesis: Every outlier detection method relies on creating a mathematical model of normal data behavior and evaluating the degree of deviation of individual points as an outlier score against that model.
2. Core design tradeoffs: The fundamental tradeoff between full-dimensional proximity analysis (which suffers from distance concentration and masking in high dimensions) and subspace projection methods (which isolate relevant local features but incur combinatorial search complexity).
3. Primary failure mode: Selecting an inappropriate normal data model for the underlying data distribution, such as applying linear regression to non-linearly correlated or clustered data.
4. Key operational boundary: Unsupervised anomaly detection methods produce continuous numeric outlier scores rather than deterministic binary labels, requiring statistical tail modeling or human domain expertise to establish operational decision thresholds.
5. Principal abstraction mechanism: The abstraction of complex multidimensional data into lower-dimensional projection hyperplanes, local distance neighborhoods, or probabilistic mixture distributions.
6. Data engineering implications: Features must undergo statistical normalization and variance scaling prior to distance or linear analysis to prevent high-magnitude attributes from disproportionately dominating outlier scores.
7. Verification philosophy: Model effectiveness and threshold tuning are validated using Precision-Recall curves and Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) analysis against ground-truth labels or rare-class surrogates.
8. System scalability constraints: Proximity-based distance algorithms exhibit $O(N^2)$ computational complexity, requiring cell-based or grid-based indexing approximations for large data streams.
9. Domain-specific contextual dependencies: Data dependencies in temporal, spatial, or discrete sequence contexts require contextual neighborhood modeling rather than independent identically distributed (i.i.d.) assumptions.
10. Human-in-the-loop requirement: High-dimensional or complex industrial anomalies require intensional subspace explanations and expert human review before taking irreversible operational actions.

## 4. Source-backed structure
1. Theme: Foundations & Data Models for Outlier Analysis
   - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
   - Structural locator: 1. An Introduction to Outlier Analysis
   - Spirit locator: Establishing that outlier detection algorithms depend fundamentally on mathematical models of normal data patterns.
2. Theme: Linear & Dimensionality Reduction Models
   - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
   - Structural locator: 3. Linear Models for Outlier Detection
   - Spirit locator: Using lower-dimensional subspace projections and regression residuals to isolate points that deviate from linear correlations.
3. Theme: Proximity-Based Distance & Density Methods
   - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
   - Structural locator: 4. Proximity-based Outlier Detection
   - Spirit locator: Quantifying point isolation through cluster distances, k-nearest neighbor metrics, and local density factors.
4. Theme: High-Dimensional & Subspace Outlier Analysis
   - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
   - Structural locator: 5. High-Dimensional Outlier Detection: The Subspace Method
   - Spirit locator: Mitigating the curse of dimensionality by mining anomalies within local lower-dimensional attribute projections.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. What is the fundamental problem this source addresses?
   The source addresses the challenge of identifying rare, unexpected data patterns (anomalies) that deviate significantly from background normal behavior across diverse data types.
2. What is the core architectural principle proposed?
   The core principle is "The Data Model is Everything" — the choice of normal data model determines which data points are flagged as anomalous.
3. How does the source recommend handling high-dimensional data?
   The source recommends analyzing data in local lower-dimensional subspaces to prevent full-dimensional distance concentration and attribute noise masking.
4. How does the source recommend handling temporal and streaming data?
   The source recommends evaluating contextual deviations from forecasted values using auto-regressive models or shape discords across sliding temporal windows.
5. What are the key evaluation criteria for models in this domain?
   Model effectiveness is measured by evaluating precision-recall tradeoffs and ROC curves using continuous outlier scores converted to binary labels via statistical tail thresholds.
6. What is the recommended operational interaction between automated models and human operators?
   Automated models should produce continuous anomaly scores and localized attribute explanations for human review rather than making unvalidated autonomous control decisions.

## 6. Key concepts
1. Concept: "The Data Model is Everything"
   - Definition: The fundamental postulate that all outlier detection algorithms define normal data behavior via a mathematical model and score points based on deviation from that model.
   - Why it matters: Choosing an incorrect model leads to severe false positives or missed anomalies.
   - Related concepts: Generative mixture models, linear regression, proximity models.
   - Citation: Outlier detection algorithms create a model of normal patterns and compute an outlier score based on deviations from these patterns.
   - Verification status: source-backed
2. Concept: Mahalanobis Distance & Local Covariance
   - Definition: A scale-invariant distance metric that normalizes distances by the cluster or local dataset covariance matrix along directions of correlation.
   - Why it matters: Prevents features with large variances from dominating proximity calculations and accounts for inter-attribute correlation.
   - Related concepts: Euclidean distance, local variance scaling, PCA.
   - Citation: Mahalanobis distance scales square distances by cluster variances along principal correlation directions.
   - Verification status: source-backed
3. Concept: Principal Component Analysis (PCA) Residual Scores
   - Definition: An unsupervised linear modeling technique that projects data onto principal eigenvectors and uses projection residuals as outlier scores.
   - Why it matters: Captures global linear correlations and isolates points whose feature combinations deviate from the main hyperplanes.
   - Related concepts: Regression residuals, eigenvalues, noise correction.
   - Citation: Data points with large residuals from lower-dimensional hyperplanes exhibit anomalous behavior.
   - Verification status: source-backed
4. Concept: Local Outlier Factor (LOF)
   - Definition: A density-based algorithm that compares the local density of a data point to the average local density of its k-nearest neighbors.
   - Why it matters: Enables detection of local anomalies in datasets with varying density distributions across different regions.
   - Related concepts: Local Correlation Integral (LOCI), density-based clustering, k-nearest neighbors.
   - Citation: Local Outlier Factor measures relative local density variations between points and their neighborhoods.
   - Verification status: source-backed
5. Concept: Curse of Dimensionality & Distance Concentration
   - Definition: The phenomenon in high-dimensional space where data becomes extremely sparse and all pair-wise distances converge, losing contrast.
   - Why it matters: Full-dimensional proximity metrics degrade rapidly and mask true anomalies behind noisy irrelevant features.
   - Related concepts: Data sparsity, projected outliers, subspace exploration.
   - Citation: In high-dimensional space, pairs of points become almost equidistant, causing full-dimensional distance contrast to be lost.
   - Verification status: source-backed
6. Concept: Subspace Outlier Degree (SOD)
   - Definition: A subspace outlier detection metric that calculates the normalized distance of a point to its reference set mean within axis-parallel subspaces of low variance.
   - Why it matters: Isolates localized anomalies that only manifest when specific combinations of attributes are examined.
   - Related concepts: Projected outliers, OUTRES, feature bagging ensembles.
   - Citation: Subspace Outlier Degree computes reference set distances specifically within low-variance subspaces.
   - Verification status: source-backed
7. Concept: Autoregressive (AR/ARMA) Forecasting Errors
   - Definition: Time-series contextual models that predict future sequence values based on historical window observations and flag large prediction errors as anomalies.
   - Why it matters: Captures sudden temporal trend shifts and abrupt changes in dynamic data streams.
   - Related concepts: Contextual outliers, shape discords, HOTSAX.
   - Citation: Temporal contextual outliers are flagged by measuring deviations from forecasted values in auto-regressive models.
   - Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
1. Pattern: Feature Normalization & Standard Deviation Scaling
   - Problem solved: Dominance of high-magnitude attributes in multi-attribute distance and variance calculations.
   - Recommended approach: Apply variance scaling and standard deviation normalization across all continuous features before executing proximity or linear modeling.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Ingestion pipelines or preprocessing scripts explicitly invoke standard scaling transformations prior to model fitting.
   - Limitations: Extreme outliers present during scaling can distort mean and variance estimates unless robust scaling is used.
   - Source citation:
     - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
     - Structural locator: 3. Principal Component Analysis
     - Spirit locator: Normalizing feature variances ensures equal weighting across attributes in linear and proximity computations.
2. Pattern: PCA Residual Thresholding for Linear Outliers
   - Problem solved: Detecting anomalous records in datasets governed by strong linear correlations.
   - Recommended approach: Transform features using principal components, compute normalized residual distances across small-eigenvalue components, and apply chi-square distribution tests for scoring.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Pipeline configurations define PCA transformer modules combined with explicit residual calculation and chi-square threshold evaluation.
   - Limitations: Fails when data is clustered in non-linear manifolds rather than aligned along linear hyperplanes.
   - Source citation:
     - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
     - Structural locator: 3. Linear Models for Outlier Detection
     - Spirit locator: Evaluating squared projection distances to principal component hyperplanes provides a robust metric for linear anomaly scoring.
3. Pattern: Random Subspace Ensembles (Feature Bagging)
   - Problem solved: High-dimensional feature noise masking local subspace anomalies.
   - Recommended approach: Sample multiple lower-dimensional attribute subsets, fit base outlier models on each subset, and aggregate scores using combination functions.
   - Repository-level manifestation: Ensembling scripts that invoke random feature sub-selection wrappers around base detectors (e.g. LOF or k-NN).
   - Limitations: Random sub-sampling may require numerous iterations if the anomalous subspace is extremely low-dimensional relative to total feature count.
   - Source citation:
     - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
     - Structural locator: 5. Combining Outliers from Multiple Subspaces
     - Spirit locator: Combining outlier scores across multiple sub-sampled feature spaces improves detection robustness against high-dimensional noise.

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
1. Failure mode: Inappropriate Model Assumption (Linear vs. Clustered Data)
   - Why it happens: Applying linear regression or global PCA to data that naturally forms separate non-linear clusters.
   - Early warning signs: High residual error across the majority of dataset points and poor anomaly precision during validation.
   - Repository evidence: Model scripts fitting linear estimators without prior linearity checks or cluster exploratory analysis.
   - Severity tendency: error
2. Failure mode: Full-Dimensional Distance Concentration (Curse of Dimensionality)
   - Why it happens: Running full-dimensional nearest-neighbor or distance algorithms on datasets with dozens or hundreds of features.
   - Early warning signs: Outlier scores for all data points converge to a narrow numerical band with zero contrast.
   - Repository evidence: Proximity detectors configured with full feature matrices without dimensionality reduction or subspace sub-sampling.
   - Severity tendency: warning
3. Failure mode: Hardcoded Static Anomaly Thresholds
   - Why it happens: Hardcoding numeric cutoffs (e.g., `if score > 0.95`) without statistical distribution modeling or dynamic calibration.
   - Early warning signs: High alert volume during operational data drift or zero alerts when underlying data variance changes.
   - Repository evidence: Model code containing hardcoded comparison floats for alert generation without configuration files or statistical tail tests.
   - Severity tendency: error
4. Failure mode: Unvalidated Control-Plane Coupling
   - Why it happens: Wiring raw anomaly detector outputs directly into industrial control paths or automated system actions without human review or fallback logic.
   - Early warning signs: Frequent false-positive system trips or sensor noise triggering automated shutdowns.
   - Repository evidence: Automation scripts connecting model prediction endpoints directly to actuator control calls without authorization or exception wrappers.
   - Severity tendency: critical

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated)
- Presence of feature normalization or standard scaling transformers in data pipeline configurations.
- Verification that anomaly score threshold values are stored in version-controlled configuration files rather than hardcoded floats in execution scripts.
- Check for presence of validation split configurations or temporal test sets in model training code.
- Verification that model tracking metadata or model card specifications exist alongside deployed model artifacts.
### Human review (contextual)
- Assessment of whether the selected normal data model matches the actual topological distribution of the dataset.
- Evaluation of alert fatigue tolerance and human-in-the-loop operational review workflows.
- Review of feature selection relevance and intensional subspace explanations for high-dimensional alerts.

## 10. Candidate rules

- Rule ID: dataops-anomaly-001
- Title: Anomaly threshold configuration file present
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Hardcoding anomaly threshold values in script logic prevents dynamic calibration and causes operational alert drift.
- Source-backed basis: Outlier detection algorithms output continuous scores that require statistical tail modeling and tunable decision thresholds.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Pipeline configuration files, YAML/JSON config manifests.
- Pass condition: Anomaly score thresholds are defined in external configuration files or parameterized specs.
- Fail condition: Anomaly score thresholds are absent or hardcoded directly as numeric literals in executable script files.
- Exceptions or limitations: Experimental exploratory notebooks.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A
- Source citation:
  - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
  - Structural locator: 1. Extreme Value Analysis
  - Spirit locator: Continuous outlier scores require calibrated thresholding to establish binary decision boundaries.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-model-card-001
- Title: Production model card or version metadata present
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Deploying anomaly detection models without model cards or metadata prevents auditability and lineage tracking.
- Source-backed basis: Industrial ML governance requires clear documentation of model assumptions, training data scope, and operational boundaries.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Model card Markdown files (`MODEL_CARD.md`), metadata JSON files.
- Pass condition: A repository publishing or deploying a production model contains a valid model card or metadata file.
- Fail condition: Production model deployment files exist without accompanying model card documentation or version metadata.
- Exceptions or limitations: Internal utility scripts.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A
- Source citation:
  - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
  - Structural locator: 1. The Data Model is Everything
  - Spirit locator: Documenting the underlying normal data model and assumptions is essential for verifying model applicability.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: dataops-drift-001
- Title: Temporal validation split or drift monitoring config present
- Profile: dataops
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Time-series and streaming anomaly models must be validated using temporal splits rather than random cross-validation to prevent data leakage.
- Source-backed basis: Time-series data exhibits temporal continuity and autocorrelation, requiring specialized sequential validation logic.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Training scripts, cross-validation configurations.
- Pass condition: Training scripts implement temporal train/test splitting or drift monitoring configs.
- Fail condition: Time-series training code uses random k-fold cross-validation or lacks drift monitoring logic.
- Exceptions or limitations: Static non-temporal datasets.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Requires AST analysis of temporal split functions beyond simple pattern matching.
- Source citation:
  - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
  - Structural locator: 8. Time Series and Multidimensional Streaming Outlier Detection
  - Spirit locator: Temporal continuity dictates that time-series models must be evaluated against sequential historical horizons.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: iiot-model-001
- Title: Human review wrapper or fallback logic on control path
- Profile: iiot
- Severity tendency: critical
- Rationale: Anomaly detection models produce probabilistic scores and must not trigger physical control actions without human authorization or safe fallback logic.
- Source-backed basis: Anomaly models serve as advisory systems; treating them as deterministic fault classifiers leads to unvalidated physical actions.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Actuator integration scripts, OT control code.
- Pass condition: Control-plane calls consuming model predictions include authorization checks, human approval steps, or fallback error wrappers.
- Fail condition: Model prediction outputs directly trigger physical actuation commands without validation logic.
- Exceptions or limitations: Purely advisory alerting systems.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Cannot deterministically verify control-plane fallback wrappers via static regex without false positives.
- Source citation:
  - Source: Charu C. Aggarwal (2013) Outlier Analysis
  - Structural locator: 1. Introduction
  - Spirit locator: Anomaly detection outputs represent statistical deviations requiring human domain verification in operational settings.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment
```yaml
references:

  concept: "anomaly-threshold-config"

  type: "file_exists"

  error_when: "file_absent"

- id: "dataops-model-card-001"

  message: "Production model deployment directory lacks required MODEL_CARD.md documentation."
```

### Candidates not emitted
- `dataops-drift-001`: Marked `needs-review` because AST parsing is required to verify temporal train/test splitting logic without false positives.
- `iiot-model-001`: Marked `needs-review` because static regex cannot deterministically verify control-plane authorization and fallback wrappers without false positives.

# API Design Patterns — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** API Design Patterns
- Title: API Design Patterns
- Author(s): JJ Geewax
- Branch: webapp
- RepoScope profile: webapp
- Primary source file: API Design Patterns.pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

## 2. Scope
- In scope: Resource identification, naming conventions, standard method definitions, field mask structures for partial updates/retrievals, custom methods, request validation, and idempotent operation handling.
- In scope: Contract definitions, schema declarations, error payload standardization, and request deduplication patterns.
- In scope: Long-running operation metadata definitions, batch request syntax, and filtering/pagination parameter formats.
- In scope: Deprecation signaling and revision tracking in web application API surfaces.
- Out of scope: Database storage engine optimization and SQL indexing strategies.
- Out of scope: Network infrastructure routing, firewall rule configurations, and load balancer IP allocation.
- Out of scope: Organizational developer hiring, API team staffing models, and sales pricing strategies.

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Core thesis: Predictable, resource-oriented web APIs are built on standardized behavioral primitives, explicit schemas, and strict operational invariants rather than ad-hoc remote procedure calls.
2. Architectural stance: The API boundary is a immutable public contract that must isolate internal domain models from external consumer representations.
3. Primary trade-off embraced: Accepting additional structural boilerplate and interface verbosity in exchange for strict backward compatibility, clear client expectations, and long-term maintainability.
4. Primary risk feared: Accidental breaking changes caused by uncoordinated payload mutations, implicit field modifications, or unversioned resource transformations.
5. Definition of quality: Consistency across resource names, predictable response schemas, deterministic error structures, and strict adherence to HTTP method semantics.
6. View on automation: Automated validation of request parameters, contract schemas, and field masks is essential to prevent invalid state transitions before execution.
7. View on human process: API design reviews must evaluate resource hierarchy and domain abstractions before code implementation begins.
8. Ideal repository posture: The repository contains explicit OpenAPI/JSON-Schema specifications, field mask helper definitions, and strict request validation rules checked during continuous integration.
9. Anti-pattern priority: Eliminating non-standard custom methods, deep resource hierarchies, and unpaginated collection endpoints.
10. Long-term lifecycle vision: APIs evolve gracefully through additive schema changes, explicit field masks, and revision-tracked resources without disrupting existing integrations.

## 4. Source-backed structure
### Theme 1: Resource Naming and Identification
- Source: API Design Patterns
- Structural locator: Chapter 3: Naming
- Spirit locator: Resource identifiers and field names must be explicit, human-readable, and unambiguous, incorporating physical units directly into field identifiers to prevent misinterpretation.

### Theme 2: Standard Methods and Partial Operations
- Source: API Design Patterns
- Structural locator: Chapter 7: Standard methods
- Spirit locator: Core CRUD operations must strictly map to standard methods, using query-based field masks for partial retrievals and updates rather than full object replacements.

### Theme 3: Custom Methods and Long-Running Operations
- Source: API Design Patterns
- Structural locator: Chapter 9: Custom methods
- Spirit locator: Specialized business logic that cannot fit standard CRUD operations must use custom methods separated by a colon, returning long-running operation handles for asynchronous execution.

### Theme 4: Request Validation and Safety
- Source: API Design Patterns
- Structural locator: Chapter 27: Request validation
- Spirit locator: High-risk operations must support dry-run validation flags allowing clients to verify request correctness without mutating server state.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. What does this source consider the most dangerous form of technical debt or architectural drift?
Uncoordinated schema changes and inconsistent resource representations that break client contracts and force emergency downstream refactoring.

2. How does this source balance automated verification against human judgment?
Automated tools must strictly enforce structural contracts, field mask validation, and schema presence, while human review must assess domain modeling, resource scoping, and abstraction boundaries.

3. What is the source's stance on standardization versus local autonomy?
Standardization across naming, error formats, and standard methods is non-negotiable across the entire engineering organization to ensure a unified developer experience.

4. What runtime or operational failure modes does the source trace back to repository-level static structure?
Missing pagination parameters cause memory exhaustion during large collection queries, and missing validation schemas allow malformed payloads to corrupt persistent storage.

5. What repository artifacts does the source treat as non-negotiable proof of health?
Machine-readable API specifications (OpenAPI/JSON Schema), deterministic route definitions, and automated contract test suites.

6. How does the source handle evolution, versioning, and change over time?
APIs must preserve backward compatibility by restricting changes to purely additive modifications and using resource revision metadata for historical tracking.

## 6. Key concepts
### Concept 1: Field Mask
- Definition: A list of string paths indicating specific fields to retrieve or modify in a resource payload.
- Why it matters: Enables partial updates and retrievals, drastically reducing network bandwidth and preventing race conditions during concurrent updates.
- Related concepts: Partial Updates, Implicit Field Mask, Read Mask.
- Citation: Chapter 8: Partial updates and retrievals
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 2: Resource Identifier
- Definition: A unique, permanent, and readable string that addresses a specific resource within an API layout.
- Why it matters: Provides permanent referencing across collections without exposing internal database auto-incrementing integers.
- Related concepts: Resource Layout, Hierarchy Scope, URI Path.
- Citation: Chapter 6: Resource identification
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 3: Custom Method
- Definition: An API method used for custom actions when standard CRUD methods do not fit the business domain, syntax-separated by a colon.
- Why it matters: Prevents overloading standard update or delete semantics with complex state machine side-effects.
- Related concepts: Standard Methods, Long-Running Operations, Imperative Actions.
- Citation: Chapter 9: Custom methods
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 4: Long-Running Operation (LRO)
- Definition: A handle returned by an API when a request cannot complete synchronously, tracking progress and status over time.
- Why it matters: Prevents HTTP connection timeouts and allows clients to poll or receive callbacks on lengthy background tasks.
- Related concepts: Asynchronous Processing, Operation Metadata, Polling.
- Citation: Chapter 10: Long-running operations
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 5: Request Validation (validateOnly)
- Definition: A boolean request parameter specifying that an API call should validate inputs without committing changes.
- Why it matters: Gives clients a safe preview mechanism to catch validation errors prior to execution.
- Related concepts: Dry Run, Idempotency, Input Sanitization.
- Citation: Chapter 27: Request validation
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 6: Resource Revisions
- Definition: Immutable snapshots of a resource stored over time, referenced with a revision identifier.
- Why it matters: Allows auditing historical changes, restoring previous states, and managing document versions cleanly.
- Related concepts: Soft Delete, Audit Logs, Time Travel.
- Citation: Chapter 28: Resource revisions
- Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
### Pattern 1: Partial Update with Field Masks
- Problem solved: Overwriting concurrent modifications when updating specific fields of a complex resource.
- Recommended approach: Pass a string array of modified field paths alongside the resource payload in PATCH requests.
- Repository-level manifestation: Route definitions and controller signatures taking explicit field mask parameters or inferring them from JSON keys.
- Limitations: Cannot update items inside repeated array fields by positional index.
- Source citation: Chapter 8: Partial updates and retrievals

### Pattern 2: Explicit Naming with Units
- Problem solved: Misinterpretation of numerical fields (e.g., duration or file size) leading to incorrect calculations.
- Recommended approach: Append exact unit suffixes to field names (e.g., `sizeBytes`, `timeoutSeconds`, `dimensionsPixels`).
- Repository-level manifestation: Interface definitions and database schemas using explicit unit-suffixed property names.
- Limitations: Requires disciplined code reviews to prevent brief non-suffixed name additions.
- Source citation: Chapter 3: Naming

### Pattern 3: Dry-Run Request Validation
- Problem solved: Accidental execution of destructive or irreversible custom operations due to malformed payloads.
- Recommended approach: Support a `validateOnly` field in request schemas to execute validation logic without side effects.
- Repository-level manifestation: Request schema interfaces including `validateOnly?: boolean`.
- Limitations: Backend logic must explicitly guard state-mutating code paths when `validateOnly` is true.
- Source citation: Chapter 27: Request validation

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
### Failure Mode 1: Unpaginated Collection Endpoint
- Why it happens: Developers query database tables directly and return all rows in a single JSON response array.
- Early warning signs: High memory usage on GET collection endpoints as database row counts grow.
- Repository evidence: Collection handlers lacking `pageSize` and `pageToken` parameters in OpenAPI specs or route controllers.
- Severity tendency: critical

### Failure Mode 2: Overloading Standard UPDATE with Side Effects
- Why it happens: Coupling external side-effects (e.g. sending emails or triggering external builds) directly into standard REST update calls.
- Early warning signs: Unpredictable API updates and unexpected background jobs running during bulk data Syncs.
- Repository evidence: Update request handlers calling third-party notification or external queue services.
- Severity tendency: error

### Failure Mode 3: Missing Field Units in API Payload Schemas
- Why it happens: Using generic field names like `size` or `duration` without defining whether units represent bytes, seconds, or milliseconds.
- Early warning signs: Client-side bugs where values are scaled incorrectly by orders of magnitude.
- Repository evidence: Interface schemas containing numeric fields named `size` or `time` without unit suffixes.
- Severity tendency: warning

### Failure Mode 4: Destructive Replace Semantics on Partial Modification
- Why it happens: Using HTTP PUT to replace an entire resource when clients only intended to modify a single field.
- Early warning signs: Unintentional deletion of newly added resource fields when updated by older API clients.
- Repository evidence: Absence of PATCH endpoints or field mask handling logic in API route declarations.
- Severity tendency: error

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated via RepoScope)
- Presence of an OpenAPI or JSON Schema specification file in repository docs or root.
- Absence of unversioned public API route declarations.
- Presence of package manifest and associated lockfile.
- Absence of hardcoded secret tokens in configuration or environment template files.

### Human review (contextual & strategic)
- Evaluation of domain model boundaries and resource layout hierarchy.
- Verification that custom methods are justified and cannot be modeled as standard methods.
- Assessment of field mask granularity and error handling semantics.
- Evaluation of rate-limiting policies and quota allocation strategy.

## 10. Candidate rules

- Rule ID: webapp-api-001
- Title: Missing API Specification File
- Profile: webapp
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Repositories providing web APIs must maintain an explicit OpenAPI or schema specification for contract auditing.
- Source-backed basis: The source emphasizes machine-readable API contracts to guarantee predictable response shapes.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Absence of `openapi.json`, `openapi.yaml`, or `swagger.json` in the codebase.
- Pass condition: At least one API specification file exists in the repository.
- Fail condition: No API specification file is present in the repository.
- Exceptions or limitations: Repositories that do not expose public HTTP endpoints.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A
- Source citation: Chapter 5: Design in Practice
- Source: API Design Patterns
- Structural locator: Chapter 5: Design in Practice
- Spirit locator: API schemas must be formally defined to ensure predictable client consumption.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: webapp-api-002
- Title: Absence of Validation Parameter in High-Risk API Schema
- Profile: webapp
- Severity tendency: info
- Rationale: High-risk state-mutating requests should support preview validation via a `validateOnly` field.
- Source-backed basis: The source specifies dry-run request validation patterns for safe operation previews.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Route controllers or schema files matching request parameters for `validateOnly`.
- Pass condition: Route schemas or request interfaces include `validateOnly` pattern match.
- Fail condition: Request interfaces omit `validateOnly` validation flags.
- Exceptions or limitations: Cannot verify runtime execution logic using static regex.
- YAML readiness: needs-review
- Reason if not ready: Requires AST parsing of route schemas to reliably identify high-risk state-mutating handlers.
- Source citation: Chapter 27: Request validation
- Source: API Design Patterns
- Structural locator: Chapter 27: Request validation
- Spirit locator: Validation preview parameters protect clients from accidental side effects.
- Verification status: source-backed

- Rule ID: webapp-api-003
- Title: Incorrect Architectural Domain Boundary Placement
- Profile: webapp
- Severity tendency: error
- Rationale: Service boundaries must align cleanly with bounded contexts rather than arbitrary database tables.
- Source-backed basis: The source cautions against making every internal database table an exposed public resource.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Domain service boundaries and controller layouts.
- Pass condition: Service boundaries reflect domain contexts.
- Fail condition: Microservices mirror database schemas 1-to-1.
- Exceptions or limitations: Cannot be statically verified without domain modeling context.
- YAML readiness: blocked
- Reason if not ready: Requires high-level human architectural judgment and organizational domain knowledge.
- Source citation: Chapter 4: Resource scope and hierarchy
- Source: API Design Patterns
- Structural locator: Chapter 4: Resource scope and hierarchy
- Spirit locator: Resource boundaries must reflect functional abstractions rather than raw database schemas.
- Verification status: source-backed

## 11. Suggested YAML fragment
```yaml

  - id: webapp-api-001
```

# Web Application Security — RepoScope Knowledge Note

## 1. Source identity

- **Title:** Web Application Security
- Title: Web Application Security: Exploitation and Countermeasures for Modern Web Applications
- Author(s): Andrew Hoffman
- Branch: webapp
- RepoScope profile: webapp
- Primary source file: Andrew Hoffman - Web Application Security_ Exploitation and Countermeasures for Modern Web Applications (2024, O'Reilly Media).pdf
- Note status: reviewed-with-candidate-rules

## 2. Scope
- In scope: Web application security posture, defense-in-depth countermeasures, security headers (CSP, HSTS, COOP, CORP, CORS), input validation and output encoding patterns.
- In scope: Static security auditing, dependency management hygiene, CSRF defense mechanisms, and secret leakage prevention in repositories.
- In scope: Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) mitigations, XML External Entity (XXE) protections, and rate-limiting patterns.
- In scope: Reconnaissance surface minimization and security policy file presence.
- Out of scope: Physical server datacenter security and hardware lockbox access control.
- Out of scope: Network layer BGP routing security and telecom hardware configuration.
- Out of scope: General corporate employee security awareness training and HR policy enforcement.

## 3. Source spirit card
1. Core thesis: Robust web security requires combining offensive vulnerability analysis with defensive architectural countermeasures embedded directly into application code and configuration.
2. Architectural stance: Security must be engineered into every layer of the web application stack as an active, defense-in-depth posture rather than an afterthought.
3. Primary trade-off embraced: Adding strict browser security policies and enforcement overhead to prevent entire classes of client-side exploitation.
4. Primary risk feared: Silent remote code execution, session hijacking, and catastrophic data exfiltration resulting from unmitigated injection flaws.
5. Definition of quality: Codebases that systematically enforce secure defaults, eliminate dangerous DOM sinks, and contain hardened HTTP security headers.
6. View on automation: Automated security scanners and CI static analysis checks are essential for detecting vulnerable dependencies and misconfigured headers early.
7. View on human process: Threat modeling, security architecture reviews, and developer security champions are required for complex authorization logic.
8. Ideal repository posture: The repository contains explicit security header configurations, automated dependency audit steps in CI, strict CSP declarations, and an up-to-date SECURITY.md file.
9. Anti-pattern priority: Eliminating inline script execution, unescaped DOM insertions, wildcard CORS origins, and unmaintained third-party dependencies.
10. Long-term lifecycle vision: Continuous security integration where every code commit is automatically audited for vulnerabilities, secrets, and policy regressions.

## 4. Source-backed structure
### Theme 1: Web Application Reconnaissance and Dependency Tracking
- Source: Web Application Security
- Structural locator: Chapter 2: Introduction to Web Application Reconnaissance
- Spirit locator: Mapping application endpoints and auditing third-party dependencies is essential to understand the application's attack surface and supply chain risks.

### Theme 2: Cross-Site Scripting and Client-Side Mitigations
- Source: Web Application Security
- Structural locator: Chapter 10: Cross-Site Scripting
- Spirit locator: Preventing client-side code injection requires eliminating unsafe DOM sinks and enforcing context-aware output encoding.

### Theme 3: Content Security Policy and Browser Controls
- Source: Web Application Security
- Structural locator: Chapter 18: Content Security Policy
- Spirit locator: Restricting resource loading origins via a strict Content Security Policy neutralizes XSS and unauthorized script execution.

### Theme 4: HTTP Security Headers and Cross-Origin Controls
- Source: Web Application Security
- Structural locator: Chapter 22: HTTP Security Headers
- Spirit locator: Hardening HTTP responses with transport security and cross-origin policies protects user sessions and isolates browsing contexts.

## 5. Source-backed spirit
1. What does this source consider the most dangerous form of technical debt or architectural drift?
Unmonitored third-party dependencies and legacy unhardened HTTP endpoints that expose the application to known exploits.

2. How does this source balance automated verification against human judgment?
Automated tools must continuously inspect static artifacts, dependencies, and headers, while human security engineers conduct threat modeling and manual penetration testing.

3. What is the source's stance on standardization versus local autonomy?
Centralized security libraries and standardized header configurations must be used across all applications to eliminate custom, flawed security implementations.

4. What runtime or operational failure modes does the source trace back to repository-level static structure?
Missing security header configurations allow clickjacking and XSS attacks, while committed `.env` files lead to credential theft and cloud takeover.

5. What repository artifacts does the source treat as non-negotiable proof of health?
A clear `SECURITY.md` contact policy, explicit header configurations (including CSP and HSTS), and dependency manifest lockfiles.

6. How does the source handle evolution, versioning, and change over time?
Security posture must evolve alongside web browser standards, regularly updating security headers, dependency versions, and isolation policies.

## 6. Key concepts
### Concept 1: Content Security Policy (CSP)
- Definition: An HTTP header allowing site operators to restrict the resources (such as JavaScript, CSS, Images) that the browser is allowed to load.
- Why it matters: Serves as a primary defense-in-depth layer against Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) and data injection attacks.
- Related concepts: Directives, Nonce, Strict Dynamic, Report-URI.
- Citation: Chapter 18: Content Security Policy
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 2: Cross-Origin Resource Sharing (CORS)
- Definition: A browser mechanism that uses HTTP headers to tell browsers if a web application running at one origin has permission to access resources from a different origin.
- Why it matters: Misconfigured wildcard CORS allows unauthorized third-party sites to read sensitive user data via authenticated AJAX requests.
- Related concepts: Same-Origin Policy, Access-Control-Allow-Origin, Preflight Request.
- Citation: Chapter 22: HTTP Security Headers
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 3: HTTP Strict Transport Security (HSTS)
- Definition: A header forcing browsers to communicate with the server exclusively over encrypted HTTPS connections.
- Why it matters: Prevents man-in-the-middle SSL-stripping attacks during initial connection negotiation.
- Related concepts: HTTPS, Max-Age, IncludeSubDomains, Preload.
- Citation: Chapter 22: HTTP Security Headers
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 4: DOM-Based XSS Sink
- Definition: A JavaScript function or DOM object (e.g. `innerHTML`, `eval`, `document.write`) that executes untrusted input as code.
- Why it matters: Unsanitized data reaching a DOM sink allows attackers to execute arbitrary JavaScript in the context of the victim's browser session.
- Related concepts: XSS Source, Sanitization, Context-Aware Encoding.
- Citation: Chapter 10: Cross-Site Scripting
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 5: Dependency Supply Chain Vulnerability
- Definition: A security flaw introduced into an application through third-party open-source libraries or package manager dependencies.
- Why it matters: Malicious or outdated third-party packages can compromise the entire server or client environment.
- Related concepts: Software Bill of Materials (SBOM), Package Lockfile, CVE.
- Citation: Chapter 6: Identifying Third-Party Dependencies
- Verification status: source-backed

### Concept 6: Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF)
- Definition: An attack that forces an authenticated user to execute unwanted actions on a web application in which they are currently authenticated.
- Why it matters: Allows malicious sites to state-mutate user accounts without their knowledge or consent.
- Related concepts: Anti-CSRF Token, SameSite Cookie Attribute, Double-Submit Cookie.
- Citation: Chapter 11: Cross-Site Request Forgery
- Verification status: source-backed

## 7. Practical patterns and examples
### Pattern 1: Strict Content Security Policy Header Configuration
- Problem solved: Neutralizing unauthorized script execution caused by XSS flaws.
- Recommended approach: Configure HTTP response headers with `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'self'; script-src 'self' 'nonce-...'`.
- Repository-level manifestation: Server configuration files, framework header middleware, or `next.config.js` declaring strict CSP directives.
- Limitations: Requires dynamic nonce generation for inline scripts or strict adherence to external script files.
- Source citation: Chapter 18: Content Security Policy

### Pattern 2: Security Vulnerability Reporting Policy
- Problem solved: Inability of external security researchers to responsibly report discovered vulnerabilities to maintainers.
- Recommended approach: Maintain a dedicated `SECURITY.md` file in the repository root or `.github/` folder detailing disclosure channels.
- Repository-level manifestation: Presence of `SECURITY.md` file in root or `.github/SECURITY.md`.
- Limitations: Does not guarantee that incoming reports will be triaged promptly by maintainers.
- Source citation: Chapter 2: Introduction to Web Application Reconnaissance

### Pattern 3: Lockfile Preservation for Supply Chain Auditing
- Problem solved: Inconsistent dependency builds introducing unvetted or compromised package versions.
- Recommended approach: Always commit dependency lockfiles (`package-lock.json`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `yarn.lock`, `poetry.lock`) alongside package manifests.
- Repository-level manifestation: Presence of lockfile in version control alongside `package.json` or `pyproject.toml`.
- Limitations: Requires automated CI audit steps (`npm audit`) to catch newly disclosed vulnerabilities in locked packages.
- Source citation: Chapter 6: Identifying Third-Party Dependencies

## 8. Common failure modes and risks
### Failure Mode 1: Committed Secret Environment File
- Why it happens: Developers accidentally commit populated `.env` files containing live API keys, database credentials, or secret keys to version control.
- Early warning signs: Unprotected AWS credentials or database connection strings visible in git commit history.
- Repository evidence: `.env` file committed without being matched in `.gitignore`.
- Severity tendency: critical

### Failure Mode 2: Missing Security Contact Policy in Public API Repository
- Why it happens: Project maintainers omit a `SECURITY.md` file, forcing security researchers to publish vulnerabilities publicly or contact random maintainers.
- Early warning signs: Vulnerabilities being reported publicly in open GitHub issues.
- Repository evidence: Absence of `SECURITY.md` and `.github/SECURITY.md`.
- Severity tendency: warning

### Failure Mode 3: Absence of HTTP Strict Transport Security
- Why it happens: Server configuration fails to set the `Strict-Transport-Security` header on production HTTPS responses.
- Early warning signs: Application allows plain HTTP requests without forcing immediate permanent redirect and HSTS caching.
- Repository evidence: Header configuration scripts missing `Strict-Transport-Security` directives.
- Severity tendency: error

### Failure Mode 4: Wildcard CORS Configuration
- Why it happens: Setting `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` to solve cross-origin development issues and leaving it in production.
- Early warning signs: Authenticated API endpoints accessible to arbitrary third-party web domains.
- Repository evidence: Configuration files containing pattern `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: "*"`.
- Severity tendency: critical

## 9. Repository-audit translation
### Static checks (automated via RepoScope)
- Presence of `SECURITY.md` or `.github/SECURITY.md`.
- Presence of a dependency lockfile alongside package manifest files.
- Absence of plaintext `.env` files committed to version control.
- Pattern match verification for Content-Security-Policy header declarations in web application configuration files.

### Human review (contextual & strategic)
- Evaluation of CSRF protection token validation logic on state-mutating requests.
- Audit of custom authentication and session token storage strategy.
- Assessment of DOM data flows to ensure untrusted user inputs do not reach dangerous sinks.
- Review of threat modeling documents and SSDL process compliance.

## 10. Candidate rules

- Rule ID: webapp-security-001
- Title: Missing Security Policy File
- Profile: webapp
- Severity tendency: warning
- Rationale: Repositories providing web application services must contain a `SECURITY.md` file to guide responsible vulnerability disclosure.
- Source-backed basis: The source highlights the importance of established reporting channels for application security findings.
- Repository evidence to inspect: Absence of `SECURITY.md` and `.github/SECURITY.md`.
- Pass condition: `SECURITY.md` exists in root or `.github/`.
- Fail condition: Neither `SECURITY.md` nor `.github/SECURITY.md` is present.
- Exceptions or limitations: None for public or enterprise web applications.
- YAML readiness: ready
- Reason if not ready: N/A

- Source: Web Application Security

- Fail condition: Package manifest exists but lockfile is absent.
- Exceptions or limitations: None.

- Rule ID: webapp-security-003
- Title: Absence of Content Security Policy Header Declaration
- Profile: webapp

- Rationale: Web application header configurations must explicitly define a Content-Security-Policy to mitigate client-side script injection.

```yaml
        file_glob: "{next.config.*,server.js,express.js,nginx.conf,headers.json}"

        must_exist: true
        error_when: pattern_absent
```

===== FILE: docs/notes/webapp/building-microservices.md =====
# RepoScope Knowledge Note: Building Microservices

## 1. Source Identity
* **Title:** Building Microservices: Designing Fine-Grained Systems
* **Author:** Sam Newman
* **Edition / Year:** Second Edition / 2021 (O'Reilly Media)
* **Primary Domain:** Distributed Systems Architecture, Microservices, Domain-Driven Design, Software Architecture
* **Core Thesis:** Microservices are an architectural style focused on independent deployability, where small, autonomous services are modeled around business domains. To realize the benefits of scale and velocity without incurring catastrophic complexity, teams must prioritize loose coupling, strong cohesion, information hiding, and encapsulated state while avoiding shared databases and distributed transactions.

---

## 2. Scope
### Included Domains & Topics
* Architectural foundations of microservices and SOA differentiation
* Domain-driven design (DDD) mapping: aggregates, bounded contexts, ubiquitous language
* Microservice decomposition strategies and Splitting the Monolith (Strangler Fig pattern)
* Communication styles: synchronous vs. asynchronous, request-response vs. event-driven, RPC (gRPC) vs. REST vs. GraphQL
* Workflow orchestration vs. choreography and Sagas
* Build, deployment, and repository strategies (monorepos vs. multirepos, isolated execution, CI/CD)
* Resiliency, stability patterns (circuit breakers, bulkheads, retries, timeouts, idempotency), and testing strategies
* Organizational design, Inverse Conway's Law, and team alignment

### Explicit Non-Goals & Out-of-Scope
* Specific vendor cloud infrastructure configuration files (e.g., detailed AWS/GCP terraform manifests)
* End-to-end code implementations for full-stack user interfaces
* Universal recommendation of microservices for all projects regardless of domain maturity

---

## 3. Source Spirit Card
1. **Primary Architectural Value:** Independent Deployability (ability to deploy a single service to production without changing or coordinating deployments with any other service).
2. **Primary Operational Stance:** Automation-first with isolated execution environments and pervasive telemetry.
3. **Default Stance on Monoliths:** Start with a modular monolith first; decompose incrementally only when domain boundaries and scaling needs are well understood.
4. **Default Stance on Code Reuse / Libraries:** Exercise extreme caution with shared code; prefer duplicating small code snippets or service templates over shared domain libraries that leak coupling across boundaries.
5. **Coupling Tolerance:** Minimal tolerance for content or common coupling; strong preference for domain coupling over technical or temporal coupling.
6. **State Management Philosophy:** Encapsulated state per service (Database per Service); strictly avoid shared databases across microservice boundaries.
7. **Delivery Unit Boundary:** Microservice per repository (multirepo default) with independent CI pipelines.
8. **Communication Protocol Bias:** Technology-agnostic protocols; event-driven asynchronous non-blocking for east-west traffic; REST/gRPC for explicit request-response.
9. **Primary Failure Mode Warning:** Distributed Monolith—systems composed of multiple services that must be built, tested, and deployed in lockstep.
10. **Organizational Alignment Rule:** Align service boundaries to cross-functional stream-aligned teams (Conway's Law / Inverse Conway's Law).

---

## 4. Source-Backed Structure

### Theme A: Modeling and Boundary Definition
* Foundations of microservices as fine-grained, independently deployable units.
* Application of Domain-Driven Design (DDD) to align service boundaries with bounded contexts and aggregates.
* Principles of modular software: information hiding, high cohesion, and low coupling.

### Theme B: Integration and Communication Styles
* Synchronous blocking (REST, gRPC) vs. asynchronous non-blocking (event-driven messaging).
* Structural vs. semantic contract breakages; role of explicit schemas (OpenAPI, Protobuf).
* Microservice boundary hygiene: "Dumb pipes, smart endpoints" vs. bloated API Gateways/ESBs.

### Theme C: Migration, Workflows, and Sagas
* Decomposing monolithic databases and replacing database joins with application-level joins or asynchronous projection.
* Replacing distributed ACID transactions (2PC) with Sagas (orchestration vs. choreography).
* Incremental migration via the Strangler Fig pattern and parallel runs.

### Theme D: Resilience, Testing, and Operations
* Resilience engineering: circuit breakers, bulkheads, timeouts, retries with backoff, and idempotency.
* Testing strategies: consumer-driven contract testing (CDC) over brittle end-to-end test suites.
* Observability: correlation IDs, aggregated logging, and metrics telemetry across distributed call chains.

---

## 5. Source-Backed Spirit

### Question 1: How does the text define a microservice?
A microservice is an independently deployable service built around a specific business domain, encapsulating its own state and exposing clear, technology-agnostic network interfaces while keeping internal implementation details hidden from consumers.

### Question 2: What is the primary metric of architectural success?
The ability of a team to make changes to a single microservice, test it, and release it to production independently without requiring changes or coordinated deployments across other microservices or teams.

### Question 3: What is the text's view on shared databases?
Shared databases are identified as one of the worst anti-patterns in microservices because they create tight content coupling, destroy information hiding, prevent independent schema evolution, and inevitably create a distributed monolith.

### Question 4: How should cross-service business processes be coordinated?
Cross-service workflows should be modeled as Sagas using local transactions and compensating actions, strongly favoring event-driven choreography for multi-team autonomy or light orchestration within a single team boundary.

### Question 5: What is the rule regarding code reuse across service boundaries?
While technical utilities (like logging or metrics) can be reused via standard libraries, domain code and data models should not be shared via common libraries across services, as upgrading those libraries forces lockstep service redeployments.

### Question 6: What is the text's warning on API Gateways and middleware?
Middleware should remain "dumb pipes." Pushing business logic, protocol transformations, or call aggregation into central API gateways or enterprise service buses introduces centralized ownership bottlenecks, coordination friction, and operational fragility.

---

## 6. Key Concepts

### Concept 1: Independent Deployability
* **Definition:** The fundamental property where any microservice can undergo internal changes, pass automated validation, and be released into production without requiring any other service to be recompiled or deployed simultaneously.
* **Manifestation:** Distinct source control repositories, dedicated CI build pipelines, independent deployment artifacts (e.g., separate container images), and zero-downtime deployment capabilities.

### Concept 2: Bounded Context & Aggregate Mapping
* **Definition:** DDD concepts where a bounded context defines an explicit boundary within which a domain model applies, and aggregates act as self-contained state machines.
* **Manifestation:** Microservices mapped 1:1 to bounded contexts or containing discrete aggregates; explicitly isolated domain modules in code without cross-context entity leaking.

### Concept 3: Database per Service
* **Definition:** Pattern where each microservice exclusively owns and manages its backing datastore. External access to data is strictly prohibited except via public network APIs exposed by the owning service.
* **Manifestation:** Separate database instances or completely isolated schemas/credentials per service repository; absence of cross-service SQL joins or direct database connection strings.

### Concept 4: Choreographed Sagas & Correlation IDs
* **Definition:** An event-driven workflow pattern where services react to domain events broadcast over event buses without a central coordinator, passing a correlation ID across all event payloads to trace execution.
* **Manifestation:** Event schemas containing mandatory header fields (`correlation_id`, `trace_id`), event consumer handlers executing local transactions and emitting follow-up domain events.

### Concept 5: Consumer-Driven Contract Testing (CDC)
* **Definition:** A testing methodology where downstream consumers define their expectations of an API contract as automated tests, which are executed against the producing microservice's build pipeline.
* **Manifestation:** Dedicated contract test files (e.g., Pact files) stored in repositories and checked during the provider's build pipeline before deployment.

### Concept 6: Dumb Pipes, Smart Endpoints
* **Definition:** An architectural principle stating that message routing and gateway infrastructure should remain minimal and stateless, keeping all business domain logic within the service boundaries.
* **Manifestation:** Lightweight reverse proxies (e.g., NGINX, Envoy) configured solely for TLS termination, rate limiting, and basic routing, without embedded domain scripts or transformation logic.

---

## 7. Practical Patterns and Examples

### Pattern 1: Strangler Fig Migration Pattern
* **Context:** Migrating functionality out of an existing monolithic application into new microservices incrementally without high-risk "big bang" rewrites.
* **Implementation Structure:**
  1. Place an interception/routing layer (e.g., reverse proxy or API gateway) in front of the monolith.
  2. Implement a new microservice for a specific bounded context extracted from the monolith.
  3. Route requests for that specific route/endpoint to the new microservice at the proxy layer.
  4. Gradually replace monolithic capabilities until the old monolith can be safely decommissioned.

### Pattern 2: Circuit Breaker with Fail-Fast & Fallback
* **Context:** Preventing cascading failures across synchronous inter-service calls when a downstream microservice experiences slowness or outages.
* **Implementation Structure:**
  1. Wrap outbound HTTP/gRPC requests in a circuit breaker state machine (Closed, Open, Half-Open).
  2. Track failure rates and response latency timeouts.
  3. When threshold is exceeded, trip breaker to `Open` state to immediately fail fast or return cached/degraded fallback data without making network calls.
  4. Periodically allow probe requests in `Half-Open` state to check downstream recovery.

### Pattern 3: Service Template / Paved Road Bootstrapping
* **Context:** Standardizing operational standards (logging, health checks, metrics, tracing) across polyglot or multi-team microservices without creating shared code coupling.
* **Implementation Structure:**
  1. Create a template repository containing pre-configured boilerplate for health checks (`/healthz`), structured JSON logging, Prometheus metrics endpoints, and correlation ID propagation middleware.
  2. Developers clone/copy the template when creating a new microservice rather than linking to a monolithic shared domain library.

---

## 8. Common Failure Modes and Risks

### Failure Mode 1: The Distributed Monolith
* **Description:** A system that has been split into multiple networked services, but due to tight coupling, shared libraries, or breaking API changes, all services must be built, tested, and deployed together in lockstep.
* **Root Cause:** Inappropriate service boundaries, shared domain code, shared databases, or reliance on synchronous call chains.

### Failure Mode 2: Shared Database / Content Coupling
* **Description:** Multiple microservices connecting directly to the same database instance or sharing SQL tables.
* **Root Cause:** Attempting microservices decomposition at the application layer while postponing database decomposition, resulting in hidden schema coupling and broken independent deployability.

### Failure Mode 3: Smart Pipes and Centralized Gateways
* **Description:** Embedding domain logic, request transformation, or orchestration scripts inside API Gateways or ESBs.
* **Root Cause:** Attempting to solve inter-service communication challenges by centralizing control, leading to organizational bottlenecks and brittle middleware.

### Failure Mode 4: Cascading Failures from Unbounded Retries & Missing Timeouts
* **Description:** A downstream outage causes upstream clients to retry requests aggressively without backoff or circuit breaking, exhausting server thread pools and crashing the entire ecosystem.
* **Root Cause:** Default infinite timeouts, naive retry loops, and lack of bulkheads or circuit breakers.

---

## 9. Repository-Audit Translation

### Static Checks (Automated Repo Inspection)
* **Checkable Attributes:**
  * File presence: Dedicated build configuration per repository (e.g., `Dockerfile`, `Makefile`, `CI pipeline definition`).
  * File absence: Absence of direct database connection strings pointing to external service databases.
  * Pattern matching: Presence of health check endpoints (`/healthz`, `/livez`, `/readyz`), structured logging configurations, and correlation ID HTTP headers.
  * Pattern matching: Protocol buffer definitions (`.proto`) or OpenAPI specifications (`openapi.yaml`) enforcing explicit service schemas.
  * Pattern matching: Inclusion of circuit breaker and timeout configurations in HTTP/gRPC client initialization.

### Human Review (Contextual Architecture Audits)
* **Subjective / Contextual Boundaries:**
  * Evaluating whether service boundaries correctly align with business bounded contexts vs. arbitrary technical layering.
  * Reviewing whether a Saga should use choreography vs. orchestration based on team ownership structures.
  * Assessing domain model maturity before splitting a monolith to prevent premature decomposition.

---

| Rule ID | Readiness | Target | Rationale |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |

| `MS-DATA-001` | `ready` | Database & App Config | Verifies that services do not contain shared database connection configurations pointing to external service DBs (`file_absent` / `pattern_match`). |

## 11. Suggested YAML Fragment

```yaml
# RepoScope Candidate Rules: Building Microservices (Sam Newman)
rules:
  - id: MS-BUILD-001
    name: Independent CI/CD Pipeline
    description: Enforce that every microservice repository contains its own isolated build and deployment definition.
    severity: error
    check:
      type: file_exists

        - "Dockerfile"
        - ".github/workflows/*.yml"
        - ".gitlab-ci.yml"
        - "Jenkinsfile"

  - id: MS-OBS-001

        - "src/**/*.py"
        - "src/**/*.go"
        - "src/**/*.java"
        - "src/**/*.ts"
        - "app/**/*.js"

  - id: MS-RES-001
    name: Explicit Network Timeout Configuration
    description: Prevent cascading failures by ensuring all outbound HTTP/RPC clients specify explicit timeout parameters.
    severity: warning
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      files:
        - "src/**/*.py"
        - "src/**/*.go"
        - "src/**/*.java"
        - "src/**/*.ts"
      pattern: "(timeout\s*=|Timeout\s*=|connect_timeout|read_timeout|http.Client\{\s*Timeout)"

  - id: MS-SCHEMA-001
    name: Explicit API Contract Definition
    description: Enforce that microservices define explicit schema contracts using OpenAPI, AsyncAPI, or Protocol Buffers.
    severity: error
    check:
      type: file_exists
      paths:
        - "openapi.yaml"
        - "openapi.json"
        - "api/v1/*.proto"
        - "asyncapi.yaml"

  - id: MS-DATA-001
    name: Prohibit Shared Monolithic Database Connections
    description: Prevent content coupling by disallowing direct cross-service database connection configuration strings.
    severity: error
    check:
      type: file_absent
      paths:
        - "config/shared_monolith_db.json"
        - "config/external_service_db.yml"
```
