===== FILE: docs/notes/webapp/clean-architecture.md =====
# RepoScope Knowledge Note: Clean Architecture

## 1. Source Identity
* **Title**: Clean Architecture: A Craftsman's Guide to Software Structure and Design
* **Author**: Robert C. Martin
* **Publication / Reference**: Prentice Hall (Robert C. Martin Series)
* **Domain Focus**: Software Architecture, Component Principles, SOLID Design Principles, Architectural Boundaries, and System Maintainability.

---

## 2. Scope
This Knowledge Note synthesizes the core architectural philosophy, structural component rules, and dependency management principles established in Robert C. Martin's Clean Architecture. It focuses on translating guidelines—such as the Dependency Rule, SOLID principles at the component level, Screaming Architecture, and the Humble Object pattern—into deterministic repository audit instructions. The scope encompasses identifying static code and configuration boundaries, separating business rules from delivery mechanisms, and defining automated checks versus human architectural reviews.

---

## 3. Source Spirit Card

| Field | Definition / Value |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Core Thesis** | Software architecture exists to minimize the human resources required to build and maintain a software system over its lifetime by enforcing strict dependency boundaries and keeping options open. |
| **Primary Goal** | Protect core business rules (Entities and Use Cases) from changes in peripheral details such as frameworks, databases, user interfaces, and external agencies. |
| **Architectural Philosophy** | Dependencies must always point inward toward higher-level policies (The Dependency Rule). High-level policies must not know about low-level delivery details or infrastructure. |
| **Primary Boundary Separation** | Inner Circle (Enterprise Entities & Application Use Cases) vs. Outer Circle (Interface Adapters, Controllers, Presenters, Gateways, Databases, Web Frameworks, and Devices). |
| **Component Cohesion Strategy** | Balance the tension between Reuse/Release Equivalence Principle (REP), Common Closure Principle (CCP), and Common Reuse Principle (CRP) based on project maturity. |
| **Component Coupling Strategy** | Enforce Acyclic Dependencies Principle (ADP), Stable Dependencies Principle (SDP: depend in direction of stability), and Stable Abstractions Principle (SAP: stability implies abstractness). |
| **Framework View** | Frameworks are useful tools but dangerous masters; they must be kept at arm's length as plugins rather than deeply married to core business entities. |
| **Testing Philosophy** | The architecture must be intrinsically testable in isolation; unit and use case tests should run fast without requiring live databases, web servers, or external frameworks. |
| **Database View** | The database is a low-level detail/mechanism, not the core of the application; business logic must operate against abstract gateway interfaces rather than database schemas or ORM models. |
| **Screaming Architecture Principle** | The directory structure and component layout of a project should proclaim its business domain and use cases, not the delivery framework. |

---

## 4. Source-Backed Structure

### Theme 1: SOLID Design Principles at the Architectural Scale
* **Single Responsibility Principle (SRP)**: A module/component should change for one, and only one, actor (person or stakeholder group).
* **Open-Closed Principle (OCP)**: Software artifacts must be open for extension but closed for modification; higher-level policies are shielded from lower-level detail changes.
* **Liskov Substitution Principle (LSP)**: Subtypes must conform to well-defined contracts so components can be substituted without breaking higher-level callers.
* **Interface Segregation Principle (ISP)**: Modules should avoid depending on methods or interfaces they do not use, preventing unwanted transitive re-compilations.
* **Dependency Inversion Principle (DIP)**: High-level policies must not depend on low-level details; both must depend on abstract interfaces, and details must depend on policies.

### Theme 2: Component Cohesion and Coupling Principles
* **Component Cohesion (REP, CCP, CRP)**:
  * **REP**: The unit of reuse is the unit of release.
  * **CCP**: Gather into components those classes that change for the same reasons and at the same times (SRP at component level).
  * **CRP**: Do not force users of a component to depend on things they do not need (ISP at component level).
* **Component Coupling (ADP, SDP, SAP)**:
  * **ADP**: Allow no cycles in the component dependency graph.
  * **SDP**: Depend in the direction of stability (instability metric should decrease along dependency arrows).
  * **SAP**: A component should be as abstract as it is stable (stable components must be abstract).

### Theme 3: The Clean Architecture & Architectural Boundaries
* **Concentric Layering**:
  * **Entities**: Enterprise-wide critical business rules and data.
  * **Use Cases**: Application-specific business rules that orchestrate entities.
  * **Interface Adapters**: Adapters converting data between use cases and external mechanisms (Controllers, Presenters, Gateways).
  * **Frameworks & Drivers**: Outermost details (Web, DB, Devices, UI).
* **Crossing Boundaries**: Data crossing boundaries must be simple Data Transfer Objects (DTOs) or primitive structs, never DB entity rows or framework-specific objects.
* **Flow of Control vs. Dependency Inversion**: When control flows outward (e.g., Use Case invoking a Presenter or DB), dependency inversion via boundary interfaces ensures source code dependencies still point inward.

### Theme 4: Decoupling Delivery Mechanisms, Persistence, and Frameworks
* **Database as a Detail**: Storage mechanisms (SQL, NoSQL, flat files) are details plugged in behind data access gateways.
* **Web as a Delivery Mechanism**: The web is an I/O device; business rules should be agnostic of whether input arrives via HTTP, CLI, gRPC, or message queues.
* **Keeping Frameworks at Arm's Length**: Framework annotations (e.g., ORM decorators or injection annotations) must not contaminate domain entity classes or use case interactors.
* **The Humble Object Pattern**: Split boundary logic into humble (hard to test, pure detail) and smart (easy to test, framework-free) objects.

---

## 5. Source-Backed Spirit

1. **What is the primary purpose of software architecture?**
   The primary purpose is to minimize the human resources required to build, maintain, and evolve a software system by maintaining structural flexibility and keeping options open for as long as possible.

2. **How does Clean Architecture enforce boundary separation?**
   By enforcing the Dependency Rule: source code dependencies must point strictly inward toward higher-level policies. Inner layers possess zero knowledge of outer layers, names, or data formats.

3. **Why are frameworks considered details rather than foundations?**
   Frameworks are created by third parties with goals distinct from the application's business requirements. Coupling domain code directly to frameworks creates an asymmetric marriage where framework updates break core business logic.

4. **How should a repository's directory structure reflect its purpose?**
   According to Screaming Architecture, top-level directories and packages should immediately tell the reader what the business domain does (e.g., `orders`, `billing`, `inventory`) rather than how it is delivered (e.g., `controllers`, `models`, `views`).

5. **What is the relationship between testability and architecture?**
   An architecture is clean only if its core use cases and entities can be thoroughly tested in isolation without booting up web servers, connecting to real databases, or initializing external frameworks.

6. **How are boundary crossings managed without violating the Dependency Rule?**
   Through dynamic polymorphism and the Dependency Inversion Principle. Inner layers define input/output boundary interfaces, and outer layers implement or invoke them, allowing control flow to exit while source dependencies point inward.

---

## 6. Key Concepts

### 1. The Dependency Rule
* **Definition**: The fundamental rule stating that source code dependencies must point exclusively inward toward higher-level policies and enterprise business rules.
* **Repository Manifestation**: Import statements in domain/entity modules must never reference outer packages like HTTP handlers, database mappers, ORM frameworks, or web frameworks.

### 2. Entities & Critical Business Rules
* **Definition**: Objects embodying critical business data and associated logic that would exist even if the software system were executed manually on paper.
* **Repository Manifestation**: Plain language classes (e.g., POJOs, Dataclasses, pure structs) devoid of ORM decorators, framework imports, or HTTP request dependencies.

### 3. Use Cases & Interactors
* **Definition**: Application-specific business logic that orchestrates the flow of data to and from Entities to achieve a specific user or system goal.
* **Repository Manifestation**: Dedicated service or interactor classes that accept primitive request DTOs and return primitive response DTOs via explicit boundary interfaces.

### 4. Boundary Interfaces (Input/Output Ports)
* **Definition**: Abstract interfaces defined by the use case layer that decouple interactors from controllers (input) and presenters/gateways (output).
* **Repository Manifestation**: Explicit interface declarations in the application layer that are implemented by presenter classes or repository adapters in infrastructure modules.

### 5. The Humble Object Pattern
* **Definition**: A design pattern that separates behaviors that are difficult to test (such as GUI views or database access code) into humble wrapper objects, extracting all testable logic into framework-free presenters or interactors.
* **Repository Manifestation**: Light controllers that merely extract parameters and invoke interactors, and presenters that convert interactor DTOs into view models without business logic.

### 6. Screaming Architecture
* **Definition**: A structural organization where the top-level directories and packages clearly declare the domain intent and use cases of the application rather than technical layer categories.
* **Repository Manifestation**: Project structures organized by domain features (`order/`, `payment/`, `user/`) or explicit use case folders (`create_order/`, `process_payment/`) rather than generic `views/`, `controllers/`, `models/`.

### 7. Main Component (The Ultimate Detail)
* **Definition**: The lowest-level, dirtiest component in the architecture whose sole purpose is to instantiate factories, wire up dependency injection, and pass control to high-level policies.
* **Repository Manifestation**: A centralized bootstrap file (e.g., `main.go`, `index.ts`, `app.py`, `Application.java`) where framework wiring, container initialization, and route bindings reside.

---

## 7. Practical Patterns and Examples

### Pattern 1: Dependency Inversion for Persistence (Data Access Gateway)
* **Description**: The Use Case layer defines a repository/gateway interface for loading and saving entities. The actual ORM or database access implementation resides in an outer infrastructure package and implements the interface.
* **Structure Example**:
  ```
  core/
    domain/
      entity/
        order.go
    usecase/
      port/
        order_repository.go  <-- Abstract interface defined here
      create_order.go        <-- Interactor depends on order_repository
  infrastructure/
    persistence/
      postgres_order_repo.go <-- Implements port.OrderRepository
  ```

### Pattern 2: DTO Boundary Crossing
* **Description**: Passing raw database rows, ORM active records, or HTTP request objects across architectural boundaries is strictly prohibited. Use cases take primitive request DTOs and return response DTOs.
* **Structure Example**:
  ```python
  # Core Use Case Layer (No ORM / No Web Framework)
  @dataclass(frozen=True)
  class CreateUserRequestDTO:
      email: str
      full_name: str

  class CreateUserUseCase:
      def __init__(self, user_repo: UserRepositoryPort):
          self.user_repo = user_repo

      def execute(self, request: CreateUserRequestDTO) -> CreateUserResponseDTO:
          # Business logic executed purely on domain entities
          pass
  ```

### Pattern 3: Framework Isolation via Main Bootstrap
* **Description**: Keep framework dependencies (such as Spring annotations, FastAPI, or Express) isolated in the entry point module or explicit configuration classes, preventing annotation pollution in core domain logic.
* **Structure Example**:
  ```typescript
  // main.ts - Bootstrap entrypoint (Dirtiest component)
  import { PostgresUserRepository } from './infrastructure/PostgresUserRepository';
  import { CreateUserUseCase } from './core/usecase/CreateUserUseCase';
  import { UserController } from './delivery/http/UserController';

  const userRepo = new PostgresUserRepository(process.env.DATABASE_URL);
  const createUserUseCase = new CreateUserUseCase(userRepo);
  const userController = new UserController(createUserUseCase);
  // Wire up HTTP routes...
  ```

---

## 8. Common Failure Modes and Risks

1. **Framework Encroachment (The Asymmetric Marriage)**:
   * *Description*: Allowing framework decorators, base classes, or ORM field annotations to permeate domain entities.
   * *Risk*: Updating or replacing the framework breaks core enterprise business rules and forces massive re-testing.

2. **The Leaky Database abstraction (Active Record Overuse)**:
   * *Description*: Passing ORM models directly from the database layer into controllers, views, or business logic.
   * *Risk*: Changes in database schemas ripple through the entire codebase, coupling business rules directly to database storage structures.

3. **Anemic Domain Models with Fat Controllers**:
   * *Description*: Stripping business rules out of Entities and placing business validation, calculations, and orchestration inside HTTP controllers.
   * *Risk*: Business rules become untestable without simulating HTTP request/response objects, leading to slow and fragile integration tests.

4. **Cyclic Package Dependencies**:
   * *Description*: Allowing domain logic packages to import infrastructure or delivery packages, creating cycles in the component graph.
   * *Risk*: Violates ADP, prevents independent compilation and deployment of components, and triggers build issues.

---

## 9. Repository-Audit Translation

### Static Checks (Automated)
* **Domain Layer Isolation**: Verify that files within domain/entity directories do not contain import statements referencing web frameworks (Express, Spring MVC, FastAPI, Django), ORM libraries (Hibernate, SQLAlchemy, Prisma), or delivery protocols.
* **Circular Dependency Detection**: Verify that module dependency graphs contain no cycles across packages.
* **Unit Test Isolation**: Verify that unit tests for core domain logic do not require database connections, environment variables for external services, or HTTP server initialization.
* **Architecture Rules Config Presence**: Check for static analysis configuration files (e.g., ArchUnit rules, Dependency-Cruiser configs, Import-Linter rules) that enforce package boundary boundaries at build time.

### Human Review (Manual Architectural Audit)
* **Screaming Architecture Evaluation**: Assess whether top-level folder naming reflects domain use cases or generic technical layers.
* **Appropriateness of Boundary Layers**: Evaluate whether small projects are over-engineered with premature horizontal boundaries or if large projects lack necessary interface seams.
* **Humble Object Adherence**: Check whether controllers and UI views are truly humble or if they contain hidden business validation and calculations.
* **Domain Entity Cohesion**: Review whether entities embody critical business data and behavior together or if they are mere data structures manipulated by monolithic services.

---

## 10. Candidate Rules

| Rule ID | Statement / Description | Check Type | Target File / Pattern | Status |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `clean_arch_import_linter_exists` | Verify that an architectural linter configuration file exists to enforce package dependency boundaries. | `file_exists` | `.importlinter` OR `.dependency-cruiser.js` OR `archunit.xml` OR `depcover.json` | `ready` |
| `clean_arch_domain_no_orm_import` | Verify that core domain entity files do not import ORM or persistence frameworks directly. | `pattern_match` | `domain/**/*.py` (disallowing `sqlalchemy`, `django.db`, `peewee`) | `ready` |
| `clean_arch_domain_no_web_import` | Verify that core domain entity/usecase files do not import HTTP or web delivery frameworks. | `pattern_match` | `domain/**/*.ts` OR `core/**/*.go` (disallowing `express`, `fastapi`, `gin`, `net/http`) | `ready` |
| `clean_arch_usecase_test_exists` | Verify that a dedicated test suite directory exists for unit testing use cases without external dependencies. | `file_exists` | `tests/unit/` OR `test/usecase/` OR `spec/domain/` | `ready` |
| `clean_arch_no_direct_db_in_domain` | Verify that domain files do not contain raw database manipulation statements. | `pattern_match` | `domain/**` (disallowing direct database manipulation) | `ready` |
| `clean_arch_boundary_interface_check` | Verify that usecase interactors depend on repository interface abstractions rather than concrete DB implementations. | `needs-review` | Core interactor constructor signatures | `needs-review` |
| `clean_arch_screaming_structure_eval` | Evaluate whether top-level directory layout screams domain intent over framework mechanisms. | `blocked` | Full directory tree evaluation | `blocked` |
| `clean_arch_humble_controller_eval` | Verify that HTTP controllers contain no domain calculations or business rule validations. | `blocked` | Controller method implementations | `blocked` |

---

## 11. Suggested YAML Fragment

```yaml
rules:
  - id: clean_arch_import_linter_exists
    description: "Ensure a static architectural linter configuration file exists to enforce Clean Architecture boundary constraints."
    severity: error
    check_type: file_exists
    files:
      - ".importlinter"
      - ".dependency-cruiser.js"
      - "dependency-cruiser.json"
      - "archunit.xml"
      - ".depguard.json"

  - id: clean_arch_domain_no_orm_import
    description: "Ensure core domain entities do not import ORM frameworks directly."
    severity: error
    check_type: pattern_match
    target_files:
      - "src/domain/**/*.py"
      - "core/entities/**/*.py"
    disallowed_patterns:
      - "import_sqlalchemy"
      - "from_sqlalchemy"
      - "from_django_db"
      - "import_peewee"

  - id: clean_arch_domain_no_web_import
    description: "Ensure core domain entities and use cases do not import web delivery frameworks."
    severity: error
    check_type: pattern_match
    target_files:
      - "src/domain/**/*.ts"
      - "src/usecase/**/*.ts"
      - "core/domain/**/*.go"
    disallowed_patterns:
      - "from_express"
      - "import_express"
      - "gin_gonic"
      - "net_http"

  - id: clean_arch_usecase_test_exists
    description: "Ensure a dedicated unit test directory exists for testing domain and use case logic in isolation."
    severity: warning
    check_type: file_exists
    files:
      - "tests/unit"
      - "test/unit"
      - "spec/domain"
      - "src/domain/__tests__"

  - id: clean_arch_no_direct_db_in_domain
    description: "Ensure core domain logic contains no hardcoded raw queries."
    severity: error
    check_type: pattern_match
    target_files:
      - "src/domain/**/*"
      - "core/domain/**/*"
    disallowed_patterns:
      - "raw_sql_query"
      - "direct_db_exec"
```

# RepoScope Knowledge Note: Continuous Delivery

## 1. Source Identity
* **Title:** Continuous Delivery: Reliable Software Releases through Build, Test, and Deployment Automation
* **Authors:** Jez Humble, David Farley
* **Publication Year:** 2010
* **Domain / Subject Area:** Software Engineering, DevOps, Release Engineering, Build & Deployment Automation
* **Primary Architectural Focus:** Deployment Pipeline Architecture, Continuous Integration, Automated Testing, Configuration Management, and Low-Risk Release Strategies

---

## 2. Scope
* **In-Scope:**
  * Automated deployment pipeline design from version control commit to production release.
  * Comprehensive configuration management (code, infrastructure, environment configs, schema migrations).
  * Continuous Integration (CI) mechanics, automated testing strategy, and feedback loops.
  * Deployment and release strategies (blue-green deployments, canary releases, feature toggles, dark launching).
  * Automated infrastructure management and environment provisioning.
* **Out-of-Scope:**
  * Specific cloud vendor API implementations or proprietary orchestration tool syntaxes.
  * Product management frameworks and backlog prioritization methodologies.
  * Low-level application programming algorithms or framework-specific coding conventions.

---

## 3. Source Spirit Card
1. **Primary Thesis:** Software delivery should be a routine, reliable, predictable, and low-risk process achieved through total automation of the build, test, provision, and deployment pipeline.
2. **Core Quality Attribute:** Deployability and Release Cycle Time (Shrinking the time from code commit to running production software while preserving operational stability).
3. **Primary Anti-Pattern:** Manual deployment steps, out-of-band environment configuration changes ("snowflake servers"), and infrequent, massive release deployments.
4. **Core Architectural Trade-off:** Upfront investment in automated testing and pipeline infrastructure vs. ongoing manual testing, delayed feedback, and catastrophic release failures.
5. **Definition of Done:** Software is checked into version control, fully validated through an automated deployment pipeline, and proven to be in a demonstrably deployable state in a production-like environment.
6. **Non-Negotiable Practice:** Every change (application code, database schemas, scripts, configuration settings, environment definitions) must be stored in version control.
7. **Verification Boundary:** Automated static analysis, unit tests, acceptance tests, performance tests, and deployment smoke tests running inside the deployment pipeline.
8. **Human vs Machine Split:** Machines execute all building, testing, provisioning, and deployment tasks; humans make strategic release decisions and conduct high-level exploratory testing and security auditing.
9. **Governance Philosophy:** Fast feedback, continuous visibility, and automated compliance auditing embedded directly within the deployment process.
10. **Evolution Strategy:** Incremental, zero-downtime releases utilizing automated database migrations, trunk-based development, and feature toggles.

---

## 4. Source-Backed Structure
1. **Foundations of Delivery:**
   * Configuration management for code, environment, and data.
   * Continuous Integration as a disciplined developer practice.
   * Testing strategies across the automated pipeline.
2. **The Deployment Pipeline:**
   * Anatomy and stages of the deployment pipeline (Commit stage, Automated Acceptance test stage, Performance & Security stage, Manual testing & Production release).
   * Artifact management and single-pass build binaries.
   * Environment management and deployment scripting.
3. **Deployment Strategies:**
   * Zero-downtime release mechanics (Blue-Green, Canary).
   * Feature toggles, branch by abstraction, and dark launching.
   * Automated rollback and forward-recovery mechanisms.
4. **Data and Infrastructure Management:**
   * Database change management and automated schema migration scripts.
   * Infrastructure as Code (IaC) and configuration baselines.
   * Managing non-functional requirements (NFRs) and operational compliance.

---

## 5. Source-Backed Spirit
1. **What is the fundamental goal of architecture according to this text?**
   To make releasing software a repeatable, reliable, and routine process with minimal human intervention and minimal cycle time.
2. **How should teams manage change and evolution?**
   Through continuous small-batch integrations directly into trunk/main, supported by comprehensive automated test suites and incremental database migrations.
3. **What is the relationship between application code and operational infrastructure?**
   Infrastructure and environment specifications must be treated as code, version-controlled, and provisioned automatically in an identical manner across all environments.
4. **How are risk and security managed in the delivery lifecycle?**
   By automating security and compliance checks early in the pipeline and eliminating manual production access through automated pipeline deployments.
5. **What constitutes a valid build artifact?**
   A single, immutable binary compiled once during the commit stage and promoted unchanged through subsequent test and deployment environments.
6. **Where should configuration state reside?**
   Outside the application binary, stored in version control or a central configuration provider, and injected dynamically into environments at deploy time.

---

## 6. Key Concepts
1. **Deployment Pipeline:**
   * *Definition:* An automated manifestation of the process for taking software from version control into the hands of end users.
   * *Repo Manifestation:* CI/CD configuration files (e.g., GitHub Actions workflows, GitLab CI definitions, Jenkinsfiles) defining multi-stage build, test, and release jobs.
2. **Immutable Build Artifact:**
   * *Definition:* A compiled application binary, container image, or package built exactly once and promoted through testing to production without re-compilation.
   * *Repo Manifestation:* Dockerfiles, container registry publishing configs, or build scripts producing version-tagged artifact packages.
3. **Trunk-Based Development:**
   * *Definition:* A version control management practice where developers merge small, frequent updates into a single main branch, avoiding long-lived feature branches.
   * *Repo Manifestation:* Branch protection rules, lack of persistent feature branches, and frequent commit activity directly against main/trunk.
4. **Infrastructure as Code (IaC):**
   * *Definition:* Managing and provisioning computing infrastructure through machine-readable definition files rather than interactive configuration tools.
   * *Repo Manifestation:* Terraform templates, Ansible playbooks, CloudFormation manifests, or Kubernetes YAML files stored alongside source code.
5. **Evolutionary Database Migration:**
   * *Definition:* Applying incremental, versioned schema change scripts automatically as part of the deployment process.
   * *Repo Manifestation:* Sequential SQL migration files managed by tools like Flyway, Liquibase, or ORM migration suites (e.g., `migrations/V1__init.sql`).
6. **Feature Toggles (Feature Flags):**
   * *Definition:* A technique enabling feature activation or deactivation at runtime without deploying new application code.
   * *Repo Manifestation:* Configuration files or code modules referencing feature flag evaluations to conditionally execute code paths.

---

## 7. Practical Patterns and Examples
1. **Blue-Green Deployment Pattern:**
   * *Context:* Releasing new application versions without downtime or traffic disruption.
   * *Implementation:* Maintaining two identical production environments (Blue and Green). Traffic routes to Blue; Green is deployed and validated, then router configuration switches traffic to Green.
   * *Audit Criteria:* Router/Load Balancer scripts or Kubernetes service deployment manifests specifying dual target groups or active/standby services.
2. **Single Pass Binary Build Pattern:**
   * *Context:* Ensuring that the exact code tested in lower environments is what runs in production.
   * *Implementation:* Build step produces a single Docker container or ZIP package; subsequent pipeline steps pull and test this exact artifact without rebuilding.
   * *Audit Criteria:* CI configuration showing a dedicated `build` job that stores artifacts, followed by `test` and `deploy` jobs referencing the built artifact.
3. **Decoupled Database Migration Pattern:**
   * *Context:* Preventing schema changes from breaking running instances during zero-downtime deployments.
   * *Implementation:* Expanding the schema (adding nullable columns/tables) in step 1, deploying application code using new schema in step 2, and contracting old schema elements in step 3.
   * *Audit Criteria:* Schema migration scripts adhering to non-destructive changes (no direct `DROP COLUMN` without multi-phase deprecation).

---

## 8. Common Failure Modes and Risks
1. **Snowflake Servers (Configuration Drift):**
   * *Symptom:* Environments exhibit subtle behavioral differences; deployments pass in staging but fail in production due to manual server tweaks.
   * *Root Cause:* Manual configuration edits directly on production or staging servers without updating version-controlled IaC templates.
   * *Detection Method:* Absence of automated provisioning scripts; presence of manual SSH access logs or untracked environment variables.
2. **Rebuilding Artifacts Per Environment:**
   * *Symptom:* Pipeline compiles source code separately for Staging, QA, and Production using environment-specific flags.
   * *Root Cause:* Baking environment-specific configurations directly into application binaries during compilation.
   * *Detection Method:* CI pipeline definitions invoking `build` or `compile` commands inside environment-specific deployment jobs.
3. **Committed Secrets and Credentials:**
   * *Symptom:* API keys, database passwords, or SSL private keys checked directly into git repositories.
   * *Root Cause:* Hardcoding environment secrets for developer convenience.
   * *Detection Method:* Pattern match checks scanning repo files for private key blocks, connection strings, or plain-text passwords.
4. **Long-Lived Feature Branches:**
   * *Symptom:* Massive, painful merge conflicts, delayed integration feedback, and integration hell before release.
   * *Root Cause:* Developers working in isolation on feature branches for weeks or months without merging to trunk.
   * *Detection Method:* Git commit history showing stale feature branches or pull requests remaining open for extended periods.

---

## 9. Repository-Audit Translation
* **Static Checks (Automated Pipeline Audits):**
  * Verify existence of pipeline configuration files (e.g., `.github/workflows/*.yml`, `.gitlab-ci.yml`).
  * Verify existence of IaC provisioning manifests (e.g., `main.tf`, `Dockerfile`, `k8s/`).
  * Verify existence of version-controlled database migration directory (e.g., `db/migrations/`).
  * Verify absence of hardcoded plain-text credentials and private keys across all source files.
  * Verify presence of automated health-check endpoints in application configuration.
* **Human Review (Contextual & Architectural Audits):**
  * Review test suite coverage and quality beyond simple pass/fail execution.
  * Assess organizational culture surrounding zero-downtime deployment practices and rollback readiness.
  * Evaluate emergency release procedures and incident post-mortem feedback loops.

---

## 10. Candidate Rules

| Rule Identifier | Description | Target | Check Type | Status |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `cd-pipeline-defined` | Ensure repository contains a version-controlled CI/CD pipeline definition | `.github/workflows/` | `file_exists` | `ready` |
| `container-spec-exists` | Ensure containerized application packaging specification is present | `Dockerfile` | `file_exists` | `ready` |
| `db-migrations-present` | Ensure database schema changes are managed via versioned migration scripts | `db/migrations/` | `file_exists` | `ready` |
| `no-committed-private-keys` | Ensure no RSA or PEM private keys are committed into version control | `**/*` | `pattern_match` | `ready` |
| `health-check-endpoint-configured` | Ensure application exposes an automated readiness/liveness probe route | `src/` | `pattern_match` | `ready` |
| `env-specific-configs-separated` | Verify environment configurations are loaded dynamically from environment variables | `config/` | `needs-review` | `needs-review` |
| `zero-downtime-release-strategy` | Evaluate deployment readiness for seamless active-pass deployment transitions | `infra/` | `needs-review` | `needs-review` |

---

## 11. Suggested YAML Fragment

```yaml
rules:
  - name: cd-pipeline-defined
    description: Verifies that the repository defines an automated CI/CD deployment pipeline.
    severity: high
    check_type: file_exists
    params:
      paths:
        - ".github/workflows"
        - ".gitlab-ci.yml"
        - "Jenkinsfile"

  - name: container-spec-exists
    description: Verifies that the repository contains a Dockerfile for reproducible container packaging.
    severity: medium
    check_type: file_exists
    params:
      paths:
        - "Dockerfile"
        - "Containerfile"

  - name: db-migrations-present
    description: Ensures database changes are stored as versioned schema migration files.
    severity: high
    check_type: file_exists
    params:
      paths:
        - "db/migrations"
        - "migrations"
        - "src/main/resources/db/migration"

  - name: no-committed-private-keys
    description: Scans source code and configuration files for accidentally committed PEM/RSA private keys.
    severity: critical
    check_type: pattern_match
    params:
      patterns:
        - "-----BEGIN RSA PRIVATE KEY-----"
        - "-----BEGIN EC PRIVATE KEY-----"
        - "-----BEGIN PRIVATE KEY-----"
      exclude_paths:
        - "tests/fixtures/**"

  - name: health-check-endpoint-configured
    description: Ensures application code implements a health check endpoint for deployment verification.
    severity: medium
    check_type: pattern_match
    params:
      patterns:
        - "/healthz"
        - "/actuator/health"
        - "/health"
        - "healthcheck"
```

# RepoScope Knowledge Note: Designing Web APIs

## 1. Source Identity
* **Title**: Designing Web APIs: Building APIs That Developers Love
* **Authors**: Brenda Jin, Saurabh Sahni, and Amir Shevat
* **Publication Focus**: Architectural design, API paradigm selection, developer experience (DX), authorization standards, rate limiting, and long-term lifecycle management for web APIs.
* **Primary Target Audience**: Web API architects, backend software engineers, platform engineering leads, and technical product managers.

---

## 2. Scope
* **In-Scope**:
  * API paradigm evaluation (REST, RPC, GraphQL, WebHooks, WebSockets).
  * HTTP method semantics, URL design, and status code standardization.
  * API Security: OAuth 2.0 framework, HMAC signature validation for WebHooks, and rate limiting.
  * API design workflows, OpenAPI/Swagger specification definitions, and developer documentation.
  * Change management, API versioning strategies, and deprecation lifecycles.
* **Out-of-Scope**:
  * Lower-level network protocol details (e.g., TCP/IP packet optimization, TLS handshake cipher suites).
  * Vendor-specific cloud infrastructure setup (e.g., AWS API Gateway specific IAM policies).
  * Database schema indexing and query tuning internals behind API handlers.

---

## 3. Source Spirit Card
1. **Core Architectural Thesis**: Web APIs are primary developer products; their design must prioritize clarity, predictability, security, and developer experience to drive platform adoption and maintain long-term stability.
2. **Primary Optimization Objective**: Minimize integration friction and developer cognitive load while ensuring backward compatibility and operational resilience.
3. **Primary System Quality Attribute**: Predictability and Usability (Developer Experience).
4. **Primary Failure Metric**: Breaking API changes that silently corrupt client integrations or unhandled rate limits leading to cascading system outages.
5. **Architectural Style / Paradigm**: Pragmatic Resource-Oriented REST, Event-Driven WebHooks, and Structured RPC/GraphQL where appropriate.
6. **Coupling Philosophy**: Loose coupling through strict adherence to standardized HTTP protocols, open API specifications, and explicit scope-based authorization.
7. **Change Management Principle**: Non-breaking additive changes preferred; explicit URL or header versioning for breaking structural shifts with deprecation headers.
8. **Security Stance**: Defense-in-depth with centralized OAuth 2.0 access tokens, cryptographic WebHook signatures, and strict per-client rate limiting.
9. **Observability Approach**: Comprehensive API usage logging, rate-limit header exposure, and centralized API documentation portals.
10. **Trade-off Philosophy**: Prefer standardized consistency and explicit contracts over clever, bespoke protocol optimizations.

---

## 4. Source-Backed Structure
The core themes established throughout the text include:

1. **API Paradigms and Protocol Selection**: Evaluating REST, RPC, GraphQL, WebHooks, and WebSockets based on communication patterns, payload efficiency, and event handling requirements.
2. **Resource Design and URL Hierarchy**: Structuring intuitive endpoint hierarchies, mapping business domain entities to plural nouns, and standardizing HTTP verbs (`GET`, `POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH`, `DELETE`).
3. **API Security and Access Control**: Implementing token-based authentication (OAuth 2.0), granular scopes, rate limiting counters, and HMAC request validation.
4. **API Lifecycle, Specifications, and Documentation**: Establishing machine-readable API contracts (OpenAPI/Swagger), mocking endpoints, versioning models, and managing deprecation timelines.

---

## 5. Source-Backed Spirit
1. **What is the central problem this source attempts to solve?**
   * The proliferation of ad-hoc, inconsistent, and poorly documented HTTP endpoints that cause integration bugs, break consumer applications during updates, and introduce security vulnerabilities.
2. **How does this source define architectural success?**
   * Success is an API that developers can integrate intuitively without reading long documentation, backed by explicit machine-readable specifications, robust rate limiting, and zero unannounced breaking changes.
3. **What trade-offs does this source explicitly advocate?**
   * Prefers protocol standardization (e.g., OpenAPI specs, OAuth 2.0) over custom internal frameworks, even if custom frameworks appear faster to build initially.
4. **What practices does this source consider fundamentally harmful?**
   * Introducing breaking schema shifts on existing endpoints without versioning, relying on undocumented side effects, and failing to sign outgoing WebHook payloads.
5. **How should systems evolve over time according to this source?**
   * Evolve additively by introducing optional fields and new endpoints. When breaking changes are unavoidable, introduce a new API version path and run deprecation windows with HTTP warnings.
6. **What is the source's attitude toward developer ergonomics vs. operational convenience?**
   * Developer ergonomics take precedence in contract design; the internal server implementation must adapt to provide a clean, predictable API surface for the client.

---

## 6. Key Concepts
1. **OpenAPI Specification (OAS)**:
   * *Definition*: A vendor-neutral, machine-readable format (YAML/JSON) for describing RESTful APIs, enabling automated documentation, client SDK generation, and mock servers.
   * *Manifestation*: Central spec file (e.g., `openapi.yaml`) stored in the repository root or contract directory.
2. **OAuth 2.0 Scopes**:
   * *Definition*: Fine-grained access permissions attached to OAuth access tokens that restrict a client's access to specific API endpoints or resources.
   * *Manifestation*: Authorization middleware validating `scope` claims on HTTP request context (e.g., `read:users`, `write:orders`).
3. **HMAC WebHook Signatures**:
   * *Definition*: A cryptographic message authentication code computed using a shared secret and request payload to ensure WebHook authenticity and integrity.
   * *Manifestation*: Request headers containing SHA-256 signatures (e.g., `X-Signature-256`) verified by client receiver endpoints.
4. **Rate Limiting and Throttling**:
   * *Definition*: Mechanism to control the rate of incoming API traffic per client key or IP address to prevent service abuse and denial-of-service conditions.
   * *Manifestation*: Middleware exposing standard response headers (`X-RateLimit-Limit`, `X-RateLimit-Remaining`, `X-RateLimit-Reset`) and returning status `429 Too Many Requests`.
5. **Idempotency Keys**:
   * *Definition*: Unique headers passed with non-idempotent HTTP requests (`POST`) allowing servers to recognize duplicate operations safely without repeating execution.
   * *Manifestation*: Middleware checking `Idempotency-Key` headers against a distributed key-value store before handler invocation.
6. **API Versioning Strategies**:
   * *Definition*: Explicit mechanisms (URL path `/v1/`, custom media types, or headers) used to route clients to specific API schema versions.
   * *Manifestation*: Versioned router mounts or controller directory structures (`/api/v1/`, `/api/v2/`).

---

## 7. Practical Patterns and Examples
1. **Standardized Resource Endpoint Hierarchy**:
   * *Description*: Modeling domain resources with plural nouns and nesting child resources logically while limiting URI nesting depth to at most two levels.
   * *Example Pattern*:
     * `GET /v1/organizations` (List organizations)
     * `POST /v1/organizations` (Create organization)
     * `GET /v1/organizations/{org_id}/members` (List members within an organization)
2. **WebHook Delivery with Exponential Backoff**:
   * *Description*: Retrying failed outgoing WebHook deliveries with exponential backoff delays and cryptographic signature headers.
   * *Example Pattern*:
     * WebHook payloads include `X-Hub-Signature-256: sha256=<hmac_hex>`.
     * Worker queue retries failed `5xx` deliveries at intervals: 10s, 30s, 2m, 10m, 1h.
3. **Deprecation Header and Sunsetting Pattern**:
   * *Description*: Communicating upcoming endpoint deprecation using standard HTTP headers without immediately disabling the endpoint.
   * *Example Pattern*:
     * HTTP response headers include `Deprecation: true` and `Sunset: Wed, 11 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT`.

---

## 8. Common Failure Modes and Risks
1. **Breaking Schema Changes in Minor Releases**:
   * *Risk*: Removing fields, changing data types, or adding required request parameters to existing endpoints breaks existing client applications.
   * *Impact*: High client outage risk and damaged API reputation.
2. **Missing Rate Limiting**:
   * *Risk*: Endpoints exposed to the internet without request counters are vulnerable to brute-force attacks and resource exhaustion.
   * *Impact*: Service instability and unexpected infrastructure cost spikes.
3. **Unsigned WebHook Callbacks**:
   * *Risk*: Sending WebHook events without HMAC signatures allows attackers to spoof events and trigger unauthorized actions in client applications.
   * *Impact*: Severe security vulnerabilities in downstream integrated systems.
4. **Monolithic Undocumented Endpoints**:
   * *Risk*: Building ad-hoc endpoints without an OpenAPI spec leads to drift between server code, SDKs, and developer documentation.
   * *Impact*: Increased integration friction, developer frustration, and high support overhead.

---

## 9. Repository-Audit Translation

### Static Checks
* **OpenAPI Spec Presence**: Verify the existence of `openapi.yaml` or `swagger.json` in contract or repository root directories.
* **Route Path Structure**: Audit API router definition files for explicit versioning prefixes (e.g., `/v1/` or `/v2/`).
* **Rate Limiting Middleware Registration**: Inspect HTTP router setup files to confirm rate limiting middleware is mounted on public routes.
* **WebHook Signature Handlers**: Check outgoing WebHook dispatch code for HMAC key signature calculation.

### Human Review
* **API Domain Model Ergonomics**: Evaluate whether URI resource names accurately reflect business domain concepts rather than underlying database table structures.
* **Error Message Usability**: Review API error response payloads to ensure they provide helpful human-readable guidance and machine-readable error codes without leaking system stack traces.
* **Granular Scope Design**: Assess whether OAuth scope definitions provide sufficient isolation between read, write, and administrative actions.

---

## 10. Candidate Rules

| Rule ID | Rule Description | Check Type | Target Artifacts | Status |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `api_spec_file_exists` | Require an OpenAPI / Swagger specification file in the codebase. | `file_exists` | `openapi.yaml`, `openapi.json`, `swagger.yaml` | `ready` |
| `api_versioning_in_routes` | Require API route paths or controllers to include version prefix patterns. | `pattern_match` | `src/routes/**`, `src/controllers/**` | `ready` |
| `rate_limiting_middleware_present` | Ensure rate limiting middleware or annotations are configured on public HTTP endpoints. | `pattern_match` | `src/app.*`, `src/main.*`, `src/routes/**` | `ready` |
| `webhook_hmac_signing_present` | Require outgoing WebHook delivery code to compute and attach HMAC signatures. | `pattern_match` | `src/services/webhook*`, `src/jobs/webhook*` | `ready` |
| `no_raw_stacktrace_in_responses` | Prohibit printing raw exception stack traces in API response handlers. | `pattern_match` | `src/controllers/**`, `src/middleware/error.*` | `ready` |
| `oauth_scope_validation_check` | Verify that access tokens are validated for scope permissions in route handlers. | `pattern_match` | `src/middleware/auth.*`, `src/routes/**` | `needs-review` |

---

## 11. Suggested YAML Fragment

```yaml
rules:
  - id: api_spec_file_exists
    description: "Verify that an OpenAPI specification file exists in the repository."
    severity: "error"
    check:
      type: "file_exists"
      paths:
        - "openapi.yaml"
        - "openapi.json"
        - "swagger.yaml"
        - "docs/openapi.yaml"
        - "api/openapi.yaml"

  - id: api_versioning_in_routes
    description: "Ensure API endpoints incorporate explicit version prefixes (e.g., /v1/ or /v2/)."
    severity: "warning"
    check:
      type: "pattern_match"
      files:
        - "src/routes/**/*.js"
        - "src/routes/**/*.ts"
        - "src/controllers/**/*.go"
        - "src/api/**/*.py"
      pattern: "(/api/v[0-9]+|/v[0-9]+)"

  - id: rate_limiting_middleware_present
    description: "Ensure rate limiting middleware is mounted on public API route handlers."
    severity: "error"
    check:
      type: "pattern_match"
      files:
        - "src/app.ts"
        - "src/app.js"
        - "src/server.go"
        - "src/main.py"
      pattern: "(rateLimit|RateLimiter|throttle|ThrottleRequests)"

  - id: webhook_hmac_signing_present
    description: "Verify that WebHook dispatchers compute HMAC signatures for payload integrity."
    severity: "error"
    check:
      type: "pattern_match"
      files:
        - "src/**/*.ts"
        - "src/**/*.js"
        - "src/**/*.go"
        - "src/**/*.py"
      pattern: "(createHmac|hmac\.New|X-Signature|X-Hub-Signature)"

  - id: no_raw_stacktrace_in_responses
    description: "Prohibit returning raw internal error stack traces in API responses."
    severity: "error"
    check:
      type: "pattern_match"
      files:
        - "src/middleware/error.*"
        - "src/controllers/**/*"
      pattern: "(err\.stack|traceback\.format_exc|debug_backtrace)"
```

# RepoScope Knowledge Note: Production-Ready Microservices

## 1. Source Identity
* **Title**: Production-Ready Microservices: Building Standardized Systems Across an Engineering Organization
* **Author**: Susan J. Fowler
* **Publisher**: O'Reilly Media (2016)
* **Primary Focus**: Establishing organization-wide standards for microservice production readiness across stability, scalability, fault tolerance, monitoring, documentation, and catastrophe preparedness.

---

## 2. Scope
* **Target Audience**: Software architects, platform engineers, site reliability engineers, and engineering managers standardizing microservice architectures across multi-team engineering organizations.
* **Applicability Domain**: Distributed systems, microservices infrastructure, platform engineering, service ecosystem governance, and operational readiness audits.
* **Exclusions**: Broad monolithic application development, detailed language-specific syntax, deep network hardware engineering, and low-level kernel tuning.

---

## 3. Source Spirit Card
* **Core Thesis**: A microservice architecture cannot succeed through decentralization alone; long-term ecosystem health requires organizational standardization across eight core principles of production readiness to ensure stability, reliability, and catastrophe preparedness.
* **Architectural Ideal**: Standardized, resilient microservices operating within an organized ecosystem where every service meets clear, quantifiable operational standards.
* **Primary Value**: High operational reliability and service predictability across decentralized engineering teams.
* **Key Trade-off**: Standardized governance vs. absolute team autonomy in tool selection and service architecture.
* **Primary Anti-Pattern**: Unregulated Microservice Sprawl (introducing dozens of microservices without operational standards or production-readiness criteria).
* **Guiding Constraint**: Services must be production-ready before handling live customer traffic.
* **Target Environment**: Distributed microservice ecosystems operating in cloud or containerized environments.
* **Decision Criterion**: Does introducing or altering this service maintain or improve the overall ecosystem's stability, observability, and disaster resilience?
* **Core Discipline**: Systematic, automated production-readiness reviews and standardized service tiering.
* **Success Metric**: Zero cascading outages, predictable MTTR (Mean Time to Resolution), and 100% production-readiness compliance across all active microservices.

---

## 4. Source-Backed Structure
The material is structured around key themes that define microservice standardization and production readiness:
1. **Microservice Ecosystems and Production Readiness**: Defining microservice maturity, service standardization, and establishing audit frameworks across engineering organizations.
2. **Stability and Reliability**: Standardizing fault-isolation mechanisms, connection timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, rate limiters, and graceful degradation strategies.
3. **Scalability and Performance**: Capacity planning, resource optimization, database scaling strategies, caching tiers, and auto-scaling policies.
4. **Fault Tolerance and Catastrophe Preparedness**: Disaster recovery planning, graceful fallback handling, game day/chaos testing, and post-mortem incident remediation.
5. **Monitoring, Observability, and Alerting**: Standardized metrics collection, distributed tracing, structured logging, centralized dashboards, and actionable alert rules.
6. **Documentation and Service Metadata**: Centralized service registries, architectural design docs, operational runbooks, and standardized service manifests.

---

## 5. Source-Backed Spirit
1. **What is the central problem this text solves?**
   * Preventing distributed chaos, cascading failures, and high operational overhead when transitioning from a monolithic application to a decentralized ecosystem of hundreds of microservices.
2. **What is the primary architectural goal?**
   * Creating a predictable, standardized microservice ecosystem where every service satisfies explicit, measurable production-readiness criteria across stability, scalability, and disaster recovery.
3. **How does the author evaluate trade-offs?**
   * Enforcing organization-wide operational standards and production-readiness gates takes precedence over unrestricted developer autonomy in selecting custom infrastructure or deployment mechanisms.
4. **What is the recommended approach to change management?**
   * Change should occur through automated deployment pipelines, standardized service templates, explicit production-readiness checklists, and continuous operational reviews.
5. **What is the relationship between policy and mechanism?**
   * Organization-wide policies define production-readiness standards, while platform engineering tools, standardized microservice chassis, and infrastructure frameworks enforce these mechanisms automatically.
6. **How should failure be handled?**
   * Failure must be treated as inevitable. Microservices must be engineered for fault isolation, employing circuit breakers, explicit network timeouts, graceful degradation, and post-mortem continuous improvement.

---

## 6. Key Concepts
1. **Production Readiness Checklist**: A structured, quantifiable set of operational requirements across stability, scalability, fault tolerance, monitoring, and documentation that every microservice must satisfy before receiving production traffic.
2. **Microservice Chassis**: A standardized shared framework or template that embeds common operational concerns—such as logging, metrics collection, tracing, circuit breaking, and health endpoints—into every service.
3. **Circuit Breaker Pattern**: A stability mechanism that wraps network calls to external dependencies, automatically tripping to open state when failure thresholds are exceeded to prevent resource exhaustion and cascading failures.
4. **Service Tiering**: Categorizing microservices by criticality (e.g., Tier 1 for core payment/auth path, Tier 3 for background analytics) to prioritize operational resources, SLOs, and incident responses.
5. **Graceful Degradation**: Designing a microservice to provide fallback functionality or partial responses when non-critical downstream dependencies fail or become unreachable.
6. **Operational Runbook**: Standardized documentation attached to every service detailing step-by-step procedures for diagnosing alerts, handling incidents, and performing routine maintenance.
7. **Distributed Tracing**: Injecting and propagating unique trace and span IDs across inter-service HTTP/gRPC headers to enable end-to-end request tracking across microservice call graphs.

---

## 7. Practical Patterns and Examples
1. **Standardized Health & Readiness Probes**:
   * *Description*: Every microservice implements `/healthz` (liveness probe) and `/readyz` (readiness probe) endpoints to report operational status to orchestrators and load balancers.
   * *Example*: Liveness checks verify process execution, while readiness checks verify connectivity to required backing datastores before accepting traffic.
2. **Resilience Middleware & Explicit Timeouts**:
   * *Description*: All inter-service communications wrap HTTP/gRPC clients with explicit connection timeouts, request timeouts, and exponential backoff retry policies.
   * *Example*: Configuring a maximum 500ms timeout for downstream user-profile API calls, preventing thread pool exhaustion when downstream services stall.
3. **Service Manifest and Registry Integration**:
   * *Description*: Inclusion of a standardized repository metadata file (`service.yaml` or `microservice.json`) defining owner, tier, repository link, runbook URL, and dependency graphs.
   * *Example*: CI/CD pipelines read `service.yaml` during build steps to validate service ownership, tier assignment, and central service catalog registration.

---

## 8. Common Failure Modes and Risks
1. **Unregulated Microservice Sprawl**: Launching microservices rapidly without standardized production-readiness checks, resulting in unmonitored, fragile, and unmaintained services.
2. **Cascading Failures via Missing Timeouts**: Failing to configure explicit network timeouts on inter-service HTTP or RPC clients, causing resource exhaustion across calling services when a downstream service experiences latency.
3. **Missing Runbooks and Incident Ambiguity**: Deploying services to production without actionable runbooks, leading to prolonged outage durations (MTTR) as engineers scramble to locate diagnostic steps during off-hours incidents.
4. **Silent Degradation from Unmonitored Fallbacks**: Implementing silent fallbacks without emitting error metrics or alerting logs, masking underlying infrastructure degradation until secondary systems fail.
5. **Inconsistent Logging and Uncorrelated Traces**: Allowing individual microservices to format logs arbitrarily without correlation IDs, rendering cross-service incident debugging nearly impossible.

---

## 9. Repository-Audit Translation

### Static Checks
* Verify presence of standardized service manifest (`service.yaml`, `microservice.json`, or `app.json`).
* Validate inclusion of containerization configurations (`Dockerfile`, `Containerfile`).
* Audit code for explicit HTTP/gRPC client network timeout configurations.
* Ensure health probe endpoints (`/healthz`, `/readyz`, `/health`, or `/status`) exist in route definitions.
* Check for operational documentation files (`RUNBOOK.md`, `README.md`, or external runbook URL in manifest).
* Verify absence of committed secrets, private keys, or plain-text credentials in repository files.

### Human Review
* Assess appropriateness of assigned service tiering (Tier 1 vs. Tier 2 vs. Tier 3) relative to user impact.
* Evaluate adequacy of disaster recovery plans, backup restoration procedures, and game-day chaos testing scenarios.
* Review quality and clarity of runbook instructions for step-by-step incident triage.
* Validate that fallback mechanisms deliver acceptable degraded user experiences without breaching domain business constraints.

---

## 10. Candidate Rules

| Rule ID | Title | Scope | Readiness |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `RS-PRM-001` | Service Manifest File Presence | Validate that a standardized service metadata file exists in repository root. | `ready` |
| `RS-PRM-002` | Operational Runbook Availability | Ensure a dedicated runbook file or explicit runbook URL is defined in repo. | `ready` |
| `RS-PRM-003` | Health Check Endpoint Route | Check that a health/status route is registered in server source code. | `ready` |
| `RS-PRM-004` | Explicit Client Timeout Configuration | Verify that outgoing HTTP/gRPC clients configure explicit timeout parameters. | `ready` |
| `RS-PRM-005` | Container Specification File Presence | Ensure container build spec (Dockerfile or Containerfile) exists in repository. | `ready` |
| `RS-PRM-006` | Prohibit Hardcoded Credentials | Ensure no private keys or hardcoded API tokens exist in source files. | `ready` |
| `RS-PRM-007` | Production Readiness Gate Approval | Verify manual audit approval from platform engineering before major release. | `needs-review` |
| `RS-PRM-008` | Disaster Recovery Drills | Verify cadence of game-day chaos tests and simulated failure exercises. | `blocked` |

---

## 11. Suggested YAML Fragment

```yaml
rules:
  - id: RS-PRM-001
    title: Service Manifest File Presence
    severity: error
    check:
      type: file_exists
      paths:
        - service.yaml
        - service.json
        - microservice.yaml
        - app.json

  - id: RS-PRM-002
    title: Operational Runbook Availability
    severity: warning
    check:
      type: file_exists
      paths:
        - RUNBOOK.md
        - docs/RUNBOOK.md
        - runbook.md

  - id: RS-PRM-003
    title: Health Check Endpoint Route
    severity: error
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      pattern: '(?i)(/healthz|/readyz|/health|/status|/ping)'
      files:
        - "**/routes.*"
        - "**/router.*"
        - "**/server.*"
        - "**/app.*"
        - "**/main.*"

  - id: RS-PRM-004
    title: Explicit Client Timeout Configuration
    severity: warning
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      pattern: '(?i)(timeout|connect_timeout|read_timeout)\s*[:=]'
      files:
        - "**/client.*"
        - "**/http.*"
        - "**/rpc.*"
        - "**/config.*"

  - id: RS-PRM-005
    title: Container Specification File Presence
    severity: error
    check:
      type: file_exists
      paths:
        - Dockerfile
        - Containerfile

  - id: RS-PRM-006
    title: Prohibit Hardcoded Private Keys
    severity: critical
    check:
      type: pattern_match
      pattern: '-----BEGIN (RSA|EC|OPENSSH|PRIVATE) KEY-----'
      files:
        - "**/*"
```

===== FILE: docs/notes/webapp/site-reliability-engineering.md =====
# RepoScope Knowledge Note: Site Reliability Engineering (Google SRE Team)

## 1. Source Identity
- **Title**: Site Reliability Engineering: How Google Runs Production Systems
- **Author(s)**: Betsy Beyer, Chris Jones, Jennifer Petoff, and Niall Richard Murphy
- **Primary Domain**: Systems Engineering, Site Reliability Engineering, Production Operations, Infrastructure Automation, Service Level Management
- **Target Audience**: Site Reliability Engineers, DevOps Engineers, Infrastructure Architects, Systems Administrators, Software Engineering Leaders

---

## 2. Scope
This Knowledge Note distills the operational, architectural, and organizational principles of Site Reliability Engineering (SRE) as practiced at Google. It translates SRE philosophy—treating operational engineering as a software problem—into actionable repository-level static checks and human review guidelines. The scope encompasses service level management (SLIs, SLOs, Error Budgets), toil reduction through automation, distributed systems monitoring and alerting, emergency response, release engineering, and failure mode mitigation (circuit breaking, load shedding, cascading failure prevention).

---

## 3. Source Spirit Card
| Field | Value / Principle |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Target Architecture** | Highly Distributed, Scalable Service Ecosystems & Infrastructure Platforms |
| **Core Architectural Thesis** | SRE is what happens when a software engineer is tasked with designing an operations function; operational workloads must be capped via automation and error budgets to maximize feature velocity safely. |
| **Chief Architectural Value** | Reliability via Software Automation and Rigorous Reliability Budgeting |
| **Chief Anti-Pattern / Failure Mode** | Operational Toil Overload & Alert Fatigue (manual, repetitive, unscalable operational overhead leading to burnout and system fragility) |
| **Primary Boundary Principle** | 50% Cap on Operational Workload (Toil/On-Call); remaining >=50% engineering time dedicated to permanent software fixes and automation. |
| **Static Audit Suitability** | High for SLO/SLI definition files, alert configurations, health endpoints, graceful shutdown hooks, andIaC manifests; Low for organizational culture and post-mortem review dynamics. |
| **Dominant Code-Level Smell** | Missing network timeouts, unhandled retries without exponential backoff/jitter, hardcoded infrastructure endpoints, lack of structured health metrics. |
| **Dominant Repo-Level Smell** | Missing alerting rules, absent SLO tracking configs, lack of runbooks/playbooks in codebase, missing automated deployment rollback hooks. |
| **Primary Human Review Focus** | Blameless post-mortem quality, SLO target selection sanity, on-call rotation fairness, operational risk acceptance, and architectural error budget alignment. |
| **Key Engineering Metric / Trade-off** | Error Budget (100% - SLO Target Reliability) vs. Feature Velocity; Operational Toil Percentage (<50%). |

---

## 4. Source-Backed Structure
The architectural and operational framework of Site Reliability Engineering is structured across four core themes:

1. **Service Level Management & Reliability Budgeting**: Establishing quantitative measurements (SLIs), setting user-centric reliability targets (SLOs), and utilizing Error Budgets as the neutral arbiter for balancing product feature velocity with operational stability.
2. **Automation & Toil Reduction**: Engineering software solutions to eliminate repetitive, manual, non-creative operational work ("toil"), enforcing a hard 50% limit on operational overhead.
3. **Distributed Systems Resilience & Overload Protection**: Designing systems to withstand infrastructure failures through graceful degradation, load shedding, traffic throttling, exponential backoff with jitter, and circuit breaking.
4. **Monitoring, Emergency Response & Incident Management**: Transitioning from reactive, symptomatic host-level checking to white-box monitoring, alert suppression/grouping, structured runbooks, blameless post-mortems, and standardized incident command structures.

---

## 5. Source-Backed Spirit
1. **What is the fundamental problem this architecture solves?**
   It solves the fundamental conflict between product developers (who want to push new features rapidly) and operations teams (who want to preserve stability by minimizing changes), while preventing operational labor costs from growing linearly with system scale.
2. **What trade-offs does this architecture explicitly accept?**
   It explicitly accepts that 100% uptime is almost always the wrong target (due to economic infeasibility and diminishing returns to end users), accepting planned unreliability within an Error Budget in exchange for rapid feature deployment and operational agility.
3. **Where does this architecture draw hard boundaries?**
   It draws a hard boundary at 50% operational workload (toil and ticket handling). If toil exceeds 50%, operational work is handed back to product engineering teams until software mitigations reduce the burden.
4. **What happens when teams violate these principles?**
   Violating SRE principles leads to operational burnout, high turnover, "toil sinks" where engineers spend all their time manually maintaining systems, alert fatigue, and catastrophic cascading outages due to missing circuit breakers and improper load management.
5. **What can be checked automatically versus manually?**
   *Automated*: Presence of SLO definitions, alerting threshold configs, health check handlers, runbook links in alert rules, structured logging formats, and deployment pipeline manifests.
   *Manual*: Quality of post-mortem root-cause analysis, realism of SLO targets relative to user expectations, organizational blamelessness culture, and architectural review of failure domains.
6. **How does this architecture evolve over time?**
   As services mature, manual operational tasks are iteratively encapsulated into automated self-healing software platforms (e.g., automated cluster management, automated canary analysis, self-remediating infrastructure).

---

## 6. Key Concepts

### Concept 1: Service Level Indicator (SLI)
- **Definition**: A carefully defined quantitative measure of a service's performance or availability at a point in time (e.g., request latency, error rate, throughput).
- **Repo-Level Manifestation**: Defined in monitoring configuration files (e.g., Prometheus rules, Datadog metric manifests) as structured queries computing good events divided by total events.

### Concept 2: Service Level Objective (SLO)
- **Definition**: A target percentage or range for a Service Level Indicator specified over a set time window (e.g., 99.9% request latency < 200ms over 30 days).
- **Repo-Level Manifestation**: Configured in service metadata manifests, YAML monitoring specs, or deployment policy definitions.

### Concept 3: Error Budget
- **Definition**: The mathematical room for failure calculated as `100% - SLO`. It represents the allowable unreliability that product teams can spend on risky deployments, experiments, and feature launches.
- **Repo-Level Manifestation**: Integrated into release pipeline gating files, CI/CD scripts, or deployment policy manifests that freeze deployments when the error budget is exhausted.

### Concept 4: Toil
- **Definition**: The kind of operational work that tends to be manual, repetitive, automatable, tactical, devoid of enduring value, and scales linearly as a service grows.
- **Repo-Level Manifestation**: Absence of manual shell scripts or ticket-driven tasks; presence of infrastructure-as-code and self-healing automation scripts.

### Concept 5: White-Box Monitoring
- **Definition**: Monitoring based on internal telemetry exposed by the application (e.g., request counters, queue depths, internal latencies) rather than external ping tests.
- **Repo-Level Manifestation**: Instrumentation libraries imported in code exposing `/metrics` or `/health` endpoints with four golden signals (Latency, Traffic, Errors, Saturation).

### Concept 6: Blameless Post-Mortem
- **Definition**: A structured post-incident investigation document that focuses on systemic failures, process gaps, and missing safeguards rather than individual human error.
- **Repo-Level Manifestation**: A mandatory `postmortems/` directory or template markdown file in repository root, linked in incident documentation.

---

## 7. Practical Patterns and Examples

### Pattern 1: The Four Golden Signals Monitoring Pattern
Every production service must expose and monitor the four golden signals:
- **Latency**: Time taken to serve a request (differentiating successful requests from errors).
- **Traffic**: A measure of demand on the system (e.g., HTTP requests/sec).
- **Errors**: Rate of requests that fail (explicit 5xx status codes, implicit failures, or policy timeouts).
- **Saturation**: How "full" the service is (CPU usage, memory footrpint, thread pool exhaustion).

*Implementation Example*: Exposing a standard `/metrics` endpoint using Prometheus instrumentation libraries in code, configured with alert manifests alerting on high error rates or saturation limits.

### Pattern 2: Graceful Degradation & Load Shedding Pattern
When incoming traffic exceeds capacity or downstream dependencies fail, services must drop low-priority requests or disable non-essential features rather than crashing entirely.

*Implementation Example*: Middleware checking saturation metrics or thread pool capacity and returning HTTP 503 (or HTTP 429) immediately for non-critical endpoints, while shielding critical paths.

### Pattern 3: Exponential Backoff with Jitter Pattern
Client calls to downstream services must avoid synchronized retry storms ("thundering herd") during recovery by introducing randomized exponential delays between retry attempts.

*Implementation Example*: Network client library configurations enforcing `delay = min(cap, base * 2 ^ attempt) + random_jitter`.

---

## 8. Common Failure Modes and Risks

### Failure Mode 1: Cascading Failure via Unbounded Retries
- **Risk**: A minor transient slowdown in a downstream database causes upstream callers to retry aggressively without backoff or limit, creating a feedback loop that completely overwhelms and crashes the database.
- **Mitigation**: Enforce client-side retry budgets (e.g., maximum 10% retry traffic allowance), exponential backoff with random jitter, and deadline/timeout propagation across service boundaries.

### Failure Mode 2: Toil Sink & Operational Burnout
- **Risk**: Operational tasks are performed manually via SSH/CLI commands without automation, causing operational overhead to grow linearly with user scale until engineering capacity is consumed entirely by operational firefighting.
- **Mitigation**: Enforce the 50% SRE toil cap; mandate that every manual operational task performed twice must yield an automated script or self-healing platform feature.

### Failure Mode 3: Alert Fatigue & Symptom-Based Paging
- **Risk**: On-call engineers receive dozens of non-actionable or transient alerts per shift (e.g., high CPU on a single host), causing desensitization and missed critical outage alerts.
- **Mitigation**: Configure alerts exclusively on SLO-impacting symptoms (e.g., high user-visible error rates) rather than causes; ensure every page links directly to an actionable runbook.

### Failure Mode 4: Deploying without Error Budget Tracking
- **Risk**: Teams deploy risky features continuously without measuring reliability impact, leading to extended service degradation with no objective mechanism to pause deployments.
- **Mitigation**: Implement automated CI/CD release gating that checks error budget depletion and halts non-emergency deployments when budgets are exhausted.

---

## 9. Repository-Audit Translation

### Static Checks (Automated in Repository)
- **SLO/SLI Spec Files**: Verify presence of declarative monitoring and SLO definition files (e.g., `slo.yaml`, `alerts.yaml`).
- **Health Check Handlers**: Verify HTTP/gRPC services implement standard `/healthz` or `/livez`/`/readyz` endpoints.
- **Runbook Anchors**: Verify alert rule files contain valid `runbook_url` or `summary` annotations.
- **Timeout & Retry Limits**: Verify network clients configure explicit timeouts and retry backoff settings.
- **Deployment & Rollback Manifests**: Verify presence of automated canary deployment or health-gated pipeline configurations (`Dockerfile`, Kubernetes manifests, Terraform configs).

### Human Review (Contextual / Architecture)
- **SLO Target Realism**: Review whether the configured SLO targets align with true user perception and business impact.
- **Blameless Culture & Post-Mortem Quality**: Evaluate post-mortem records for actionable systemic remedies rather than human blame.
- **Capacity Planning & Saturation Models**: Review load test results and capacity forecasts against growth projections.
- **Toil Audit**: Periodically review on-call ticket logs to identify repetitive tasks requiring software automation.

---

```yaml
    check_type: pattern_match

  - id: SRE-004
    name: SLO Definition File Presence
    severity: WARNING

    check_type: file_exists

    name: Post-Mortem Template File Presence
    severity: INFO
    description: "Repository should contain a post-mortem template to encourage structured blameless incident reviews."
    check_type: file_exists
    files:
      - "PULL_REQUEST_TEMPLATE.md"
      - "docs/postmortem_template.md"
      - ".github/postmortem_template.md"
```
